MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION
FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
JEFFREY BERGFALK AND IIAN B. SMYTHE
Abstract. We consider classification problems for manifolds and discrete sub-
groups of Lie groups from a descriptive set-theoretic point of view. This work is
largely foundational in conception and character, recording both a framework
for general study and Borel complexity computations for some of the most fun-
damental classes of manifolds. We show, for example, that for all n ≥0, the
homeomorphism problem for compact topological n-manifolds is Borel equiv-
alent to the relation =N of equality on the natural numbers, while the homeo-
morphism problem for noncompact topological 2-manifolds is of maximal com-
plexity among equivalence relations classifiable by countable structures.
A
nontrivial step in the latter consists of proving Borel measurable formulations
of the Jordan–Schoenflies and surface triangulation theorems.
Turning our
attention to groups and geometric structures, we show, strengthening results
of Stuck–Zimmer and Andretta–Camerlo–Hjorth, that the conjugacy relation
on discrete subgroups of any noncompact semisimple Lie group is essentially
countable universal. So too, as a corollary, is the isometry relation for complete
hyperbolic n-manifolds for any n ≥2, generalizing a result of Hjorth–Kechris.
We then show that the isometry relation for complete hyperbolic n-manifolds
with finitely generated fundamental group is, in contrast, Borel equivalent
to the equality relation =R on the real numbers when n = 2, but that it is
not concretely classifiable when n = 3; thus there exists no Borel assignment
of numerical complete invariants to finitely generated Kleinian groups up to
conjugacy. We close with a survey of the most immediate open questions.

1. Introduction
A curiosity of the enormous advances in the fields of manifold classification and
invariant descriptive set theory over the past forty years has been their compar-
ative lack of interaction. We can hardly attribute this, for example, to any in-
herent antipathy between manifold theory and logic; against the background of
the extensive literature interrelating manifolds and computability theory [BHP68,
Mat03, CS06, HTM24], in fact, this curiosity grows all the more striking. Add to
this not only invariant descriptive set theory’s programmatic interest in classifi-
cation problems throughout mathematics (particularly in manifold-adjacent fields
like dynamics [FRW11, FW22] and geometric group theory [Tho08, PS21, CC24]),
but Hjorth’s framing, in one of the field’s foundational texts, of the classification
of compact surfaces as among the most exemplary of solutions to such problems
[Hjo00, Exam. 0.1], and we have the makings of a positive mystery.
The purpose of this paper is to begin to remedy this lack (this involves, naturally,
identifying one of the mystery’s likely culprits; see below). A few sample theorems
will help to suggest the benefits of doing so. First, note the empirical fact that
classes of mathematical objects, and their classification up to standard notions of
equivalence, routinely admit natural parametrizations as, respectively, Polish (or at
least standard Borel) spaces and analytic equivalence relations thereon (see Section
2.2 below for fuller discussion of this paragraph’s terms and claims). The existence
of Borel measurable maps between two such spaces respecting these relations de-
termines the partial order ≤B of Borel reducibility; modulo bireducibility, or Borel
equivalence, we term the elements of this partial order Borel complexity degrees,
and it is the study both of the rich structure of these degrees and of the locations of
particular classification problems throughout mathematics among them by which
we mean the phrase invariant descriptive set theory. Within this framework, the
relation E ≤B F carries the heuristic that the classification problem represented by
F is at least as hard as that represented by E, since a map witnessing E ≤B F will
convert any solution for F into a solution for E. The strict relation E <B F then
carries the heuristic that the classification problem represented by F is significantly
harder than that represented by E, in the sense that merely countable resources
(instantiated by a Borel map) will never suffice to convert a solution for E into one
for F. See [For18] for a non-technical overview of this area.
Against this background, consider the following theorem of Markov [Mar58].
Theorem (Markov, 1958). There exists an effective map M from the class of finite
presentations P of groups G(P) to that of compact 4-manifolds M(P) such that:
i. π1(M(P)) ∼= G(P) for all P, and
ii. M(P) ∼= M(Q) if and only if G(P) ∼= G(Q).
This M reduces the recursively unsolvable group isomorphism problem to the clas-
sification of compact 4-manifolds up to homeomorphism and carries the folklore
corollary that the latter is “impossible”, but this inference should be handled with
care. For we have, in contrast, the following (our Corollaries 3.24 and 3.26 below):
Theorem A. For all n ≥0, both the classification
• of compact topological n-manifolds up to homeomorphism, and
• of compact smooth n-manifolds up to diffeomorphism
are Borel equivalent to the relation =N of equality on the natural numbers.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
3
In particular, from an invariant descriptive set-theoretic perspective, the classifi-
cation of compact 4-manifolds up to homeomorphism amounts to the simplest of
all infinite classification problems (that is, of equivalence relations possessing in-
finitely many distinct equivalence classes). This is, of course, compatible with a
view, deriving from Markov’s theorem, of the classification of compact 4-manifolds
as hard, but only alongside a view of classification problems generally accredited
as solved as being even harder. The degrees appearing in the following theorems
(corresponding to our Corollary 7.7, Theorem 8.5, Corollary 6.7, and Theorem 4.1
and Corollary 4.30, respectively), for example, form a strictly increasing <B-chain
above =N.
Theorem B. The classification of complete hyperbolic 2-manifolds with finitely
generated fundamental group up to isometry is Borel equivalent to the relation =R
of equality on the real numbers.
Theorem C. The classification of complete hyperbolic 3-manifolds with finitely
generated fundamental group up to isometry is of Borel complexity at least that of
the relation E0 of eventual equality of infinite binary sequences.
Theorem D. For any n ≥2, the classification of all complete hyperbolic n-
manifolds up to isometry is essentially countable universal, or, in other words,
Borel equivalent to the degree E∞.
Theorem E. The classification of connected topological 2-manifolds (i.e., surfaces)
up to homeomorphism is complete for countable structures, as is the classification
of connected smooth 2-manifolds up to diffeomorphism.
Put differently, each is
complete for orbit equivalence relations induced by Borel actions of the infinite
permutation group S∞.
Especially alert readers will have observed that the classification problems as-
sociated to Theorems B and E each have well-known classical solutions; our proof
of the latter, for example, amounts to an analysis of the Borel content of the
Kerékjártó–Richards [vK23, Ric63] classification of noncompact surfaces. A pre-
liminary component of this analysis consists in establishing Borel measurable (with
respect to the appropriate spaces) forms of the classical Jordan–Schoenflies and
triangulation theorems for surfaces (our Theorems 4.13 and 4.16, respectively):
Theorem F. There is a Borel measurable function which takes any embedding γ
of S1 into R2 to a self-homeomorphism of R2 which extends γ.
Theorem G. There is a Borel measurable function which takes any surface S to
a simplicial complex whose geometric realization is homeomorphic to S.
Theorem C, like Theorem E, is largely a story of manifolds’ ends and draws heav-
ily on a celebrated classification result, namely the Ending Lamination Theorem
[Min10, BCM12] and its aftermath. Theorem D is somewhat different in nature,
as we will see. In any case, the apparent tension of Theorems A through E with
the implications of Markov’s is, of course, ultimately illusory, for they involve dif-
ferent registers: while the latter derives its force from the question of whether the
word problem admits resolution with finite resources, the underlying concern in
the former is, again, for what is or is not achievable with countable resources.1 To
1Note that if it were merely a matter of assigning arbitrary complete invariants to objects,
then any classification problem would be trivially solvable by the Axiom of Choice. What finitary

J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
summarize our discussion so far: One value of the Borel complexity framework is
its description of a finer and more definite structure of “degrees of difficulty” within
the study of manifolds than has tended to be perceived.
Extending this point is one outstanding exception to the comparative lack of
interactions between manifold theory and Borel complexity theory cited above,
namely Hjorth and Kechris’s The complexity of the classification of Riemann sur-
faces and complex manifolds [HK00]. Therein, motivated by questions regarding the
complexity of conformal invariants from [BHR80], the authors show the following:
Theorem (Hjorth–Kechris, 2000).
1. The classification of Riemann surfaces up to biholomorphism is essentially
countable universal.
2. For any n ≥2, the biholomorphism relation on complex n-manifolds does
not admit classification by countable structures.
Since by the uniformization theorem all but an exceptional few Riemann surfaces
are hyperbolic, our Theorem D may be viewed as a generalization of (1). Clause (2),
on the other hand, is an application of Hjorth’s theory of turbulence [Hjo00]; more
colloquially, it says that there exists no reasonable assignment of countable groups
or algebras, let alone numerical invariants, to distinguish between, for example,
complex 2-manifolds up to biholomorphism.
We highly recommend this paper
to the reader; it forms the present work’s principal inspiration.
But we should
also note that roughly 9 of its 34 pages are taken up with what its authors term
the “technically cumbersome, although mathematically rather shallow” business of
parametrizing its objects of study, and we do so for what it suggests of at least
one of the culprits behind the “mystery” framing our work: it seems in large part
to have been a deficiency of compelling parametrizations of manifolds that has
hindered their descriptive set-theoretic study thus far.
For what is ultimately wanted is an approach to parametrization which is suffi-
ciently versatile to accommodate the multiplicity of manifold structures arising in
practice, but sufficiently precise not to confuse them; a transparency facilitating
such analyses as underlie the theorems above is, of course, a value as well. To
underscore the challenge, note that these are not criteria that Gromov–Hausdorff
parametrizations, for example, ultimately satisfy (invoking, as they do, metrics
which topological manifolds don’t intrinsically possess; see Remark 3.19 for further
discussion). Perhaps this paper’s most fundamental contribution is a parametriza-
tion framework which does meet these criteria. In Section 3 below, after briefly
reviewing the pseudogroup approach to manifold theory, we introduce the notion
of a Borel pseudogroup and prove the following result (as Theorems 3.9 and 3.22).
Theorem H. For any Borel pseudogroup G on a locally compact Polish space X,
there is a standard Borel parameter space M(G, X) of all (G, X)-manifolds on which
the relation of (G, X)-equivalence is an analytic equivalence relation.
Prominent examples of Borel pseudogroups include Top and C∞on Rn, and Isom on
Hn, determining standard Borel parameter spaces of all topological, smooth, and
or countable constraints as above help to formalize is the notion of a reasonable solution to a
classification problem: only solutions making good, economical use of properties of the objects
in question will tend to satisfy them. See Section 2.2 for further discussion, one emphasizing the
complementary heuristic of definable solutions to classification problems.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
5
hyperbolic n-manifolds, respectively (see Theorem 3.14 and Examples 3.15 and 3.17
below).
In particular cases, of course, manifold theory boasts some of the most celebrated
parameter spaces in all of mathematics, namely moduli spaces whose geometric
structures encode deep properties of the families of objects under study; examples
will figure in our Sections 7 and 8 below.
Theorem H may be read as a more
uniform provision of moduli spaces of manifolds, but at the cost of coarsening their
structure to that of standard Borel spaces. Borel complexity theory can then help
clarify which of these parameter spaces meaningfully support further topological or
geometric structures (see, for example, Corollary 8.24).
In contrast to the situation just described for manifolds, the discrete subgroups
of a locally compact group form a well-known parameter space, via the Chabauty
(also known as the Fell or geometric) topology, on which the ambient group acts
by conjugation. The resulting conjugacy relations have been extensively studied
in both descriptive set theory and ergodic theory. To briefly summarize the most
relevant results for our present work: Stuck and Zimmer showed in [SZ94] that
the conjugacy relation on subgroups of the free group on two generators F2 is not
concretely classifiable, meaning it does not Borel reduce to =R, from which they
derived the same conclusion for the conjugacy relation on the discrete subgroups of
any noncompact semisimple Lie group. Thomas and Velickovic [TV99] showed that
the conjugacy relation on subgroups of F2 is, in fact, countable universal, which
was generalized by Andretta, Camerlo, and Hjorth [ACH01] to all countable groups
which contain F2. We extend these results to a large class of Lie groups and prove
a best-possible sharpening of the Stuck and Zimmer result (our Theorem 6.5):
Theorem I. The conjugacy relation on discrete subgroups of any noncompact
semisimple Lie group is essentially countable universal.
This detour through groups and conjugacy relations connects back to our study of
manifolds via the classical correspondence between complete hyperbolic n-manifolds
up to isometry and discrete subgroups of the isometry group Isom(Hn) up to con-
jugacy. We show in Section 5 that this correspondence is witnessed by Borel re-
ductions in both directions, by way of which we deduce Theorem D as a corollary
of Theorem I.
We then turn our attention to the groups of orientation-preserving isometries
Isom+(H2) and Isom+(H3); these naturally identify with the Lie groups PSL(2, R)
and PSL(2, C), and their discrete subgroups are exactly what Poincaré dubbed the
Fuchsian and Kleinian groups, respectively. Thus, by way of the correspondence
cited just above, the families of finitely generated torsion-free Fuchsian and Kleinian
groups, respectively, parametrize the classes of complete orientable hyperbolic 2-
and 3-manifolds possessing a finitely generated fundamental group. For brevity, as
well as for contrast with other related manifold finiteness conditions, we frequently
term such manifolds algebraically finite below.
Observe that within a countable group, there are only countably many finitely
generated subgroups, hence their conjugacy relation trivially reduces to =N. Less
trivially, Stuck and Zimmer proved in [SZ94] that, in any Lie group, the conjugacy
problem for lattices, i.e., for discrete subgroups with finite co-volume (which are
necessarily finitely generated), is always concretely classifiable. In the special case
of PSL(2, R), our Theorem B translates via the aforementioned correspondence to
the following extension of these results.

6
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Theorem J. The conjugacy relation on finitely generated torsion-free Fuchsian
groups is Borel equivalent to =R.
The algebraically finite hyperbolic 2-manifolds are exactly the geometrically fi-
nite ones, meaning that their convex core is always of finite volume, but in higher
dimensions, these conditions no longer coincide.
In three dimensions, the alge-
braically finite class is, in senses both of inclusion and of complexity, the strictly
bigger one, by the following reformulation of our Theorem 8.5.
Theorem K. The Borel complexity degrees of the conjugacy relation on the major
finiteness classes of torsion-free Kleinian groups are as follows:
• for lattices, it is =N;
• for geometrically finite groups, it is =R;
• for finitely generated groups, it is at least E0.
Corresponding results hold for the isometry relation on the classes of finite volume,
geometrically finite, and algebraically finite hyperbolic 3-manifolds, respectively.
Much as for Theorem D and Theorem I, one might regard Theorems B and
C as corollaries of Theorems J and K, respectively.
In actuality, however, our
arguments of the latter run in the opposite direction: both essentially proceed from
the manifold side to the group side, to the degree that these two sides can even
be meaningfully disentangled. The third item in Theorem K, for example, largely
derives from the following (our Theorem 8.4); here, by D[Γ] we mean the set of
discrete subgroups of PSL(2, C) which are group-isomorphic to Γ, endowed with
the Chabauty topology.
Theorem L. Let S be a closed orientable hyperbolizable surface. The classification
up to isometry of doubly degenerate hyperbolic manifolds homeomorphic to S ×R is
Borel equivalent to E0. In particular, there exists no Borel assignment of complete
numerical invariants, up to PSL(2, C)-conjugacy, to the elements of D[π1(S)].
The theorem allows us to strengthen a result of [CS13], with the aid of which we
show the isometry relation on a much wider range of algebraically finite hyperbolic
3-manifolds to be essentially hyperfinite as well; see Section 8.4 for discussion. This
brings us to a sequence of concluding points.
First, our proof of Theorem J interacts closely with the recent work [BLL], while
those of Theorems K and L draw heavily on works [LS09, CS12, CS13, ABB+20,
PS21] falling broadly, as noted, in the orbit of the Ending Lamination Theorem.
They are, in other words, applications of deep results in manifold theory to a
problem originating in descriptive set theory, underscoring that the gain from closer
dialogue between these fields is bilateral.
Second, the scarcity, underscored above, of interactions between the fields of
manifold classification and invariant descriptive set theory has very recently be-
gun to abate; the present work figures even as part of an emergent countertrend.
In addition to [HK00], cited above, let us briefly survey the main exceptions to
that scarcity which we’re aware of. In the (withdrawn) preprint [Kul15], Weinstein
considered the complexity of the homeomorphism relation on different classes of
manifolds and conjectured the complexity result for surfaces that we verify in our
Theorem E. More recently, and independently from our own work, Hoganson and

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
7
Zomback showed in [HZ24] that the homeomorphism relation for orientable sur-
faces endowed with a pants decomposition is complete for countable structures.2
In their forthcoming [IW], Iannella and Weinstein show that the piecewise-linear
homeomorphism relation on simplicial complexes, in any dimension, is classifiable
by countable structures, and prove an analogue of our Theorem G for 3-manifolds
which shows that the corresponding homeomorphism relation is also complete for
countable structures. And in work currently in progress, Gompf and Panagiotopou-
los have shown that neither contractible 3-manifolds up to homeomorphism, nor
smooth structures on R4 up to diffeomorphism, are concretely classifiable [GP25];
see our conclusion for a bit more on these matters.
Third, it is largely for their status as the “generic” geometric manifolds (partic-
ularly in dimensions 2 and 3; see [Thu82]) that we’ve taken hyperbolic manifolds
as a focus, and if in the present context Theorem D is the first thing one might
wish to know about them, Theorems B and C are main steps towards the next.
Note, though, that our analyses of algebraically finite manifolds reach no higher
than 3 hyperbolic dimensions, for the reason that their mainspring, a deep and
cohesive theory of finitely generated discrete subgroups of Isom+(Hn), at present
doesn’t seem to either. As Kapovich, in the major survey of dimensions above 3,
writes: “There is a vast variety of Kleinian groups in higher dimensions: It appears
that there is no hope for a comprehensive structure theory similar to the theory of
discrete groups of isometries of H3” [Kap08, p. 485]. This is the sort of perception
which invariant descriptive set theory excels at making precise; proofs, for exam-
ple, for some n > 3, that the conjugacy relation on the class of geometrically finite
discrete subgroups of Isom+(Hn) is not concretely classifiable — or that it is not
hyperfinite on the class of all finitely generated discrete subgroups of Isom+(Hn)
— would be significant steps in this direction (see our conclusion’s Question 16),
and this prospect typifies the kinds of applications of Borel complexity theory to
manifold theory which we have ultimately in mind.
Lastly, considerations of readability have tempered a striving for results of max-
imum generality; the qualifier torsion-free in Theorems J and K, for example, is
likely unnecessary, but removing it would, in translation, entail a study of orbifolds,
taking us too far afield (of course, there may be cleverer ways of removing it). Sim-
ilarly for our restrictions to finite-dimensional manifolds and, in another direction,
for our negligence of the neighboring field of the classification of algebraic varieties
(see [Har77, Ch. I.8]). It is, after all, our most overarching point that contact be-
tween the fields of invariant descriptive set theory and manifold theory gives rise
to many more excellent questions than any one of us alone might answer. We show
that these admit satisfying answers by supplying some of them; a sample of the
many that remain is collected in our conclusion. Let us turn now to a more orderly
survey of this paper’s contents.
1.1. Organization of the paper. We hope readers will feel free, at least at a first
pass, to periodically skip ahead in their reading. This is for several reasons. One is
the text’s sheer length, another is its diversity of intended audience, and another is
the unfortunate reality that verifications that a given map or construction is Borel,
though necessary, are not as uniformly edifying as we might wish them to be.
2In [HZ24], the pants decomposition of a surface is used to identify it with a graph encoding
the relevant combinatorial information. Contrast this with our Theorem G, which extracts that
information from a purely topological presentation of the surface.

8
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Our paper’s overall structure is as follows: Section 2 organizes into the subsec-
tions Classical descriptive set theory, Invariant descriptive set theory, and Spaces
of subsets, and records the core of what we’ll need under these headings for the re-
mainder of the paper. This material is all standard, but that of the third subsection
plays a sufficiently critical role in what follows to merit special emphasis.
In Section 3, we record our overarching framework as well as the basic tools
for its manipulation and analysis. Section 3.1 recalls the definition of a (G, X)-
manifold for any pseudogroup G on a model space X; a parameter space M(G, X)
for such manifolds is defined and shown to be standard Borel whenever G is a Borel
pseudogroup on a locally compact Polish space X. The representative examples
of topological, smooth, and hyperbolic n-manifolds are each shown to fit into this
framework.
In Section 3.2, we show that the natural notion of equivalence for
(G, X)-manifolds manifests as an analytic equivalence relation on M(G, X), com-
pleting the proof of Theorem H, and derive Theorem A as a corollary. In Section 3.3,
we collect a number of lemmas for later application; we show, for example, that
exhaustions of manifolds by compact sets can be computed, and that manifolds
can be reparametrized as equivalent ones possessing charts with preferred proper-
ties (e.g., local finiteness or geodesic convexity), in Borel ways. We also show that
the subspaces of M(G, X) corresponding to complete or connected (G, X)-manifolds
are Borel, and isolate in metric contexts a Borel subspace M∗(G, X) of M(G, X) in
which the rapport between manifolds’ induced metrics and chart-metrics is optimal.
As their descriptions should suggest, Sections 3.1 and 3.2 are fundamental to all
that follows, while the more technical 3.3 admits more superficial initial readings,
to be augmented as the need arises.
Section 4 concerns the classification of surfaces. We first formulate and prove
our Borel forms of the Jordan–Schoenflies and triangulation theorems, Theorems F
and G above, by adapting graph-theoretic proofs of these results, due to Thomassen
[Tho92], to our framework. Then we show that the space of ends of a surface, as
well as the relevant topological invariants (genus, orientability, planarity), can all
be computed in a Borel way. The Kerékjártó–Richards result reduces the classi-
fication of surfaces to that of their spaces of ends; as the latter are nested triples
of compact totally disconnected Polish spaces, this is then reduced to the classifi-
cation of countable Boolean algebras equipped with a pair of nested filters, using
the techniques of [CG01], proving Theorem E. This section ends with a discussion
of disconnected 2-manifolds and, relatedly, records descriptive set-theoretic reasons
to prioritize, within various manifold classification programs, the connected ones.
The organizing concern of Section 5 is translation mechanisms between spaces
of manifolds of a given type and spaces Dtf(G) of torsion-free subgroups of an as-
sociated group G. As noted, the latter are equipped with the Chabauty–Fell or
geometric topology, and we begin by recalling that if G is locally compact and
Polish, then Dtf(G) is Polish as well. We then focus, with an eye to subsequent
sections, on the cases of G = Isom(Hn) for n ≥2, describing Borel maps in both
directions between Dtf(G) and the parameter space C∗
c(Isom, Hn) of complete con-
nected n-hyperbolic manifolds. These maps are, in essence, the quotient map in one
direction and the holonomy representation in the other, and it is this section’s main
result that they implement a classwise Borel equivalence between the isometry and
conjugacy relations, respectively, on C∗
c(Isom, Hn) and Dtf(Isom(Hn)).

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
9
In Section 6, we consider the conjugacy problem for discrete subgroups of Lie
groups. We show that whenever G is a matrix group which contains a discrete
nonabelian free subgroup, the associated conjugacy problem E(G, D(G)) is essen-
tially countable universal, extending the cited result of Andretta–Camerlo–Hjorth.
We then apply this result to prove our Theorem I, and, via the results of Sec-
tion 5, derive Theorem D as a corollary in the manner described above. As part
of this analysis, and for use in subsequent sections, we also prove several lemmas
showing how the complexity of the conjugacy problem passes between a group, its
subgroups, and its quotients. In particular, these lemmas free us to study those
relations on whichever of Isom+(Hn) and Isom(Hn) is more convenient thereafter.
After Section 6, we turn to a study of conjugacy relations on finitely gener-
ated discrete subgroups of Lie groups G; when G = Isom(Hn), the torsion-free
such subgroups correspond (via the translations of Section 5) to those hyperbolic
n-manifolds satisfying the most general of the finiteness conditions typically con-
sidered (e.g., closed, or finite volume, or geometrically finite, etc.). We term such
manifolds algebraically finite, as noted above, and they are the setting, both when
n = 2 and n = 3 for some of the subject’s most fundamental classification results.
We focus on the n = 2 case in Section 7, wherein we record one proof via moduli
space theory, along with a sketch of a second proof due to Ian Biringer, of Theorems
B and J listed above.
We then turn to the n = 3 case in Section 8. This material draws heavily, both
technically and conceptually, on the Teichmüller and moduli spaces machinery of
Section 7; here, though, the distinction between marked and unmarked manifolds
is, from a complexity theoretic perspective, a more consequential one. After noting
that the isometry classes of the former admit natural Polish parametrizations, we
work through (the manifold renderings of) the cases of Theorem K to show that the
isometry classes of unmarked algebraically finite hyperbolic 3-manifolds in general
do not. Along the way, we show that obstructions to the latter concentrate among
trivial line bundles like those considered in Theorem L. We draw on [ABB+20] and
[PS21] to prove Theorem L and apply the second work to bound the complexity
of the isometry relation for a broader class of algebraically finite manifolds; in the
process, again via the translations of Section 5, we complete the proof of Theorem
C.
In Section 9, we conclude with a brief survey of open questions. Since no survey
of any modest length could be exhaustive, we content ourselves with a discussion
of what seem to us the most conspicuous open questions, as well as of their inter-
relations. As we underscore therein, what we have aimed for is a conclusion which
opens onto further work and dialogue more than it closes anything at all.

J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
2. Preliminaries
In this section, we review for a general audience the necessary background ma-
terial in descriptive set theory, or the study of definable subsets of Polish spaces.
Experts are welcome to skip over this material, although we recommend at least
perusing Section 2.3, concerning topologies and Borel structures on spaces of sub-
sets of a Polish space, as it will be crucial for our parametrizations of spaces of
manifolds in the sequel and may be less familiar. Among our conventions, we note
without delay that we regard 0 as a natural number.
2.1. Classical descriptive set theory. Recall that a topological space X is Pol-
ish if it is separable and completely metrizable. Examples include the Euclidean
spaces Rn, Cantor space {0, 1}N, Baire space NN, any second countable Hausdorff
manifold M, and its group of self-homeomorphisms Homeo(M) when endowed with
the compact-open topology. We highlight in this section, as bullet points, basic facts
which will be used frequently throughout the rest of the paper. All of their proofs
may be found in the standard reference [Kec95].
• The class of Polish spaces is closed under the taking of countable products,
countable disjoint unions, and countable intersections of open subsets.
A subset B of a Polish space X is Borel if it is contained in the σ-algebra
generated by the open subsets of X. That is, B can be obtained from basic open
sets by taking countable unions, intersections, and complements, countably many
times. A set X together with a σ-algebra B on X is a standard Borel space if B is
the collection of Borel sets corresponding to some Polish topology on X. A map
f : X →Y between Polish or standard Borel spaces X and Y is Borel measurable
if inverse images of Borel sets are Borel; we omit the word “measurable” where it
is understood. Notice that we may view the closure of the class of Borel sets under
countable unions and intersections as saying that any set defined by a formula
consisting of finitely many quantifiers over fixed countable sets in front of a “Borel
predicate” is also Borel.
• The class of standard Borel spaces is closed under the taking of count-
able products (with Borel structure generated by products of Borel sets),
countable disjoint unions, countable increasing unions, and Borel subsets.
• All uncountable standard Borel spaces are isomorphic (via an invertible
Borel map) to R with its usual Borel structure.
• A map f : X →Y between standard Borel spaces is Borel if and only if its
graph is Borel when viewed as a subset of the product X × Y .
• (Luzin–Suslin Theorem) An injective image of a Borel set under a Borel
function is Borel.
In contrast to the previous bullet, the image of a Borel set under an arbitrary
Borel (or even continuous) function may fail to be Borel.
Such sets are called
analytic, and their complements coanalytic.
• A subset A of a standard Borel space X is analytic if and only if there is
a standard Borel space Y and a Borel set B ⊆X × Y such that A is the
projection projX[B] of B onto X.
• A useful way of rephrasing the previous point is that analytic sets can
be viewed as those defined by a formula with finitely many consecutive
existential quantifiers over objects from standard Borel spaces, in front of

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
11
a Borel predicate. E.g., if B ⊆X × Y is Borel, then the set
{x ∈X : ∃y ∈Y ((x, y) ∈B)}
is analytic. Formulas of this type, and the sets they define, are called Σ1
1.
• Coanalytic sets, then, correspond to finitely many consecutive universal
quantifiers in front of a Borel predicate. These formulas, and the resulting
sets, are called Π1
1.
• The collection of all analytic subsets of a standard Borel space is closed
under both countable unions and countable intersections. The same holds
for the collection of coanalytic subsets.
• (Suslin’s Theorem) A subset B of a standard Borel space is Borel if and
only if it is both analytic and coanalytic. In particular, if a set has both a
Σ1
1 and a Π1
1 description, then it is Borel.
Lastly, we will need the following “uniformization theorem”:
• (Luzin–Novikov Uniformization) If X and Y are standard Borel spaces
and B ⊆X × Y is a Borel set such that for every x ∈X, the section Bx =
{y ∈Y : (x, y) ∈B} is countable, then the projection projX[B] is Borel and
B has a Borel uniformization, i.e., a Borel function f : projX[B] →Y such
that for all x ∈projX[B], (x, f(x)) ∈B. In particular, countable-to-one
images of Borel sets under Borel functions are also Borel.
2.2. Invariant descriptive set theory. An equivalence relation E on a standard
Borel space is Borel (or analytic, respectively) if it is Borel (analytic) when viewed
as the set of pairs {(x, y) : xEy} in X2. Given equivalence relations E and F on
standard Borel spaces X and Y , respectively, a Borel homomorphism from E to F
is a Borel function f : X →Y such that for all x, y ∈X,
xEy
implies
f(x)Ff(y).
That is, f is a definable way of assigning objects in Y up to F-equivalence as
invariants for objects in X up to E-equivalence. Such an f is a Borel reduction
from E to F if, moreover, for all x, y ∈X,
xEy
if and only if
f(x)Ff(y).
In other words, the objects in Y modulo F are complete invariants for those in
X modulo E, computed via f. If such a reduction exists, we say that E is Borel
reducible to F and write E ≤B F. If E ≤B F and F ≤B E, we say that E and
F are Borel bireducible or Borel equivalent, written E ∼B F. Invariant descriptive
set theory is the study of such definable equivalence relations and the reducibilities
between them; for a general reference, we direct readers to [Gao09].
Borel reducibility provides a way of comparing the complexity of different clas-
sification problems, realized as analytic equivalence relations on suitable spaces,
across mathematics. The simplest such equivalence relations, which we will call
concretely classifiable,3 are those which are Borel reducible to the equality relation
=R on R (or, equivalently, any uncountable standard Borel space); in other words,
they admit numerical complete invariants in a definable way.
3Such equivalence relations are usually called “smooth” in the descriptive set theory literature;
we will forego this terminology to avoid the obvious potential confusion. Within this class are, of
course, benchmarks of an even greater simplicity; the complete picture is =1 <B =2 <B · · · <B
=N <B =R.

12
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
A paradigmatic example of a Borel equivalence relation which is not concretely
classifiable is the relation E0 of eventual equality between infinite binary sequences:
(xn)n∈N E0 (yn)n∈N
if and only if
∃m ∈N ∀n ≥m(xn = yn),
for (xn)n∈N, (yn)n∈N ∈{0, 1}N. In fact, E0 is (up to bireducibility) the least such
equivalence relation, by the following fundamental result [HKL90].
• (Glimm–Effros Dichotomy) If E is a Borel equivalence relation on a
Polish space then either E is concretely classifiable or E0 ≤B E.
For a second example, consider the orbit equivalence relation EZ induced by the
shift action of Z on the space {0, 1}Z:
m · (xn)n∈Z = (xn−m)n∈Z
for m ∈Z and (xn)n∈Z ∈{0, 1}Z. E0 and EZ are both hyperfinite, meaning that
they can be expressed as countable increasing unions of Borel equivalence relations
with finite classes, and they are Borel bireducible.
In general, if G is a Polish topological group acting in a Borel way on a standard
Borel space X, meaning that the induced map G × X →X is Borel, then the re-
sulting orbit equivalence relation on X is analytic. A particularly salient family of
examples are the isomorphism relations on classes of countable first-order (e.g., re-
lational or algebraic) structures. For instance, we can view any countably infinite
group as being the set N endowed with a ternary relation m ⊆N3 which codes the
group operation: m(p, q, r) if and only if pq = r. In this way, there is a Borel subset
Grp of the space {0, 1}N3 of all ternary relations on N, identified via characteristic
functions, which parametrizes the class of all countably infinite groups. The infinite
symmetric group S∞of all permutations of N then acts on {0, 1}N3 in a natural
way, called the logic action:
(g · m)(p, q, r) = m(g−1(p), g−1(q), g−1(r))
for g ∈S∞, m ∈{0, 1}N3, and p, q, r ∈N. The resulting orbit equivalence relation,
when restricted to Grp, is exactly the isomorphism relation ∼=Grp on all countably-
infinite groups. Similar constructions parametrize all classes of countably-infinite
first-order structures C, with their isomorphism relations ∼=C induced by Borel ac-
tions of S∞.
An analytic equivalence relation E is classifiable by countable structures if there is
some class C of countable first-order structures such that E ≤B ∼=C. This precisely
describes those classification problems which admit countable algebraic complete
invariants in a definable way. By the previous paragraph, any such equivalence
relation is reducible to the orbit equivalence relation of a Borel action of S∞; the
converse holds as well (see [Gao09, Thm. 3.6.1]). Such an E is, moreover, com-
plete for countable structures4 if ∼=C ≤B E for all classes of countable first-order
structures C. The isomorphism relations on the classes of countable groups, graphs,
rings, linear orders [FS89], and, as will be relevant for our Section 4, Boolean alge-
bras [CG01], are all complete for countable structures.
4Beginning with [FS89], such equivalence relations have typically been called “Borel complete”.
However, we feel that this terminology is misleading as these equivalence relations are never
themselves Borel (there is no Borel equivalence relation of maximal complexity), and thus conflicts
with the established usage of “
complete” in the context of other reducibility notions. We also
forgo the terminology “S∞-complete”, which while technically correct and carrying the benefit of
brevity, obscures the relationship to countable structures.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
13
A Borel equivalence relation E on X is countable if each equivalence class is
countable; these are exactly the orbit equivalence relations of Borel actions of
countable groups [FM77].
A countable Borel equivalence relation is called uni-
versal (countable) if every countable Borel equivalence relation reduces to it. Such
equivalence relations often arise through the actions of nonabelian free groups. For
example, the shift action of the free group on two generators, F2, on {0, 1}F2 pro-
duces a universal countable Borel equivalence relation [DJK94], denoted (possibly
up to bireducibility) by E∞.
A Borel equivalence relation is essentially countable if it is Borel bireducible5
with a countable Borel equivalence relation. Especially important for the present
work is the fact that orbit equivalence relations induced by Borel actions of locally
compact Polish groups are essentially countable [Kec92]. Every (essentially) count-
able Borel equivalence relation is classifiable by countable structures, but those
which are complete for countable structures are never essentially countable. Such
a Borel equivalence relation is essentially hyperfinite (or essentially universal) if it
is bireducible with a hyperfinite (universal, respectively) countable Borel equiva-
lence relation. Note that being essentially hyperfinite is equivalent to being Borel
reducible to E0 (see [DJK94]).
In closing out this section, we wish to highlight some of the reasons why Borel re-
ducibility is so propitious a notion for studying classification problems in (separable)
mathematics. Firstly, it is generous enough to capture most, if not all, examples of
complete invariants which can be computed in a “reasonably definable” way. One
may argue that it is too generous a notion, but as much of the power of this theory
comes from its ability to show that one equivalence relation does not reduce to
another, the weaker notion of definability (e.g., via Borel, as opposed to continuous
or computable, functions) translates to stronger negative results. Furthermore, if
we are given two different ways to parametrize a class of mathematical objects as
elements of standard Borel spaces, on which the natural notion of equivalence is
analytic, then the translations between the parametrizations will, invariably, be
given by Borel reductions.6
On the other hand, Borel reducibility is strict enough a notion to draw much
finer distinctions than we could otherwise make if we allowed arbitrary reductions;
the latter amounts to invoking the Axiom of Choice to compare the cardinalities of
the resulting quotients. But while the cardinalities of quotients X/E, where E is
an analytic equivalence relation on a standard Borel space X, are limited to being
countable, ℵ1, or 2ℵ0 (see [Bur78]), there are continuum many different degrees up
to Borel bireducibility and they are far from being even linearly ordered [AK00].
Lastly, the existence or non-existence of a Borel reduction is highly robust; it is not
particularly sensitive to the ambient set-theoretic universe and thus immune to the
sorts of independence phenomena that are so otherwise common in set theory. In
the terminology of the field, it is absolute.7
5Being Borel reducible to a countable Borel equivalence relation is not, in general, equivalent to
being essentially countable [Hjo05]; however, the notions coincide for equivalence relations induced
by Polish group actions [KM16].
6This is a kind of “Church–Turing Thesis” for Borel reducibility; see [Gao09, p. 328].
7More concretely, whether there is a Borel reduction between two fixed Borel equivalence rela-
tions is a Σ1
2 statement and thus absolute between transitive models of set theory by Shoenfield’s
Absoluteness Theorem. For analytic equivalence relations, the existence of a Borel reduction is

2.3. Spaces of subsets. Throughout this section, we consider a fixed locally com-
pact Polish space X. We denote by K(X), F(X), and O(X) the collections of all
compact, closed, and open subsets of X, respectively. We view K(X) as a Polish
space with the familiar Vietoris topology, generated by a subbasis consisting of open
sets of the form {K ∈K(X) : K ⊆U} and {K ∈K(X) : K ∩U ̸= ∅}, where U is
open in X. When X is endowed with a compatible metric, the Vietoris topology
is that induced by the Hausdorff metric on K(X). We wish to describe suitable
topological and Borel structures on both F(X) and O(X).
The Fell topology on F(X) is generated by open sets of the form {F ∈F(X) :
F ∩K = ∅} and {F ∈F(X) : F ∩U ̸= ∅}, where K and U range over the com-
pact and open subsets of X, respectively. When endowed with this topology, F(X)
is a compact Polish space [Kec95, Exer. 12.7]. We endow O(X) with the topol-
ogy inherited from F(X) by declaring complementation to be a homeomorphism
F(X) →O(X). The Fell and Vietoris topologies coincide on F(X) when X is
compact, and the former may be viewed as induced by the latter via the one-point
compactification of X; see [BC13].
The above topology was originally isolated by Chabauty [Cha50] for the space of
closed subgroups of a locally compact group. Later, and apparently independently,
Fell [Fel62] defined his topology on the closed subsets of an arbitrary locally compact
space. For this reason, in the geometric literature, this topology is often referred to
as the Chabauty or Chabauty–Fell topology; it is also (following Thurston [Thu97])
sometimes termed the geometric topology, in part due to the following lemma; see
p. 161 of [BP92] for a proof and the discussions in Sections 5, 7, and 8 below for
its manifestations in spaces of quotient manifolds.
Lemma 2.1. A sequence (Fn)n∈N converges to a closed set F in the Fell topology
on F(X) if and only if:
i. every x ∈F is the limit in X of some sequence (xi)i∈N, where xi ∈Fi for
all i ∈N, and
ii. every limit in X of a convergent sequence (xi)i∈N, where (Fni)i∈N is an
infinite subsequence of (Fn)n∈N and xi ∈Fni for all i ∈N, is in F.
We will adopt the convention of using “Fell topology” when referring to the spaces
F(X) and O(X) for a general locally compact Polish space X, and “Chabauty–Fell
topology” for the space of closed subgroups of a Lie group.
Being Polish, the Fell topology induces a standard Borel structure on both F(X)
and O(X). This structure coincides with the Effros Borel structure [Eff65] on F(X);
note that the latter is in fact generated by the collection of sets {F ∈F(X) :
F ∩K = ∅}, for K compact in X, alone. It will be important to know that various
standard relations and operations on F(X) and O(X) are Borel measurable, so we
list the most fundamental of these here (see [Kec95, Exer. 12.11 and 27.7]):
• The subset relation {(F1, F2) : F1 ⊆F2)} is Borel in F(X)2.
• The union operation (F1, F2) 7→F1 ∪F2 is a Borel map F(X)2 →F(X).
Σ1
3, and hence is, under sufficient large cardinal assumptions, absolute between generic extensions.
See [Jec03] for details on absoluteness.

By taking complements, each of the above induces a corresponding Borel map
or relation on O(X). Notably, while the Effros Borel structure can be defined on
any Polish space, it is the local compactness of X which ensures that intersection is
a Borel map on F(X), and thus also that union is a Borel map on O(X). We also
have the following fact (see [Chr74, p. 74]), for which local compactness is again
crucial:
• The operation F 7→X \ F is a Borel map F(X) →F(X). Consequently,
so are the maps U 7→U from O(X) →F(X) and U 7→X \ U from
O(X) →O(X).
For verifications that other fundamental relations between elements of O(X) are
Borel, see Section 3.3 below.
3. Parameter spaces of manifolds
3.1. Pseudogroups G, model spaces X, and spaces of (G, X)-manifolds.
In this section, we describe a unified framework for parametrizing many familiar
classes of manifolds. This framework applies the pseudogroup approach, perhaps
most prominently associated to Thurston and his study of geometric structures
on manifolds, as expounded in [Thu97].8 We begin with a brief overview of this
approach.
Definition 3.1. A pseudogroup G on a topological space X is a set of homeomor-
phisms between open subsets of X satisfying the following conditions:
i. the domains of the elements of G cover X,
ii. the restriction of any element of G to an open subset of X is also in G,
iii. the composition of any two elements of G is in G,
iv. the inverse of any element of G is in G, and
v. if φ : U →V is a homeomorphism between open subsets U and V of X,
and U is covered by a collection of open sets Uα with the property that the
restriction φ|Uα is in G for every α, then φ is in G.
We note that in condition (iii), we permit compositions of arbitrary pairs of
elements of G, that is, if φ : U1 →V1 and ψ : U2 →V2 are in G, then ψ ◦φ is
the resulting homeomorphism from U1 ∩φ−1[U2] onto V2 ∩ψ[V1]; included here is
the possibility that these sets may each be empty. Condition (v), which says that
G is “local”, can also be viewed as saying that unions of elements of G, when they
produce a homeomorphism, lie in G.
8The pseudogroup concept itself, though, traces back at least to [VW31]; see also [Gol19] for
a history.

16
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
The pseudogroups on any topological space X are naturally ordered by inclu-
sion. This ordering possesses both a maximum and a minimum element — namely,
the collection of all homeomorphisms between pairs of open subsets of X and the
collection of all identity maps on open subsets of X, respectively. Any collection
of homeomorphisms between open subsets of X falls in some unique minimal pseu-
dogroup, which it may thus be said to generate [Thu97, Exer. 3.1.8.a] (see also
Example 3.17 below). In particular, any group G of homeomorphisms of X may
be said to generate a pseudogroup, which we denote by a sans-serif G; these form
a prominent class of examples.
Definition 3.2. Let X denote Rn, Cn, hyperbolic space Hn = {(x1, . . . , xn) ∈Rn |
xn > 0}, or the n-dimensional unit sphere Sn, the latter two endowed with their
standard hyperbolic and spherical metrics, respectively.
1. Top is the topological pseudogroup on X, the collection of all homeomor-
phisms between open subsets of X. This is the maximal pseudogroup on
X referred to above.
2. Ck (1 ≤k ≤∞) is the pseudogroup of all Ck-diffeomorphisms between
pairs of open subsets of X.
3. In the case of X = Cn, Hol is the pseudogroup of all biholomorphic maps
between open subsets of Cn.
4. Isom is the pseudogroup generated by the group of isometries of X, and
Isom+ is that generated by the orientation-preserving isometries of X.
As in the above examples, we will often omit mention of the model space X when
it is clear from context.
The standard definition of a topological n-manifold may now be phrased as:
a second countable Hausdorff space possessing an atlas of charts whose transition
maps all lie in the topological pseudogroup on Rn. To define other sorts of manifolds,
we simply vary the pseudogroup appearing therein.
Definition 3.3. Let M and X be topological spaces and G a pseudogroup on X.
1. A (G, X)-atlas on M is a collection of pairs (U, φ), called charts, where U is
an open subset of M and φ : U →X is a homeomorphism onto its image,
such that the sets U cover M, and whenever (U, φ) and (V, ψ) are charts
with U ∩V ̸= ∅, the transition map
ψ ◦φ−1|φ[U∩V ] : φ[U ∩V ] →ψ[U ∩V ]
is in G.
2. M is a locally (G, X)-space if it is endowed with a (G, X)-atlas; if M is, in
addition, Hausdorff and second countable, it is a (G, X)-manifold.
Our language is deliberately neutral with respect to the question of whether a
choice of atlas forms part of a manifold’s intrinsic structure. Philosophically, it
does not [Thu97, p. 111]. In the generality in which we wish to work, however, the
most natural parametrizations of various classes of manifolds will be by way of just
such choices of atlases; hence in the course of these parametrizations, we will to
some degree regard such a choice as part of the data of a manifold. The essential
point for our purposes is that the relation of compatibility among such choices will
always be subsumed by the relation of manifold equivalence (Definition 3.20) under
consideration. Readers in the meantime should feel free to adopt whichever of these
perspectives suits them best.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
17
Example 3.4. The topological, smooth, and complex n-manifolds are precisely
the (Top, Rn), (C∞, Rn), and (Hol, Cn)-manifolds, respectively.
The Euclidean,
spherical, and hyperbolic n-manifolds are precisely the (Isom, Rn), (Isom, Sn), and
(Isom, Hn)-manifolds, respectively.9 Manifolds with boundary fit into this frame-
work by letting Rn
+ = {(x1, . . . , xn) ∈Rn | xn ≥0}; the topological and smooth n-
manifolds with boundary are then precisely the (Top, Rn
+)-manifolds and (C∞, Rn
+)-
manifolds, respectively.
As seen in the preceding examples (see also [Thu97, pp. 111–118]), the most
prominent classes of finite-dimensional (G, X)-manifolds are all modelled on spaces
X which are both Polish and locally compact. Since the latter condition carries
the additional benefit of entailing a more pleasant Borel structure on its space of
open subsets O(X) (see Section 2.3), in what follows we will always take X to be
a locally compact Polish space. We will comment, briefly, on infinite-dimensional
manifolds in Section 9.
We turn now to the parametrization, for some fixed pseudogroup G on X, of the

• m is the underlying set of M,
• U = {Ui | i ∈I} is a family of open subsets of X, and
• c is the collection of chart-maps patching the structures in U together “along
m”; in other words, for each Ui in U, there exists a Vi ⊆m and bijection
φi : Vi →Ui in c.
For notational convenience, we will write Ui,j for φi[Vi ∩Vj] below and Ui for Ui,i.
Provided it is second countable and Hausdorff, M is then a (G, X)-manifold if and

Our procedure will therefore be as follows: we will parametrize the collection of

setting for our subsequent work. We will conclude this subsection by verifying that
the most prominent pseudogroups G are indeed Borel.

Within this framework, it will be useful to term any Ui a chart, any Ui,j an overlap,

Any pair (U, c) as above will determine a locally (G, X)-space M(U,c) in the

such space admits a representation of this form.
We turn now to the Borel structure on P(G, X). As in Section 2.3, we endow
the set O(X) of open subsets of X with the Effros Borel structure, or equivalently,
the Borel structure induced by the Fell topology. We define a pseudogroup on X
to be Borel when its Borel structure is compatible with that of O(X), as follows:
Definition 3.6. A pseudogroup G on X is Borel if it is endowed with a standard
Borel structure such that each of the following maps are Borel on the relevant
spaces:
i. domain projection G →O(X) : φ 7→dom(φ),
ii. range projection G →O(X) : φ 7→ran(φ),
iii. domain inclusion O(X) →G : U 7→idU, the identity map on U,
iv. direct image O(X) × G →O(X) : (U, φ) 7→φ[U],
v. inverse image O(X) × G →O(X) : (U, φ) 7→φ−1[U],
vi. restriction O(X) × G →G : (U, φ) 7→φ|U,
vii. composition G × G →G : (φ, ψ) 7→ψ ◦φ, and
viii. inversion G →G : φ 7→φ−1.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
19
There are some obvious redundancies in the above definition, but we provide
the full list for ease of reference. We note that a pseudogroup G on X forms an
example of a groupoid, a (small) category in which all arrows are invertible: the set
of objects is O(X) and the set of arrows is G. By properties (iii), (vii), and (viii)
above, every Borel pseudogroup is a Borel groupoid, in the terminology of [Lup17].
The following lemma is now immediate from Definitions 3.5 and 3.6, together with
the fact that the subset relation and intersection operation on O(X) are Borel.
Lemma 3.7. If G is a Borel pseudogroup on a locally compact Polish space X,
then P(G, X) inherits from O(X)N×N × GN×N a standard Borel structure.
□
Definition 3.8. Let G be a pseudogroup on a locally compact Polish space X.
The parameter space M(G, X) of (G, X)-manifolds is the set of all pairs (U, c) in
P(G, X) such that M(U,c) is a (G, X)-manifold, or, equivalently, is Hausdorff.
In order to show that M(G, X) has the structure of a standard Borel space,
it suffices to show that it is a Borel subset of P(G, X), whenever G is a Borel
pseudogroup. The essential (and somewhat surprising) difficulty, then, is to show
that whether a pair (U, c) describes a Hausdorff manifold can be expressed in a
Borel way in the parameter space P(G, X).
Theorem 3.9. If G is a Borel pseudogroup on a locally compact Polish space X,
then M(G, X) is a Borel subset of P(G, X). In particular, M(G, X) forms a stan-
dard Borel space.
Proof. Our proof proceeds in two parts: first we show that M(G, X) is a coanalytic
subset of P(G, X), then we show that it is an analytic subset of P(G, X). It thus
follows by Suslin’s Theorem that M(G, X) is Borel. These two characterizations
of M(G, X) may be loosely regarded as targeting the equivalent conditions, for a
second countable topological manifold, of Hausdorffness and normality, respectively.
More precisely, we will first show that
¬Haus := {(U, c) ∈P(G, X) | M(U,c) is not Hausdorff}
is an analytic subset of P(G, X). For convenience, fix a countable basis B for the
topology on X. ¬Haus is the collection of those (U, c) ∈P(G, X) for which there
exists an (x, y) ∈X2 such that the following hold:
i. x ̸= y, and
ii. ∃i, j ∈N such that
a. x ∈Ui and y ∈Uj, and
b. ∀V, W ∈B [(x ∈V ∧y ∈W) →φi,j[V ] ∩W ̸= ∅].
Item (i) is an open condition in X2 and the conditions in (ii.a) are each open in
X × O(X)N×N, where O(X) has the Fell topology. All enumerated quantifications
are countable, and the implication in (ii.b) may be rewritten as
¬(x ∈V ∧y ∈W) ∨φi,j[V ] ∩W ̸= ∅,
the left half of which is closed in X2. This leaves only the right half: for any two
open sets V, W ⊆X, we would like {φ ∈G | φ[V ]∩W ̸= ∅} to be a Borel subset of G.
This is indeed the case since the direct image is a Borel operation O(X)×G →O(X),
intersection is a Borel operation in O(X), and φ[V ] ∩W ̸= ∅can be verified by
checking whether qi ∈φ[V ] ∩W, where qi (i ∈N) is a fixed enumeration of a
countable dense subset of X. Thus, the collection of ((x, y), (U, c)) ∈X2 ×P(G, X)

20

satisfying items (i) and (ii) above is Borel. The projection of this collection onto
P(G, X) is exactly ¬Haus, concluding the argument.

Hi,j = {((V, W), (U, c)) ∈O(X)2 × P(G, X) | V ⊇Ui\Ui,j and W ⊇Uj\Uj,i

Fixing i and j, note that the restricted

Remark 3.10. The pattern of the above argument will recur throughout this
work, in particular in the proofs of Theorem 3.23 and Lemma 3.45 that the classes
of compact manifolds and complete manifolds, respectively, are each Borel. Note
that, as for Hausdorff-ness, the naive framing of each — for all “reals” (x, y ∈X,
basic open covers (Ui)i∈I, Cauchy sequences (xn)n∈N, etc.), there exists a Borel
phenomenon (disjoint open sets, a finite subcover, a limit, etc.) — is Π1
1 in form.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
21
Fortunately for our purposes, and perhaps for reasons not unconnected to their
centrality, each of these notions, just as above, admits a Σ1
1 formulation as well.
It is this dynamic which accounts for the prominence of Suslin’s Theorem in our
arguments.
It remains to show that the relevant pseudogroups are, in fact, Borel. Since Top
is the largest pseudogroup, we begin there; other pseudogroups will be endowed
with the Borel structure they inherit as Borel subsets of Top. Our description of
this Borel structure and the proof that it is Borel are based on [HK00, Lem. 7.2].
We fix an injective enumeration qi (for i ∈N) of a countable dense subset of
X and u (for “undefined”) a default value outside of X. Consider X ∪{u} with
the standard Borel structure induced by adding u to X as an isolated point. The
standard Borel structure which we place on Top is that resulting from identifying
each element φ ∈Top with the triple consisting of its domain, its range, and its
sequence of values on the qi’s, using u to indicate where it is not defined. Explicitly:
Definition 3.11. Let T ⊆O(X) × O(X) × (X ∪{u})N be the set of all triples
(U, V, (ri)i∈N) in O(X) × O(X) × (X ∪{u})N such that:
i. if qi ∈U, then ri ∈V , while if qi /∈U, then ri = u, and
ii. there exists a homeomorphism φ : U →V such that for each qi ∈U,
φ(qi) = ri.
It will also be convenient in what follows to fix a compatible complete metric d
on X and an enumeration Kℓ(for ℓ∈N) of compact subsets of X which are closures
of precompact basic open sets, so that every open subset of X can be expressed
as a union of some Kℓ’s; such a family exists by the local compactness and second
countability of X. We note that O(X) can also be given a compatible metric and
so T will inherit a metric structure from O(X)×O(X)×(X ∪{u})N, but in general
we have no reason to suppose that the resulting topology is Polish (or equivalently,
that T is a Gδ subspace of the latter).
Lemma 3.12. T is a Borel subset of O(X) × O(X) × (X ∪{u})N.
Proof. We leave it to the reader to convince themselves that condition (i) in Def-
inition 3.11 is Borel and instead focus on (ii). Let Q+ denote the set of positive
rational numbers. Observe that (U, V, (ri)i∈N) ∈O(X)×O(X)×(X∪{u})N satisfies
(ii) if and only if the following conditions hold:
a. For all Kℓ⊆U,
∀ϵ ∈Q+∃δ ∈Q+∀qi, qj ∈Kℓ(d(qi, qj) < δ →d(ri, rj) < ϵ).
b.
∀i ̸= j(qi, qj ∈U →ri ̸= rj)
c.
∀ϵ ∈Q+∀qi ∈V ∃rj ∈V (d(qi, rj) < ϵ)
d. For all Kℓ⊆V ,
∀ϵ ∈Q+∃δ ∈Q+∀ri, rj ∈Kℓ(d(ri, rj) < δ →d(qi, qj) < ϵ).
Condition (a) says that the association qi 7→ri is uniformly continuous on a dense
subset of each of the compact sets Kℓin U, which is equivalent, since U is covered by
these Kℓ’s, to inducing a continuous function U →V . Condition (b) says that this
function, at least on the qi’s, is injective, and (c) says that it has dense image in V .

22
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Lastly, (d) says that the association ri 7→qi likewise induces a continuous function
V →U. We are using the default value u /∈X to avoid accidentally associating an
ri ∈V to some qi /∈U. The uniqueness of continuous extensions of functions from
dense domains will ensure that the two resulting maps are inverse to each other.
As all quantifiers range over countable sets, these are all Borel conditions.
□
We endow Top with the standard Borel structure it inherits from its natural
identification with T .

is Borel on the relevant spaces.
Proof. Observe that

and note that φ(x) ∈B is a Borel condition in (x, φ) since we can choose, uniformly
and in a Borel way, a subsequence (qin)n∈N of the qi’s which converges to x and
ask whether limn rin ∈B, where φ is represented by (U, V, (ri)i∈N) in T .
□
Theorem 3.14. Top is a Borel pseudogroup.
Proof. We must verify Borel-ness of the maps (i)–(viii) in Definition 3.6 of Borel
pseudogroup. First, note that the (i) domain and (ii) range projections Top →
O(X) are Borel, as they are just the coordinate projections O(X) × O(X) × (X ∪
{u})N →O(X). To see that the (iii) domain inclusion id : O(X) →Top is Borel,
note that id(U) = φ in Top, where φ is represented by (V, W, (ri)i∈N) in T , if and
only if U = V = W and for all i ∈N,
ri =
(
qi
if qi ∈U,
u
else.
These are clearly Borel conditions.
It is convenient to check (viii) inversion next.
We do this by verifying that
the graph {(φ, ψ) ∈Top × Top : ψ = φ−1} of this operation is Borel. Writing
(U1, V1, (ri)i∈N) and (U2, V2, (si)i∈N) for the representatives of φ in ψ respectively,
in T , observe that ψ = φ−1 if and only if U2 = V1, V2 = U1, ψ(ri) = qi for all
qi ∈U1 and φ(si) = qi for all qi ∈U2. The last two clauses are Borel conditions by
Lemma 3.13.
For the (iv) direct image, we again check that the graph of this operation is
Borel in O(X) × Top × O(X). Given (W1, φ, W2) ∈O(X) × Top × O(X), say with
(U, V, (ri)i∈N) representing φ in T , φ[W1] ⊆W2 if and only if φ[W1 ∩U] ⊆W2 ∩V ,
which in turn is equivalent to saying that for all Kℓ⊆W1 ∩U, φ[Kℓ] ⊆W2 ∩V .
Since intersection is a Borel operation in O(X) and φ[Kℓ] ⊆W2 ∩V if and only if
for all qi ∈Kℓ, φ(qi) ∈W2 ∩V , this is a Borel condition. We can then use inversion
to get that W2 ⊆φ[W1] is Borel as well. The (v) inverse image then follows from
another application of inversion.

Example 3.15. Let X be Rn, Cn, Hn, or Sn and consider the pseudogroup Ck
(1 ≤k < ∞) of all Ck-diffeomorphisms between open subsets of X. The proof
of [HK00, Lem. 7.2] essentially shows, in the case X = Cn, that C1 = Hol is a
Borel subset of T by arguing that continuity of the matrix of partial derivatives
can be determined on a fixed countable dense set. This can be easily adapted for
higher-order derivatives to show that Ck is a Borel subset of Top and, thus, a Borel
pseudogroup. Since C∞= T∞
k=1 Ck, it is also a Borel pseudogroup.
Next, we turn to pseudogroups generated by groups of homeomorphisms.
Lemma 3.16. Let X be a locally compact Polish space and Homeo(X) its group
of self-homeomorphisms. Then, Homeo(X) is a Borel subset of Top and the Borel
structures induced by Top and the compact-open topology on Homeo(X) coincide.
Proof. That Homeo(X) is a Borel subset of Top is immediate from Theorem 3.14
and the fact that, for φ ∈Top, φ ∈Homeo(X) if and only if dom(φ) = ran(φ) = X.

V (K, U) = {φ ∈Homeo(X) | φ[K] ⊆U},

topology is Borel with respect to the structure inherited from Top.
But then,

standard Borel spaces and so they must coincide.

Example 3.17. Let X be a locally compact Polish space and G a group of home-
omorphisms on X which is Borel with respect to the compact-open topology (or
equivalently, by Lemma 3.16, as a subset of Top) and has the following property:
any g ∈G is uniquely determined by its values on any nonempty open set. We note
that this holds for the groups of isometries of Rn, Sn, and Hn, with the Euclidean,
spherical, and hyperbolic metrics, respectively (see [BH99, Prop. I.2.18] or [Rat19,
§6.6], where such metric spaces are termed rigid). We claim the pseudogroup gen-
erated by G is Borel.
Let G ⊆Top be the set of all those φ : U →V for which there is some open
covering Uα (for α ∈A) of U such that for each α ∈A, there is some g ∈G such
that φ|Uα = g|Uα∩U. Clearly G contains G, and it is easy to check that it is a
pseudogroup on X. Moreover, any pseudogroup containing G must contain G, so
G is the pseudogroup generated by G. To see that G is Borel, fix a sequence Un
(for n ∈N) of nonempty basic open subsets of X. A homeomorphism φ : U →V
in Top is then in G if and only if there is a subsequence Unk (for k ∈N) of the
Un’s such that S
k∈N Unk ⊇U and for every k ∈N, there is some g ∈G such that
φ|Unk = g|Unk ∩U. This is a Σ1
1 description of G. Moreover, we can also give a Π1
1
description: φ ∈G if and only if for every x ∈U, there is an n ∈N such that
x ∈Un ⊆U and φ|Un ∈{g|Un∩U | g ∈G}. Our rigidity assumption on G ensures
that, for each n ∈N such that Un ∩U ̸= ∅, the set {g|Un∩U | g ∈G} is the image
of the Borel set G under an injective Borel mapping and is thus Borel. Hence, G is
Borel by an application of Suslin’s Theorem.
In particular, the pseudogroup Isom(Hn) generated by the group Isom(Hn) of all
isometries of Hn is Borel, as is the pseudogroup Isom+(Hn) generated by the group
of all orientation-preserving isometries of Hn. This will be important in Section 5
and its sequels below.
Example 3.18. Among the virtues of the pseudogroup approach are the compar-
isons it facilitates between the multiple sorts of structures — smooth, Riemann-
ian, etc. — which a given topological manifold may admit. Systematic relations
between such structures will tend to manifest in our framework as Borel maps
f : M(G, X) →M(H, Y ); among the simplest such examples is the inclusion of
the class of manifolds without boundary into the class of manifolds with boundary
(under the standard convention that the latter means “with possibly trivial bound-
ary”): Here G = H = Top or C∞, X = Rn, Y = Rn
+, and any embedding of X
into Y will induce a Borel map f as above; the details are left to the reader. Two
remarks on this front are in order: (i) We will adhere below to the somewhat stan-
dard convention that the absence of the phrase “with boundary” connotes “without
boundary”. (ii) The f just defined is in fact a Borel reduction of ∼=G to ∼=H in the
sense of Definition 3.20 just below.
For another example, consider the inclusion of the class of smooth manifolds into
the class of topological manifolds; here G = C∞, H = Top, and X = Y = Rn. This
map is plainly Borel, and thus a Borel reduction of ∼=C∞to ∼=Top precisely when
topological n-manifolds admit a unique smooth structure up to diffeomorphism. By
[Moi77, Mun60], this in turn holds in exactly the dimensions n ≤3; see Question 6
of our conclusion and the discussion which follows it for related matters.
Remark 3.19. To better motivate our approach to manifold parametrization, let
us briefly survey a few of its more obvious alternatives. Most prominent among
these, perhaps, is that of this paper’s main forerunner [HK00], and indeed, aspects

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
25
of our constructions are only minor modifications of those appearing therein. Its
approach differs most decisively from ours, though, in encoding manifolds as metric
structures endowed with supplementary (complex) structure — a natural enough
choice in that work’s contexts, but less so in the setting, for example, of topological
manifolds. The infelicity we have in mind here is, firstly, a conceptual one: an
analysis of the homeomorphism relation among topological manifolds M by way of
metric structures amounts to selecting compatible metrics for such M only to forget
them. Similar remarks apply to parametrizations via Gromov–Hausdorff metrics, or
to parametrizations via manifolds’ embedded images in some fixed Rn, for example.
But there are more mathematical downsides to each of these alternatives as well: it
is, in general, far from clear that the embedded manifolds of a given type define a
Borel subcollection of F(Rn); Gromov–Hausdorff metrics on noncompact manifolds
call for basepoints; mixed registers (metric and complex, for example) can grow, in
practice, unwieldy to manipulate. To be clear, though, we don’t wish to suggest
that these alternatives aren’t well-suited to any number of particular contexts, but
only that the modularity and transparency of the pseudogroup approach accords it
several distinctive advantages in the generality in which we wish to work.
3.2. Equivalences of manifolds and the case of compact manifolds. We
have shown that a Borel pseudogroup G on a locally compact Polish space X deter-
mines a standard Borel parametrization M(G, X) of the class of (G, X)-manifolds.
The natural notion of equivalence among such manifolds determines in turn an
equivalence relation on M(G, X).
Definition 3.20. A homeomorphism f : M →N between (G, X)-manifolds M and
N is a (G, X)-isomorphism if the induced maps between the charts on M and N
are locally elements of G [Thu97, p. 111]. The (G, X)- prefix will be omitted when
it is clear from context. By ∼=G we denote the equivalence relation on M(G, X)
given by letting (U, c) ∼=G (V, d) if and only if M(U,c) and M(V,d) are isomorphic.
Our main aim in the present subsection is to show that, for any Borel pseu-
dogroup G on a locally compact Polish space X, this relation ∼=G is analytic. From
this result it promptly follows that any Borel subspace of M(G, X) containing count-
ably many ∼=G-equivalence classes is Borel reducible to the relation =N of equality
on the natural numbers. Several of the most fundamental manifold classification
problems manifest in precisely this way, namely the classification of 0-manifolds,
of 1-manifolds, of compact topological manifolds (with or without boundary), and
of compact smooth manifolds (with or without boundary); by showing that the as-
sociated parameter spaces are Borel, we will establish that each of these problems
is Borel equivalent to =N. As a corollary, the computation of any ∼=G-invariant of
compact topological manifolds is a Borel operation.
We precede this section’s main theorem with the following observation, whose
straightforward verification is left to the reader.
Lemma 3.21. The map which takes an open subset of any Ui in U to its (U, c)-
saturation is Borel. More precisely, the map O(X) × N × M(G, X) →O(X)N given
by
(U, i, U, c) 7→⟨φi,j[U ∩Ui] | j ∈N⟩
is Borel.
□
Theorem 3.22. For any Borel pseudogroup G on a locally compact Polish space
X, the equivalence relation ∼=G on M(G, X) is analytic.

26
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
This assertion and its proof are essentially a translation of those of [HK00, Prop.
3.3] to our more generalized setting. In this proof and what follows, we adopt the
convention that, for parameters (U, c) and (V, d) in M(G, X), elements of U and V
are denoted by Ui,j (or Ui) and Vi,j (or Vi), respectively, while elements of c and d
are denoted by φi,j and ψi,j, respectively.
Proof. A G-good map M(U,c) →M(V,d) between two (G, X)-manifolds is a local
homeomorphism which respects their (G, X) structures; we parametrize such maps
via quadruples (X, Y, k, e) ∈O(X)N×N × O(X)N×N × NN×N × GN×N of the following
sort:
i. the family X = ⟨Xi,j ∈O(X) | (i, j) ∈N2⟩satisfies S
j∈N Xi,j = Ui for all
i ∈N;
ii. the family Y = ⟨Yi,j ∈O(X) | (i, j) ∈N2⟩satisfies Yi,j ⊆Vk(i,j) for all
(i, j) ∈N2;
iii. the family e = ⟨ρi,j : Xi,j →Yi,j | (i, j) ∈N2⟩of elements of G satisfies
ρi′,j′ ◦φi,i′ = ψk(i,j),k(i′,j′) ◦ρi,j
for all (i, i′, j, j′) ∈N4. We reiterate the possibility that the composition of
pseudogroup elements may be the empty set.
In words, (X, Y, k, e) is a family of open sets and pseudogroup elements subordinate
to the open sets in U and V which factor through the quotients M(U,c) and M(V,d),
or cohere in the sense of item (iii) above.
In particular, just as the domain of
any such element ρi,j should fall within a chart Ui of M(U,c), its range should fall
within a chart Vk(i,j) of M(V,d), and it is this latter relation which the function
k : N2 →N is recording. Write g(X,Y,k,e) for the induced mapping of the quotients
M(U,c) →M(V,d).
Let S = O(X)N×N × O(X)N×N × NN×N × GN×N, and for any (U, c) and (V, d) in
M(G, X) write Map((U, c), (V, d)) for the set of quadruples in S satisfying condi-
tions (i) through (iii) above. Note that, just as in [HK00, p. 133], these are Borel
conditions and hence the set
{((U, c), (V, d), (X, Y, k, e)) | (X, Y, k, e) ∈Map((U, c), (V, d))}
is a Borel subset of M(G, X) × M(G, X) × S. Similarly, the set Iso(M(G, X)) of all
((U, c), (V, d), (X, Y, k, e), (Z, W, ℓ, f)) ∈M(G, X) × M(G, X) × S × S
satisfying
• (X, Y, k, e) ∈Map((U, c), (V, d)),
• (Z, W, ℓ, f) ∈Map((V, d), (U, c)), and
• g(X,Y,k,e) = g(Z,W,ℓ,f)−1
is Borel. To elaborate on the third part of this assertion, writing φi,j, ψi,j, ρi,j,
and θi,j for the functions in c, d, e, and f, respectively, we have that g(X,Y,k,e) =
g(Z,W,ℓ,f)−1 if and only if
• ψk(i′,j′),i ◦ρi′,j′ = θ−1
i,j ◦φi′,ℓ(i,j) on the intersection of these composite
functions’ domains for all i, j, i′, j′ ∈N, and
• the saturations of the images of the functions θi,j cover `
i∈N Ui,
the former of which is Borel by Definition 3.6, and the latter by Lemma 3.21. As
the equivalence relation ∼=G on M(G, X) is simply the projection of the Borel set
Iso(M(G, X)) to M(G, X) × M(G, X), this concludes the proof.
□

Note for later use that our argument shows that for any G as in Theorem 3.22
and Borel pseudogroup H ⊇G, the relation ∼=H on M(G, X) is analytic as well.
We turn now to the problem of classifying compact topological manifolds. Given
a Borel pseudogroup G on a locally compact Polish space X, we denote by K(G, X)
the set of all (U, c) ∈M(G, X) such that M(U,c) is compact.
Theorem 3.23. If G is a Borel pseudogroup on a locally compact Polish space X,
then K(G, X) forms a Borel subset of M(G, X).
Proof. Much as in our argument that Hausdorff manifolds form a Borel subset of
P(G, X), we will show that K(G, X) is both a coanalytic and analytic subset of
M(G, X); an application of Suslin’s Theorem will complete the proof.
The Π1

(U, c) such that every open cover of M(U,c) admits a finite subcover. More formally,
write p for the map taking U ∈O(X)N×N to (Uk)k∈N ∈O(X)N; fix also a basis
{Bi | i ∈N} for the topology of X. This determines in turn a basis for M(U,c),
and hence the pair (U, c) ∈M(G, X) parametrizes a compact topological manifold
if and only if for every f : N2 →N, if

For the Σ1

if and only if for some N ∈N there exist compact sets Ki ⊆Ui (i < N) whose
natural images cover M(U,c). At a more formal level, there are two things to check:
first, that the set A = {(K, U) | K ⊆U} is a Borel subset of K(X) × O(X),
and second, that the covering in question is a Borel condition. Beginning with the
second, note that if the natural images of Ui (i < N) cover M(U,c), then so too
will those of Ki (i < N) if and only if T

that the latter is evidently a Borel condition. Turning now to the first, invoke the
local compactness and second countability of X to write it as an increasing union
of compact sets {Xi | i ∈N}. Note next that Ai = {(K, U) | K ⊆U} is, in the Fell-
induced topology, an open subset of K(X) × O(Xi). Now let fi : O(X) →O(Xi)
denote the Borel map U 7→U ∩Xi, and observe that A = S
