This concludes the proof.
□
Our first Borel complexity computation follows almost immediately from the
preceding theorem in combination with Theorem 3.22 and Cheeger and Kister’s
1970 result that there are, up to homeomorphism, only countably many compact
topological manifolds. Within our introduction, this result figured as Theorem A.
Corollary 3.24. For all n ≥0, the homeomorphism relation ∼=Top on K(Top, Rn) is
Borel equivalent to =N; hence the homeomorphism relation ∼=Top on the parameter
space of all compact topological manifolds `
n∈N K(Top, Rn) is as well.
Proof. By [CK70], we may identify the set of homeomorphism classes of compact
topological n-manifolds with the natural numbers. By Theorems 3.22 and 3.23,

28
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
then, the map f : K(Top, Rn) →N taking (U, c) to the homeomorphism class of
M(U,c) is Borel, for the reason that any f−1({n}) is both analytic and the com-
plement of a countable union of analytic sets, and consequently Borel, again by
Suslin’s Theorem. Any reverse map N →K(Top, Rn) selecting a representative for
each homeomorphism class is trivially Borel as well, and will be a reduction of =N
to ∼=Top.
□
The next corollary tells us that we can compute any topological invariant for
compact manifolds, e.g., genus, Euler characteristic, homotopy groups, etc, in a
Borel way. Contrast this with a theorem of Becker [Bec92] which says that, among
arbitrary compact subsets of R2, being simply connected is a Π1
1-complete property.
Corollary 3.25. Any assignment of invariants to compact topological manifolds is
a Borel operation.
Proof. Any such assignment will amount to postcomposing the Borel reduction to
=N of the previous corollary with a map from N to a countable set.
□
The above line of reasoning applies more generally.
Corollary 3.26. Corollaries 3.24 and 3.25 continue to hold when the class of
compact topological manifolds is replaced with any of the following:
• compact topological manifolds with boundary,
• compact smooth manifolds (up to diffeomorphism), or
• compact smooth manifolds with boundary (up to diffeomorphism).
Proof. By Theorem 3.23, each of these classes of manifolds may be parametrized
by a standard Borel space; the assertion therefore reduces to the claim that each
of these classification problems involves only countably many equivalence classes.
In the case of the first item, this fact is again due to [CK70]; for the second and
third, apply the fact that any compact topological n-manifold M (with or without
boundary) admits, up to equivalence, only countably many smooth structures — in
fact only finitely many if n ̸= 4 (the countability bound follows from, e.g., [Cai61];
see [Che70] for the finiteness assertion).
□
Corollary 3.27. The classification of topological (or smooth) 0-manifolds and 1-
manifolds, possibly with boundary, up to homeomorphism (or diffeomorphism, re-
spectively) are each Borel equivalent to =N.
Proof. By a 0-manifold we mean a second countable Hausdorff space modelled on
R0 = {pt}; these are the countable discrete spaces, and they are completely classi-
fied by their cardinality. Each component of a 1-manifold is either homeomorphic
to a circle or to the real line [Lee11, Thm. 5.27]; the numbers of components of
each type provide a complete invariant for such manifolds. In each case the set of
invariants is countable, and the argument of Corollary 3.24 applies just as before
to complete the proof. Similar comments apply to 1-manifolds with boundary, and
in the smooth case.
□
3.3. Exhaustions, connectedness, and metrics on manifolds. This final sub-
section represents something of a toolbox for the remainder of the paper and, we
hope, for further work beyond it. Among the results we establish here, for appro-
priate choices of Borel pseudogroups G and model spaces X, are the existence of
Borel functions which compute:

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
29
• an exhaustion of any (G, X)-manifold by compact subsets (Lemma 3.31);
• a reparametrization of any (G, X)-manifold by one with locally finite charts
(Lemma 3.37);
• a reparametrization of any (G, X)-manifold by one with simply connected
charts (Lemma 3.39); and
• the connected components of any (G, X)-manifold (Lemma 3.41).
and Borel parameter spaces for:
• all connected (G, X)-manifolds (Lemma 3.40); and
• all (metrically) complete (G, X)-manifolds (Lemma 3.45).
We will also note a few irregularities involving quotient metrics, alongside ways,
within our framework, to address them. While these facts will be crucial to several
of our main theorems, they are of a technical nature, often describing how to
perform a standard construction in a Borel way. For this reason, we suggest that
first-time readers skim this section, returning to it as needed later on.
It will be useful throughout to have a uniform way of discussing open subsets
of manifolds within our parametrization. Let us fix a Borel pseudogroup G on a
locally compact Polish space X for the duration (we will place stronger demands
on G and X later on). Given (U, c) and (V, d) in M(G, X), we call M(V,d) an open
submanifold of M(U,c) if
(3)
Vi,j ⊆Ui,j and ψi,j = φi,j

Vi,j for all (i, j) ∈N2.
Here, as before, φi,j and ψi,j denote elements of c and d, respectively. If (3) holds,
we will write d = c|V. So defined, the open submanifolds of M(U,c) correspond
exactly to its open subsets. The corresponding relation on parameters, for which
we abuse terminology and say that (V, d) is an open submanifold of (U, c), is clearly
Borel on M(G, X). Among other alternatives, we can also view an open subset of
M(U,c) as given by a sequence (Vi)i∈N of open subsets of X such that Vi ⊆Ui for
all i ∈N. We can then build an open submanifold M(V,d), in the sense just defined,
which corresponds to the same underlying open set in M(U,c), using Lemma 3.21.
3.3.1. Exhaustions. A standard fact we’ve now tacitly used more than once is that
every locally compact Polish space, and in particular, every manifold M, admits a
compact exhaustion, that is, a sequence Kn (for n ∈N) of compact subsets of M
such that for all n ∈N, Kn ⊆int(Kn+1), and M = S
n∈N Kn [Lee11, Prop. 4.76].
As a consequence, every manifold M is paracompact, meaning every open cover U
of M admits locally finite refinement U′, that is, a collection of open subsets of
elements of U which still covers M, but for which each point of M lies in at most
finitely many elements of U′ [Lee11, Thm. 4.77]. We wish to realize both of these
facts in a Borel way. To this end, we first need a lemma which says that we can
tell whether an open subset of a manifold contains the closure of another.
Lemma 3.28. The relation “M(V,d) and M(W,e) are open submanifolds of M(U,c)
and the closure of M(V,d) is contained in M(W,e)”, for (U, c), (V, d), and (W, e) in
M(G, X), is Borel on M(G, X).
To prove Lemma 3.28, we start by showing the corresponding fact for open
subsets of our fixed model space X, which we will then transfer to the manifold
setting locally via the charts. We will make repeated use the elementary fact that

We can now prove Lemma 3.28.
Proof of Lemma 3.28. Suppose (U, c), (V, d), and (W, e) are given so that the lat-

condition. In particular, d = c|V and e = c|W. Then, the closure of the submanifold
represented by (V, d) is contained in that represented by (W, e) if and only if for
all i ∈N, clUi(Vi) = Vi ∩Ui ⊆Wi, which is a Borel condition by Lemma 3.30.

3.3.2. Connectedness. In what follows it will be convenient, and to some degree
necessary, to confine our attention to model spaces X which are not only locally
compact, but locally path connected, simply connected, or geodesically convex as
well; model space properties along these lines must, after all, make an eventual
appearance in any theory of classical manifolds per se.
In the present context,
these are mild constraints: any smooth Riemannian manifold, and in fact any
second countable locally compact metric space of bounded curvature (i.e., having
a complete CAT(κ) metric), and, in particular, any Sn, Rn, or Hn (n ≥2) will
satisfy all of them. Here we follow the usual convention of saying for any property
P that a space X is locally P if every point in X possess a basis of neighborhoods
satisfying P.
Definition 3.38. Let (X, d) be a metric space. A geodesic from x to y in X is a
continuous map γ : [0, ℓ] →X such that γ(0) = x, γ(ℓ) = y, and d(γ(s), γ(t)) = t−s
for all s ≤t in [0, ℓ]; we will at times conflate a geodesic with its image. (X, d) is a
geodesic space if any two points in X are joined by a geodesic, and a subset Y of X
is geodesically convex if any two points in Y are joined by a geodesic whose image
falls in Y .
Lemma 3.39. For any Borel pseudogroup G on a locally simply connected, locally
compact Polish space X, there exists a Borel reparametrization r : M(G, X) →
M(G, X) such that for all (U, c) in M(G, X),
• each Vi (i ∈N) is simply connected, where r(U, c) = (V, d).
If in addition X is locally geodesically convex and G is a subpseudogroup of
Isom, then we may further stipulate that each Vi,j is simply connected, and in fact
geodesically convex, for all (i, j) ∈N2.
Proof. Fix a countable basis W = (Wi)i∈N of open, simply connected subsets of
X, as well as a canonical enumeration e of N2. For any (U, c) in M(G, X), this
basis induces maximal W-decompositions Ui,i = S

of e, we then have a canonical enumeration (Vk)k∈N of {W i

determining the “left side” coordinates of r(U, c) as follows: let Vk,k = Vk, and for
k, ℓenumerating W i

The point in the lemma’s concluding claim is that, in the context of a geodesic-

One works in this case from a basis W of open, geodesically convex balls, and

the bth element of (Ui)i∈N which contains qa. One then adds to Ji(n) the least index
of a W(n) ∈W containing qa such that
(1) W(n) ⊆Ui(n)

(2) W(n) ⊆Ui(n),i(m) or
(3) W(n) ∩φi(m),i(n)[W(m)] = ∅.
The point is that condition (1) at previous stages m ensures that one of the as-
sociated conditions (2) or (3) is satisfiable at stage n. The fact that any finite
combination of satisfiable requirements is satisfiable then ensures that the con-
struction can always continue; the fact that only finitely many “bits” of data are
needed to meet them ensures that the construction is Borel (this time we leave the
details to the reader). Our bookkeeping ensures that S

j = Ui for all i. The
reward for our efforts is that for all k and ℓ, either
• Vk,ℓ= ∅, or
• Vk,ℓ= Vk ∩φ−1

i,i′[Vℓ], or
• Vℓ,k = Vℓ∩φ−1

i′,i[Vk].
In other words, if nonempty, then either Vk,ℓor Vℓ,k is an intersection of geodesically
convex sets, and thus convex, and thus its counterpart is as well.

Given a Borel pseudogroup G on X, we denote by C(G, X) the collection of all
(U, c) ∈M(G, X) such that M(U,c) is connected.
The next lemma then figures
essentially as a corollary of the last one.
Lemma 3.40. For any Borel pseudogroup G on a locally path connected, locally
compact Polish space X, the parameter space C(G, X) ⊆M(G, X) of all connected
(G, X)-manifolds is a standard Borel space.
Proof. Define a reparametrization map r just as in Lemma 3.39, but with respect
to a basis of path-connected open sets; by the same argument as before, r is
Borel. A (U, c) in the image of the map r : M(G, X) →M(G, X) parametrizes
a connected manifold if and only if for any i, j ∈N there exists a finite sequence
i = i0, . . . , iℓ, . . . , in = j such that Uiℓ,iℓ+1 ̸= ∅for all ℓ< n (this observation
plays an identical role in [HK00, p. 132]). This condition is evidently Borel, hence
C(G, X) ⊆M(G, X) is the preimage of a Borel set by the Borel function r.
□
Lemma 3.41. Let X and G be as in Lemma 3.40. There is a Borel function which
computes the connected components of any (G, X)-manifold.
Proof. Fix a dense subset Q = {qi | i ∈N} of X and a standard enumeration
e of N3 and a basis W = {Wi | i ∈N}, consisting of path-connected open sets,
for the topology of X.
For each i, j ∈N, we describe a Borel function fi,j :
M(G, X) →M(G, X) recording the component, as an open submanifold, of M(U,c)
associated to the image of qi in the chart Uj. Taken together, these determine an
f : M(G, X) →M(G, X)N×N which is as desired.
fi,j(U, c) is defined as follows: if qi ̸∈Uj, then fi,j(U, c) is the element of M(G, X)
consisting of empty charts. If on the other hand qi ∈Uj, then fix Wk0 least in the
W-enumeration with qi ∈Wk0 ⊆Uj and let i0 = i and j0 = j. Select then the
e-least yet-unselected (i′, j′, k′) with
(1) qi′ ∈Wk′ ⊆Uj′, and
(2) φjs,j′[Wks] ∩Wk′ ̸= ∅for some s < t,
if such exist (if they do not, the process terminates). Let it = i′, jt = j′, and
kt = k′, and repeat.
For each m ∈N, let Vm by the (U, c)-saturation (in the
sense of Lemma 3.21) of ∪{Wks | js = m}. Put fi,j(U, c) = (V, c|V), the open
submanifold corresponding to the Vm’s; this mapping is evidently Borel. It remains
to show that N = M(V,c|V) is a connected component of M(U,c).
Since M(U,c) is locally path-connected, it will suffice to show that N contains
the path-component of any of its elements; therefore let x denote the image of qi0
in N, and let γ denote a path in M(U,c) from x to any other point y. We wish to
show that y ∈N as well — but this is almost immediate: recalling that π(U,c) is
the quotient map onto M(U,c) as in (1), γ will be covered by the π(U,c)-images of
finitely many W ∈W which are each contained in some Uj ∈U, and each of these
W will arise in the formation of N described above, by the connectedness of γ.
□
3.3.3. Metrics and completeness. Lastly, we turn our attention to metric struc-
tures. Here we append to our standing assumptions that all model spaces X are
all equipped with a compatible complete metric dX; any (Isom, X)-manifold M(U,c)
is then naturally endowed with the quotient pseudometric d(U,c) defined as follows.
For any Ui ∈U and x ∈Ui, write ⌊x, i⌋for the π(U,c)-image in M(U,c) of x, and let
(4)
d(U,c)(⌊x, i⌋, ⌊y, j⌋) = inf{dX(x0, y0) + · · · + dX(xm, ym) | P((xℓ), (yℓ))},

J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
where P((xℓ), (yℓ)) is the conjunction of the following properties:
i. the sequences (xℓ) and (yℓ) are of the same finite length m + 1;
ii. xℓand yℓboth fall in some common Uk(ℓ) for each ℓ≤m;
iii. ⌊yℓ, k(ℓ)⌋= ⌊xℓ+1, k(ℓ+ 1)⌋for all ℓ< m; and
iv. ⌊x0, k(0)⌋= ⌊x, i⌋and ⌊ym, k(m)⌋= ⌊y, j⌋.
Here we should beware of subtleties: we cannot even take for granted that d(U,c)
defines a metric, for example, nor, if it does, that it defines one agreeing with the
charts’ metrics. Under suitable assumptions, on the other hand, π(U,c) is a local
isometry, as the following lemma will show.

M(Isom, X) that

(5)

n-space, we may let ε equal infinity, or, equivalently, ignore it altogether.
Lemma 3.43. Suppose that the geodesic space X admits an ε ∈(0, ∞] such that
any x and y in X of distance less than ε are connected by a unique geodesic, and
that the image of this geodesic varies continuously with the choice of its endpoints
in open neighborhoods about x and y. Then for all δ < ε,
{U ∈O(X) | diam(U) ≤δ and U is geodesically convex}
is a closed subset of O(X). In particular, if G is a Borel subpseudogroup of Isom,
then M∗,ε(G, X) is a Borel subspace of M(G, X) intersecting each of its (G, X)-
equivalence classes, and for which any (G, X)-equivalence of parameters is an isom-

Proof. Suppose a W ∈O(X) of diameter at most δ is not geodesically convex, so
that there exists an x, y ∈W and z ∈γ(x, y)\W. Fix compact neighborhoods
Kx, Ky ⊆W of x, y, respectively (our standing assumption that X is locally com-
pact remains in force), and an open V ⊆S

diameter-≤δ element of the open neighborhood
{U ∈O(X) | U ⊇Kx ∪Ky} ∩{U ∈O(X) | U ̸⊇V }
of W is convex, and this completes the proof of our first assertion. Our second
assertion is then immediate from the preceding lemma and the following remark.
□
Remark 3.44. Speaking generally, when constraints on parameters (U, c) are de-
sired, one may impose them in either of two obvious ways: via reparametrization, as
in Lemmas 3.37 and 3.39, or via restriction, as in M∗(G, X) above and the Lemma
3.43 underpinning it. While the latter is, arguably, more conceptually natural, the
former may be the only Borel approach available. A case in point is the condition
simply connected: as noted in Section 3.2, by [Bec92] this condition is, in general,
very far from Borel, and it was this fact which motivated the approach of Lemma
3.39. Note also that it is, in essence, the construction of Lemma 3.39 which en-
sures for us that each (G, X)-equivalence class of M(G, X) intersects the latter’s
restriction to M∗,ε(G, X).
Of importance in Section 5 and beyond will be classes of complete hyperbolic
manifolds. The relevant lemma is the following.
Lemma 3.45. Let X and ε be as in Lemma 3.43, with G a Borel subpseudogroup
of Isom. The set
{(U, c) ∈M∗,ε(G, X) | d(U,c) is complete}
is then a Borel subset of M∗,ε(G, X).

J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Proof. The contours of our approach are by now routine: we will show that the class
of (parameters for) complete (G, X)-manifolds is Borel by showing that it is both
coanalytic and analytic. Most intuitive of these two characterizations is the first:
M(U,c) is complete if and only if for every d(U,c)-Cauchy sequence ⃗x = (xi)i∈N in
M(U,c), there exists an x ∈M(U,c) such that ⃗x converges to x. Since the convergence
condition may be replaced by the existence of an n ∈N and precompact V ⊆M(U,c)
such that xi ∈V for all i > n, this statement is Π1
1 in form. Somewhat more
formally, fix a countable basis B for X consisting of precompact open sets, as
well as a countable dense Q ⊆X; the complete manifolds then correspond to the
complement of the projection onto the first coordinate of the collection
{((U, c), ⃗x, k) ∈M∗,ε(G, X)×XN×NN | ⃗x is Cauchy, but non-convergent, in M(U,c)},
where the text unpacks as follows:
i. for all i ∈N, xi ∈Uk(i) for some k(i) ∈N;
ii. for all δ ∈Q+ there exists an n(δ) ∈N such that
d(U,c)(⌊xi, k(i)⌋, ⌊xj, k(j)⌋) < δ
for all i, j ≥n(δ);
iii. there does not exist an m ∈N and V ∈B with V ⊆Um and an n ∈N such
that φk(i),m(xi) ∈V for all i > n.
Here only possibly the condition d(U,c)(⌊xi, k(i)⌋, ⌊xj, k(j)⌋) < δ isn’t evidently
Borel. Note, though, that it holds if and only if this inequality holds for a sum as in
equation (4) whose linking elements are all drawn from Q. Since the quantification
is over the countable collection of finite sequences in Q, our claim follows.
For a Σ1
1 characterization, multiple approaches, drawing on the multiple equiva-
lent characterizations of completeness in this context (see [BBI01, Thm. 2.5.28] or
[Thu97, Prop. 3.4.15]), are possible; the idea of ours is the following: M(U,c) is com-
plete if there exists a sequence of compact Lj ⊆M(U,c) such that S
j∈N Lj = M(U,c)
and d(U,c)(x, y) ≥1 whenever x ∈Lj and y ∈M(U,c)\Lj+1 for some j ∈N. Since
we may fix a countable sequence (Ki)i∈N of compact sets which are closures of basic
open sets in X and (much as for Lemma 3.31), whenever sequences as described
exist, build up such Lj from finite unions of π(U,c)[Ki], this is a Σ1
1 condition: it
essentially asks if there exist functions N →N (selecting the Ki) and g : N →N
(the “cutoff” function, so that Lj = S
i≤g(j) π(U,c)[Kf(i)]) satisfying the conditions
described above. To see that the distance condition on the Lj is Borel, note that it
may be checked on a countable dense set, and since the other conditions are even
more obviously Borel, this concludes the proof.
□
4. The homeomorphism and diffeomorphism problems for surfaces
In this section, we will determine the exact Borel complexity of the classification
problem for surfaces, i.e., connected topological 2-manifolds without boundary, up
to homeomorphism. Recall that C(Top, R2) denotes the Borel parameter space of
all surfaces and ∼=Top the homeomorphism relation on it. We will show:
Theorem 4.1. The homeomorphism relation ∼=Top on C(Top, R2) is complete for
countable structures.
As a consequence, we will derive (as Corollary 4.30) that the diffeomorphism re-
lation ∼=C∞on the space of all smooth surfaces C(C∞, R2) also has the same level

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
39
of Borel complexity. Taken together, these two results prove Theorem E from our
introduction.
Proving Theorem 4.1 entails showing both an upper bound — there is some
class of countable first-order structures for which the homeomorphism relation on
surfaces reduces to the associated isomorphism relation — and a lower bound — any
such isomorphism relation reduces to homeomorphism of surfaces. Consequently,
this classification problem has precisely the same degree of complexity as that of the
isomorphism relation on all countable groups, graphs, or linear orders (see [FS89]).
Recall from Corollary 3.24 that the classification of compact surfaces reduces to
=N, so any additional complexity arising in the broader collection of all surfaces
must lie among the noncompact ones.
Our upper bound for the complexity of this classification problem will be based
on the following theorem, which says that surfaces are classified by their genus, ori-
entability, and spaces of ends, generalizing the well-known classification for compact
surfaces. This result traces to Kerékjártó [vK23], but Richards [Ric63] is generally
credited with its first complete modern proof.
Theorem 4.2 (Kerékjártó–Richards). Surfaces X and Y are homeomorphic if and
only if they have the same genus and orientability class, and the triples of spaces
(e(X), e′(X), e′′(X)) and (e(Y ), e′(Y ), e′′(Y )) are equivalent.
The triples (e(X), e′(X), e′′(X)) consist of three nested spaces, the space of ends
e(X) of X, and the subspaces consisting of the nonplanar and nonorientable ends,
e′(X) and e′′(X), respectively. Each is a compact totally disconnected Polish space.
Two such triples are equivalent if there is a homeomorphism between the first co-
ordinate spaces which restricts to a homeomorphism on each of the two subspaces.
Much like all proofs of the classification theorem for compact surfaces ([Moi77],
[FW99], [Lee11], [GX13], among others), the proof of Theorem 4.2 begins by assum-
ing that the surfaces in question have been triangulated, that is, are homeomorphic
to polyhedra constructed from simplicial complexes, itself a nontrivial fact [Rad25].
Proofs of the triangulation theorem, in turn, often rely on the Jordan–Schoenflies
theorem [Sch06]. To give an account of this classification result in our framework,
we must therefore show that the outputs of both the Jordan–Schoenflies and tri-
angulation theorems can be computed in a Borel way. We will do so by adapting
the proofs of these theorems given by Thomassen in [Tho92] (see also [MT01] and
[GX13]) to the Borel setting.
The observation that surfaces admit classification by countable algebraic invari-
ants is not itself new. For example, Goldman [Gol71] gave such a classification
using cohomological invariants based directly on Richards’s proof of Theorem 4.2
(of course, Richards’s result may itself be understood in these terms, by Stone du-
ality; cf. Lemma 4.29 below). What is novel in our account here is that we are
casting such a classification in the terminology of invariant descriptive set theory
and verifying its Borel content.
The lower bound for Theorem 4.1 was previously observed by Kulikov in [Kul15],
drawing on Clinton Conley’s answer [Con] to a question on MathOverflow. It is
based on the fact, due to Carmelo and Gao [CG01], that the homeomorphism
relation ∼= on the space K({0, 1}N) of closed subsets of the Cantor space {0, 1}N is
complete for countable structures. For reference, we sketch the argument here.

40
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Theorem 4.3. The relation ∼= on K({0, 1}N) Borel reduces to ∼=Top on C(Top, R2),
and to ∼=C∞on C(C∞, R2).
Proof. Let C be the usual middle-thirds Cantor set lying on the real line, viewed as a
subset of the sphere S = R2∪{∞}. We identify K({0, 1}N) with K(C). Consider the
map f : K(C) →O(S) given by complementation: f(K) = S\K. This map is easily
seen to be Borel. Any set S \ K in the image of this map is a planar surface whose
space of ends (see below) can be identified homeomorphically with K. That is,
S \K corresponds to the triple (K, ∅, ∅) in the notation of Theorem 4.2, and so we
have that K ∼= K′ if and only f(K) ∼= f(K′), for any K, K′ ∈K(C). We leave it to
the reader to convince themselves, using the definitions in Section 3, that O(S) can
be identified in a Borel way with a subset of C(Top, R2), through which f becomes a
Borel reduction. The smooth case follows using the same reduction, together with
the fact that surfaces possess unique smooth structures up to diffeomorphism as
cited in Example 3.18.
□
Lastly, we note the following corollary of Theorem 4.3 and the fact any equiva-
lence relation which is complete for countable structures is Σ1
1-complete11 [FS89]:
Corollary 4.4. The homeomorphism and diffeomorphism relations on surfaces are
Σ1
1-complete. In particular, they are not Borel equivalence relations.
□
4.1. A Borel form of the Jordan–Schoenflies Theorem. We begin by stating
the Jordan–Schoenflies theorem in the form most convenient for our purposes.
Theorem 4.5 (Jordan–Schoenflies). If γ : S1 →R2 is a continuous embedding,
then there is a homeomorphism h : R2 →R2 such that h|S1 = γ.
This result is a strengthening of the Jordan curve theorem, which says that if C
is any simple closed curve in R2, then R2\C has exactly two connected components;
one bounded, the interior of C, and one unbounded, the exterior of C.
To give a Borel version of Theorem 4.5, we will first need to describe the relevant
Polish spaces of maps and some aspects of their Borel structure. In what follows,
suppose that X and Y are locally compact Polish spaces. Let C(X, Y ) be the space
of all continuous functions X →Y endowed with the compact-open topology, which
is generated by subbasic open sets
V (K, U) = {f ∈C(X, Y ) | f[K] ⊆U},
where K and U range over the compact and open subsets of X and Y , respectively.
The following facts are standard (see [Eng89]):
• C(X, Y ) forms a Polish space.
• If Z is a locally compact Polish space, then the composition map C(Y, Z)×
C(X, Y ) →C(X, Z) : (f, g) 7→f ◦g is continuous.
• The evaluation map e : C(X, Y ) × X →Y : (f, x) 7→f(x) is continuous.
We will denote by Emb(X, Y ) the subset of C(X, Y ) consisting of all injective
continuous functions X →Y . In our cases of interest, X will be compact, so these
are exactly the topological embeddings of X into Y .
11An analytic subset C of a standard Borel Y is Σ1
1-complete if for any analytic subset A of a
standard Borel X, there is a Borel f : X →Y such that x ∈A if and only if f(x) ∈C. We do
not mean that any analytic equivalence relation Borel reduces to it (i.e., is a complete analytic
equivalence relation); that this is not the case for ∼
=Top and ∼
=C∞follows from our Theorem 4.1.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
41
Lemma 4.6. If X is compact, Z any metrizable space, and U ⊆X × Z is open,
then W = {z ∈Z | ∀x ∈X((x, z) ∈U)} is open in Z.
Proof. Let (zn)n∈N be a sequence in Z \W converging to some z ∈Z. For each n ∈
N, there is an xn ∈X such that (xn, zn) /∈U. Passing to a convergent subsequence,
we may assume that xn converges to x, so (xn, zn) converges to (x, z) /∈U, since U
is open. Thus, z /∈W.
□
The following lemma and its proof are adapted from [nLa] (see also [Kul17]).
Lemma 4.7. If X is compact, then Emb(X, Y ) is a Gδ subset of C(X, Y ), and
thus itself a Polish space.
Proof. Fix compatible complete metrics dX and dY on X and Y , respectively. For
each m, n ∈N, let
Um,n =

f ∈C(X, Y ) | ∀x, y ∈X

Lemma 4.8. Let C be the set of all pairs ((xn)n∈N, (yn)n∈N) of sequences for
which {xn | n ∈N} is dense in X and the assignment xn 7→yn defines a uniformly
continuous function. Then:
1. C is a Borel subset of XN × Y N.

Proof. This argument is similar to that of Lemma 3.12. Fix a compatible com-

{((xn)n∈N, (yn)n∈N, f) ∈XN × Y N × C(X, Y ) | ∀n ∈N(f(xn) = yn)},
which is Borel, since the evaluation map C(X, Y ) × X →Y is continuous.

Recall that K(X) is the set of compact subsets of X with the Vietoris topology
and F(X) is the space of closed subsets of X with the Fell topology.
Lemma 4.9. The following maps are Borel:
1. Direct image: C(X, Y ) × K(X) →K(Y ) : (f, K) 7→f[K].
2. Inverse image: C(X, Y ) × F(Y ) →F(X) : (f, E) 7→f−1[E].
Proof. It is easy to show that the map in (1) is separately continuous in each variable
(see [Kec95, Exer. 4.29(vi)] for one case), and that the map in (2) is continuous in
the first variable and Borel in the second. Thus, both maps are Borel measurable
on their domains by [AB06, Lem. 4.51].

By an arc we mean a continuous embedding γ of [0, 1] into R2, that is, an
element of Emb([0, 1], R2). The endpoints of γ are γ(0) and γ(1). A closed curve
is a continuous embedding of S1 into R2, an element of Emb(S1, R2).
We will
frequently blur the distinction between an arc or closed curve and its image im(γ);
by Lemma 4.9, the passage from the former to the latter is Borel. Lemma 4.10
allows us to compute intersection points between arcs and curves in a Borel way.
A polygonal arc (or closed curve, respectively) is an arc (closed curve) consisting
of finitely many straight line segments. By a rational point in R2, we mean a point
both of whose coordinates are rational. A rational arc is a polygonal arc all of whose
endpoints and break points are rational; a rational closed curve is defined similarly.
Rational polygonal arcs and curves have canonical parametrizations, and there are
only countably many, so we may fix an enumeration of them going forwards.
A key definition used in Thomassen’s proof of Theorem 4.5 is as follows: given
a closed subset C of R2 and Ωa nonempty open connected region in R2 \ C, a
point p ∈C is accessible from Ωif for some (equivalently, all) q ∈Ω, there is a
polygonal arc from q to p whose only intersection with C is p. We can make the
obvious modification to say that p is rationally accessible from Ω; note that p itself
need not be a rational point. The set of accessible points is dense in C [Tho92, p.
122]. However, this definition is apparently weaker than what is needed to carry out
the rest of Thomassen’s argument.12 Namely, we need points p ∈C which remain
accessible to any of the new regions in Ωcreated by adding a polygonal arc to p.
Instead, we say that a point p ∈C is (rationally) accessible* from Ωif there is a
positive angle’s worth of segments of (rational) polygonal arcs connecting p to some
12This was pointed out by Robin Chapman in a comment to an answer [Pan] on MathOverflow.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
43
(rational) q ∈Ω. Note that this is equivalent to having two distinct nonparallel
(rational) segments incident at p.
Lemma 4.11. Let C be a closed subset R2 and Ωa nonempty region in R2 \ C.
1. If Ωis a open region in R2 and p, q are rational points in Ω, then there is
a rational polygonal arc from p to q.
2. The set of rationally accessible points in C is dense in C.
3. The set of rationally accessible* points in C is dense in C.
Proof. The proofs of (1) and (2) are identical to the arguments for (not necessarily
rational) polygonal arcs and accessible points in [Tho92].
The proof of (3) comes from Jan Kyncl’s answer [Kyn] to a question asked by
the second author on MathOverflow. It suffices to show that rationally accessible*
points can be found arbitrarily close to rationally accessible points in C. Let p ∈C
be rationally accessible and P the straight line segment of a rational polygonal arc
ending at p. Let ϵ > 0 and choose some rational q on P such that d(p, q) < ϵ. Let
r ∈C be the nearest point to q. Then, the open disk D centered at q having radius
d(q, r) must be disjoint from C and lie entirely in Ω, and so r can be reached from
a positive angle’s worth of segments within D, at least two of which can be made
as rational segments. Thus, r is rationally accessible*.
□
Given γ ∈Emb(S1, R2), we denote by int(γ) and ext(γ) the bounded and un-
bounded components of R2 \ im(γ), respectively, and by int(γ) and ext(γ) their
closures.
Lemma 4.12. The following maps are Borel:
1. im : Emb(S1, R2) →F(R2).
2. int : Emb(S1, R2) →O(R2).
3. ext : Emb(S1, R2) →O(R2).
Proof. As in the proof of Lemma 4.9, the map γ 7→im(γ) = γ[S1] is continuous. To
then obtain int(γ) and ext(γ), we can take the complement of im(γ), a continuous
operation, then compute its connected components by taking unions of overlapping
basic open sets (a simplified version of Lemma 3.41). We can then detect which is
bounded and which is unbounded in a Borel way, using an exhaustion of R2.
□
We can now state our Borel form of Theorem 4.5.
Theorem 4.13. There is a Borel map H : Emb(S1, R2) →Homeo(R2) such that
H(γ)|S1 = γ
for all γ ∈Emb(S1, R2).
The proof of Theorem 4.13 largely consists of translating Thomassen’s proof of
Theorem 4.5 from [Tho92] into a Borel construction. The idea of said proof is, given
an embedding γ : S →R2, where we have replaced S1 with the unit square S, we
construct a “mesh” of finer and finer finite graphs Γn and Γ′
n, and isomorphisms
gn : Γn →Γ′
n, where Γn contains S and lies inside int(S), Γ′
n contains im(γ) and
lies inside int(γ), and gn agrees with γ on S. In the limit, we get a homeomorphism
from int(γ) to int(S) which extends γ. These graphs, besides the portion of Γ′
n
given by im(γ), consist of rational polygonal arcs. A similar procedure can then be
used to construct a homeomorphism of the exteriors which also agrees with γ on S.

44
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
We will use the following graph-theoretic terminology: A finite graph Γ is 2-
connected if it is connected and for every vertex v, Γ \ {v} remains connected.
Components of R2 \ Γ are called faces, their boundaries are facial cycles in Γ,
the (unique) unbounded face is the outer face, and its boundary in Γ is the outer
cycle. If Γ and Γ′ are 2-connected graphs consisting of closed curves C and C′, and
polygonal arcs in int(C) and int(C′), respectively, an isomorphism g : Γ →Γ′ is a
plane isomorphism if g maps facial cycles in Γ to facial cycles in Γ′ and the outer
cycle of Γ to the outer cycle of Γ′. A subdivision of a graph is obtained by inserting
vertices on edges, in the obvious sense.
Proof of Theorem 4.13. By translating via any fixed self-homeomorphism of R2
which maps S onto S1, we may work with elements of Emb(S, R2), rather than
Emb(S1, R2).
We will describe a procedure which will transform a given γ ∈
Emb(S, R2) into a homeomorphism H(γ) ∈Homeo(R2) which extends γ, and this
procedure will be evidently Borel, given the preceding lemmas.
Let γ ∈Emb(S, R2) be given. Write C = im(γ). We will extend γ to a home-
omorphism from the closed square int(S) onto int(C).
Fix bn (for n ∈N), an
enumeration of all rational points in int(C). We can find an enumeration an (for
n ∈N) of a dense set of points on C which are rationally accessible* from int(C)
as follows: fix q a rational point in int(C) and r a rational point in ext(C). Scan
across all possible rational polygonal arcs from q to r, and let an be the nth point
which occurs as the “first” intersection of two such arcs with C which are incident
at different angles. Let pn (for n ∈N) be an enumeration of all the an’s and bn’s
so that each occurs infinitely often.
Thomassen’s proof in [Tho92] proceeds as follows:13 He define sequences Γn and
Γ′
n of finite 2-connected graphs and plane isomorphisms gn : Γn →Γ′
n such that
for each n ≥1:
• Γn (Γ′
n, respectively) is an extension of a subdivision of Γn−1 (Γ′
n−1);
• gn extends gn−1 and agrees with γ on vertices of Γn which lie on S;
• Γn (Γ′
n, respectively) consists of S (C) together with simple polygonal arcs
in int(S) (int(C));
• Γn \ S (Γ′
n \ C) is connected;
• pn is a vertex of Γ′
n; and
• if pn ∈int(C), then in some sufficiently small square containing pn in its
interior all bounded faces of Γ′
n have diameter < 1/n, and all bounded faces
in Γn have diameter ≤1/2n.
It is clear that, since we have chosen the pn’s to be rational or rationally accessible*
points, the graphs Γn and Γ′
n can be chosen to consist of rational polygonal arcs,
together with S and C, respectively. There are only countably many such graphs,
so may fix an enumeration of them and simply search for ones that work at each
stage of the construction; in particular, they can be found in a Borel way. As we go,
we build enumerations (vm)m∈N of the vertices of the Γn’s and (wm)m∈N, vertices
of the Γ′
n’s, so that if vm is a vertex of Γn, then gn(vm) = wm. By construction the
vm’s are dense in int(S), the wm’s are dense in int(C), and the mapping vm 7→wm
agrees with γ on those vm’s which lie on S.
Thomassen then shows that this
mapping extends uniquely to a homeomorphism int(S) →int(C) extending γ.
13We are technically describing the inverse of the map constructed in [Tho92].

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
45
A similar argument can be used to define dense sequences sm and tm (for m ∈N)
in ext(S) and ext(C), respectively, such that the mapping sm 7→tm extends
uniquely to a homeomorphism ext(S) →ext(C) which also agrees with γ on S.
Putting these sequences together, and applying Lemma 4.8, we obtain a homeo-
morphism R2 →R2 which extends γ in a Borel way.
□
While we will not make use of this here, we note that, by [Kec95, Thm. 8.38],
Theorem 4.13 implies there is a dense Gδ subset of Emb(S1, R2) on which embed-
dings can be extended to homeomorphisms in Homeo(R2) continuously. It would
be interesting to know, from a purely topological standpoint, whether the exten-
sion map H : Emb(S1, R2) →Homeo(R2) in Theorem 4.13 can be made globally
continuous. Doing so would seem to require carrying out Thomassen’s construction
in a highly uniform way.
4.2. A Borel form of the triangulation theorem. We first review some basic
facts about simplicial complexes, largely following the presentation in [AS60], and
consider how to parametrize them by elements of a standard Borel space. For us
an (abstract) simplicial complex is a set V of vertices together with a family K of
finite subsets of V , called simplices, such that:
• every vertex is in at least one and at most finitely many simplices, and
• every subset of a simplex is a simplex.
The dimension of a simplex is one less than its size, and the dimension of a sim-
plicial complex is the supremum of the dimensions of its simplices. We term n-
dimensional simplices n-simplices and identify vertices with 0-simplices and tend
accordingly to refer to the simplicial complex simply as K. Below, we deal exclu-
sively with countable 2-dimensional simplicial complexes.
Given a simplicial complex K, its geometric realization is the space Kg consisting
of all real-valued functions λ on K such that:
• λ(x) ≥0 for all x ∈K,
• the elements x for which λ(x) > 0 form a simplex, and
• P
x∈K λ(x) = 1.
If s is a simplex in K, then we let
sg = {λ ∈Kg | λ(x) = 0 for all x /∈s}.
For example, if s is a 2-simplex, say s = {x1, x2, x3}, then elements of sg can be
identified with triples (λ1, λ2, λ3) of real numbers such that λi ≥0 and λ1+λ2+λ3 =
1; thus sg can be represented as a closed triangle in R3, from which it inherits a
topology. As s ranges over all simplices in K, the sg’s cover Kg, and we topologize
the latter by declaring a subset to be closed if its intersection with each sg is closed.
An elementary subdivision of a 2-dimensional simplicial complex K is formed as
follows: if a = {x1, x2} is a 1-simplex in K which belongs to the 2-simplex A =
{x1, x2, x3}, we introduce a new vertex x and replace a and A with three 1-simplices
{x1, x}, {x2, x}, and {x3, x}, and two 2-simplices {x1, x3, x} and {x2, x3, x}, re-
spectively.
If a happened to belong to two 2-simplices A and B, we carry out
this construction on both A and B, simultaneously. A simple subdivision of K re-
sults by applying elementary subdivisions to (possibly infinitely) many 1-simplices
a simultaneously, provided no two of them belong to the same 2-simplex. K′ is a
subdivision of K if it is obtained from K by a finite sequence of simple subdivisions.
More generally, K′ is equivalent to K if there is a finite sequence K = K1, K2, . . . ,

46
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Kn = K′ where each Ki is a subdivision of Ki+1 or vice versa. It is clear that if K
and K′ are equivalent, then Kg and K′
g are homeomorphic.
We define a space S of countable 2-dimensional simplicial complexes as follows:
S consists of all triples S = (S0, S1, S2) ∈{0, 1}N × {0, 1}N2 × {0, 1}N3 such that:
• S1(i, j) = S1(j, i) for all i, j ∈N;
• S2(i, j, k) = S2(i, k, j) = S2(j, i, k) = S2(j, k, i) = S2(k, i, j) = S2(k, j, i) for
all i, j, k ∈N;
• S2(i, j, k) = 1 implies S1(i, j) = S1(i, k) = S1(j, k) = 1 for all i, j, k ∈N;
• S1(i, j) = 1 implies S0(i) = S0(j) = 1 for all i, j ∈N; and
• for all i ∈N, there are at most finitely many j such that S2(i, j) = 1.
Clearly, S is a Borel subset of {0, 1}N × {0, 1}N2 × {0, 1}N3.
An element S =
(S0, S1, S2) of S can be identified with the abstract simplicial complex given by
K = supp(S0) = {n : S0(n) = 1}, where {i, j} is a 1-simplex if and only if
S1(i, j) = 1, and {i, j, k} is a 2-simplex if and only if S2(i, j, k) = 1. We use this
representation of the space of simplicial complexes, rather than the more familiar
way of treating spaces of countable structures described in Section 2.2, since the
finite complexes are naturally included in this representation as those for which
supp(S0) is finite. Given S ∈S, we write Sg for its geometric realization, as above.
There is a natural notion of isomorphism for complexes which can be represented
by the following equivalence relation on S: S = (S0, S1, S2) ∼= S′ = (S′
0, S′
1, S′
2) if
there is a bijection f : N →N such that for all i, j, k ∈N:
S0(i) = S′
0(f(i))
S1(i, j) = S′
1(f(i), f(j))
S2(i, j, k) = S′
2(f(i), f(j), f(k)).
Clearly, ∼= is an analytic equivalence relation, induced by an action of S∞on S.
Similarly, we can say that S and S′ in S are combinatorially equivalent, written
S ≡S′, if S and S′ have subdivisions which are isomorphic; this refinement of ∼= is
likewise an analytic equivalence relation on S.
It is fairly straightforward to characterize those 2-dimensional simplicial com-
plexes K for which Kg is a surface; we follow [AS60, Thm. 22E] in terming such K
polyhedra:
Theorem 4.14. A 2-dimensional simplicial complex K is a polyhedron if and only
if the following conditions hold:
i. every 1-simplex is in exactly two 2-simplices,
ii. the 1-simplices and 2-simplices which contain a given vertex x can be written
as a1, . . . , am and A1, . . . , Am, respectively, for m ≥3, so that a1 = A1∩Am
and ai = Ai ∩Ai−1 for 2 ≤i ≤m, and
iii. K is connected (i.e., it cannot be written as a union of two disjoint sub-
complexes).
When translated to S, conditions (i) and (ii) above are evidently Borel. For
(iii), connectivity can be rephrased in terms of having a finite path (of 1-simplices)
between any two vertices, much like for graphs, which is also a Borel condition.
Thus, we have a Borel subset Spoly of S consisting of all polyhedra.
The relevant form of the triangulation theorem for us is that every surface is
homeomorphic to the geometric realization of a polyhedron.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
47
Theorem 4.15 (Triangulation Theorem). For any surface M, there is a simplicial
complex whose geometric realization is homeomorphic to M.
Our Borel14 form of this theorem is then:
Theorem 4.16. There is a Borel map T : C(Top, R2) →Spoly such that for each
(U, c) ∈C(Top, R2), T(U, c) = K(U,c) is a simplicial complex whose geometric real-
ization is homeomorphic to M(U,c).
Our proof is, again, based on that of Thomassen [Tho92], but we more closely
follow the presentation in [GX13].
Note that the proofs in the aforementioned
references are all for compact surfaces, but we will use Lemma 3.37 to adapt them
to the general case. For a self-contained, but less obviously constructive, proof of
the triangulation theorem for arbitrary surfaces, see [AS60].
Proof of Theorem 4.16. Let (U, c) ∈C(Top, R2) be given and write M for M(U,c).
By Lemma 3.37, we may reparametrize, in a Borel way, so that (U, c) has the
following properties:
• (U, c) is locally finite, i.e., for all i ∈N, there are at most finitely many
j ∈N for which Ui,j ̸= ∅;
• for each i ∈N, Ui is a nonempty open disk in R2;
• and each Ui contains a pair of nested rectangles Q1
i and Q2

corners, which we view as rational polygonal curves, which are nested,

In the notation of Lemma 3.37, B is a basis consisting of open disks in R2 and C a

We will describe a procedure for obtaining a simplicial complex K ∈S which will
be Borel in the parameter (U, c). In the course of this construction, we will modify
the rectangles Q1

a way which is Borel at each step. The images of the resulting Q1

notational simplicity, we will use the same notation for Q1

segments, there are only finitely many very bad ones [GX13, Lem. E.2].

0 be the simplicial complex formed from points q0, q1, . . . , qn0, with 1-simplices
given by very bad segments connecting q1, . . . , qn0, together with {q0, qi} for each
1 ≤i ≤n0, and 2-simplices given by {q0, qi, qj} for 1 ≤i, j ≤n0 whenever qi and
qj are connected by a very bad segment. Next, we let qn0+1 ∈int(Q2
0) be the least
rational point in a different component than q0. We find the intersection points
qn0+2, . . . , qn1 of the cycle which bounds the component of qn0+1, subdivide and
add 1- and 2-simplices as above, and add these to S′
0 to get S′
1. We continue this
construction until we have met each of the finitely many components of int(Q2
0)
bounded by very bad segments.
Next, we repeat the same with Q2
1, however,
if any of the rational points we wish to add in the construction are already the
image, under some φi1 of a rational point which we have already added, we do not
need to add it; similar comments apply to the 1- and 2-simplices. We continue
this construction across all of the Q2
k’s, building an increasing sequence of finite
simplicial complexes of rational points, S′
k. Let S′ be their union. Finally, we obtain
S ∈S by simply forgetting the particular rational points and just remembering the
indices in N we have assigned them along the way, completing the construction.
□

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
49
Given a polyhedron K, it is straightforward to show that it can be exhausted by
a sequence of a finite subcomplexes, which when represented in S can be determined
in a Borel way. However, in our applications, we will need slightly more, namely,
to exhaust K by a sequence of finite subcomplexes which are themselves polyhedra.
More explicitly, we call a sequence Pn (for n ∈N) of finite simplicial complexes a
canonical exhaustion16 of K if for all n ∈N,
• each Pn is a polyhedron,
• Pn+1 is a subcomplex of Pn,
• the border simplices of Pn+1 are not in Pn, and
• each simplex in K belongs to some Pn.
Lemma 4.17. There is a Borel function E : Spoly →SN
poly such that for each
K ∈Spoly, E(K) is a canonical exhaustion of a subdivision of K.
Proof. The construction of such an exhaustion given in [AS60, I.4.29] is clearly
Borel, once translated into our setting; readers may consult [ibid.] for details.
□
When combined with Theorem 4.16, Lemma 4.17 can be viewed as a strengthen-
ing of Lemma 3.31 for surfaces, giving not only an exhaustion by compact sets, but
by compact submanifolds (with boundary), realized as polyhedra. In fact, this will
be the main application of Theorem 4.16 in our proof Theorem 4.1. We had initially
hoped for a simpler approach to proving the existence of such a Borel exhaustion
along the lines of the proof of Lemma 3.31, but were not able to overcome technical
issues concerning how the boundaries patch together.
We close this section with a few comments on related results and generalizations.
Clearly, equivalent complexes have homeomorphic geometric realizations. The con-
verse of this statement is known as the Hauptvermutung, and holds for complexes
whose geometric realizations are manifolds in dimensions 2 and 3 [Pap43] [Moi52],
but fails in higher dimensions (see [RCS+96]). Since we can pass from polyhedra to
surfaces by taking geometric realizations (it is routine to show that this construction
is Borel), and back by Theorem 4.16, it follows that homeomorphism of surfaces
and equivalence ≡of 2-dimensional polyhedra have the same Borel complexity. In
particular, if we knew that the latter was classifiable by countable structures, our
proof of Theorem 4.1 would be complete. However, as we do not have a direct proof
of this fact,17 we must continue onwards via Theorem 4.2. We will return to this
point in Corollary 4.31 below.
Finally, we note that, like surfaces, all topological 3-manifolds can be triangu-
lated [Moi77], but again, triangulation fails in higher dimensions [Fre82, Man16b].
The case of smooth manifolds (in any dimension) is simpler; they are always tri-
angulable, and it seems likely one could produce a Borel exhaustion by compact
submanifolds directly using regular sublevel sets of exhaustion functions and Sard’s
Theorem (see [Lee13] or [Mil65]).
4.3. The construction of the space of ends. The abstract notion of an “end”
of a manifold is due to Freudenthal [Fre31] who used it to construct a canonical
compactification which generalizes that for the plane, obtained by adjoining a single
point at ∞(“one end”), and the line, obtained by adjoining points at +∞and −∞
(“two ends”). Standard English-language presentations of this construction can be
16This is slightly weaker than the definition in [AS60], but will suffice.
17This has been recently proved independently by Iannella and Weinstein [IW].

50
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
found in [AS60] or [Ray60]. In this section, we will verify that the construction of
the space of ends of a connected topological manifold, in any dimension, can be
accomplished in a Borel way.
The key elementary fact which ensures the compactness of the space of ends is
the following lemma [Ray60, Lem. 1.1]; for a proof see [BE83, Lem. 9].
Lemma 4.18. If M is a connected manifold and K ⊆M is compact, then M \ K
has only finitely many unbounded connected components.
□
Recall that a sequence Ki (for i ∈N) of compact subsets of a manifold M is a
compact exhaustion if Ki ⊆int(Ki+1) for all i ∈N and M = S
i∈N Ki.
Definition 4.19. Given a connected manifold M and a compact exhaustion Ki
(for i ∈N), an end of M is a sequence ⟨Qi | i ∈N⟩of nonempty open subsets of M
such that for each i ∈N:
i. Qi ⊇Qi+1,
ii. Qi is an unbounded connected component of M \ Ki, and
iii. for any compact subset K ⊆M, there is some j ∈N for which Qj ∩K = ∅.
While the above definition depends on a particular choice of exhaustion, different
exhaustions will produce “equivalent” ends, in the sense that for each end ⟨Qi | i ∈
N⟩with respect to one exhaustion, there is an end ⟨Q′
i | i ∈N⟩with respect to
the other such that for all i ∈N, there is a j ∈N for which Qi ⊆Q′
j, and vice-
versa. It is a consequence of the canonical nature of the end compactification (see
Lemma 4.23 below) that it is independent, up to a homeomorphism which fixes the
underlying manifold, of the particular choice of exhaustion.
An end of a manifold is an infinite branch through a certain finitely-branching
(by Lemma 4.18) tree of finite sequences of open connected sets. We must describe
how to define this tree in a Borel way. As this is a tree of elements in an uncountable
space, some care must be taken in ensuring a Borel target space of trees. Let Tr(N)
denote the set of all finitely-branching trees on N, which inherits a standard Borel
structure when viewed as a subset of {0, 1}N<∞. The idea for the following lemma
comes from Arno Pauly’s answer [Pau] to a question asked by the second author
on MathOverflow.
Lemma 4.20. Let X be a standard Borel space. Then, there is a standard Borel
space Tr(X) of all finitely-branching trees in X such that:
1. There is a Borel map p : Tr(X) →Tr(N) which assigns to each tree in
Tr(X) an isomorphic tree on N. Moreover, this map preserves subtrees.
2. There is a Borel map ℓ: Tr(X) →(X ∪{u})N which assigns to each tree in
Tr(X) an enumeration of its nodes, where u /∈X represents that a node is
undefined.
Proof. Choose some u /∈X and view X ∪{u} as being endowed with a Borel linear
ordering < where u is the maximum. As the full tree N<∞on N is countable, it
induces a well-ordering on the nodes of any tree in Tr(N) having the property that
the root ∅is always the least element and each level of the tree is always completely
enumerated before any successive level. We can thus identify a finitely-branching
tree T on X with a “labelled tree”, that is, a pair (S, ℓ) ∈Tr(N)×(X ∪{u})N, where
S is the underlying tree structure of T and for each n ∈N, ℓ(n) is the element of
X occurring at the nth node of T, with ℓ(n) = u if there is no such node. We

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
51
require that ℓlabels siblings (i.e., immediate successors of a common parent node)
in strictly increasing order with respect to < and the ordering on nodes described
above. This ensures that each tree T corresponds to a unique pair (S, ℓ). The
subset of Tr(N) × (X ∪{u})N consisting all such pairs is clearly Borel and the maps
described in (1) and (2) are simply the coordinate projections.
□
We can now describe the construction of the space of ends of a connected
manifold M = M(U,c) in a way that will be evidently Borel in the parameter
(U, c) ∈C(Top, Rn), given our preceding lemmas. First, if M happens to be com-
pact, which we may detect in a Borel way by Theorem 3.23, then we put TM = ∅.
For M noncompact, let Ki (for i ∈N) be a compact exhaustion of M. Define a
tree TM of open subsets of M as follows: The root of the tree is the node given
by M itself. If ⟨Q0, . . . , Qn⟩has been placed in TM, then let ⟨Q0, . . . , Qn, Qn+1⟩
be in TM if and only if Qn+1 is an unbounded connected component of Qn \ Kn
(equivalently, an unbounded connected component of M \ Kn which is contained
in Qn). The (complements of the) compact exhaustion can be obtained in a Borel
way by Lemma 3.31, boundedness detected by Lemma 3.32, and the connected
components of open subsets computed using Lemma 3.41, so we have thus proven:
Lemma 4.21. The map C(Top, Rn) →Tr(C(Top, Rn)) : (U, c) 7→TM(U,c) is Borel.
□
Next, we identify [TM], the set of infinite branches through TM, with the set of
ends of M. Define
E(M) = M ⊔[TM],
which we topologize as follows: a basis for the topology consists of all open subsets
of M together with, for each i ∈N and each unbounded connected component Q
of M \ Ki, Q ∪ε(Q), where ε(Q) is the set of all ends eventually contained in Q:
ε(Q) = {⟨Qi | i ∈N⟩∈[TM] | ∃i ∈N(Qi ⊆Q)}.
To see that this is, indeed, a basis, it suffices to consider ε(Q)∩ε(Q′), where Q and
Q′ are unbounded open connected sets as above. Given q ∈ε(Q) ∩ε(Q′), say with
q = ⟨Qi | i ∈N⟩, there are i, j ∈N such that Qi ⊆Q and Qj ⊆Q′. We may assume
that i ≤j, and so Qj ⊆Qi ⊆Q, from which it follows that ε(Qj) ⊆ε(Q) ∩ε(Q′).
We endow [TM] with the induced subspace topology generated by the sets ε(Q).
There is another natural topology on [TM] which it inherits from the tree struc-
ture on TM, namely the one generated by basic open sets of the form
N⟨Q0,...,Qn⟩= {q ∈[TM] | ⟨Q0, . . . , Qn⟩⊑q}
for ⟨Q0, . . . , Qn⟩∈TM. This is the topology induced on [TM] as a subspace of the
product QN, where Q is the discrete set of the countably-many Qi’s which occur
in TM. Consequently, under this topology, [TM] is a totally disconnected compact
(since TM is finitely branching) Polish space.
Lemma 4.22. The topologies on [TM] generated by the sets ε(Q) and N⟨Q0,...,Qn⟩
above, respectively, coincide.
Proof. We will show that each basic open set is open in the other topology. Let
⟨Q0, . . . , Qn⟩∈TM, so each Qi is an unbounded connected component of M \ Ki
and M = Q0 ⊇Q1 ⊇· · · ⊇Qn. Clearly, N⟨Q0,...,Qn⟩⊆ε(Qn). If q = ⟨Q′
i | i ∈N⟩∈
ε(Qn), then since Qn and Q′
n are both connected components of M \Kn which must

52
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
overlap, we have that Q′
n = Qn. By a similar argument, Qn uniquely determines
each of its predecessors Qn−1, . . . , Q0 in TM. Hence, N⟨Q0,...,Qn⟩= ε(Qn).
Conversely, given an unbounded connected component Q of M \ Ki for some i,
take q = ⟨Qi | i ∈N⟩∈ε(Q). If we let n ∈N be the least such that Qn ⊆Q, then
q ∈N⟨Q0,...,Qn⟩⊆ε(Q). This shows that the topologies coincide.
□
The next lemma will verify that E(M) is, in fact, the end compactification of M.
Lemma 4.23. For each connected manifold M:
1. E(M) is Hausdorff, second countable, and M is dense open in E(M).
2. E(M) is connected.
3. E(M) is compact.
4. [TM] = E(M) \ M is totally disconnected.
5. For any nonempty connected open set U ⊆E(M), U \ [TM] is connected.
Consequently, E(M) can be identified with the end compactification of M.
Proof. For (1), that E(M) is second countable and M is open and dense in E(M)
follows immediately from the definition of the topology on E(M). Since both M
and [TM] are Hausdorff, to show that E(M) is Hausdorff, it suffices to show that if
m ∈M and q = ⟨Qi | i ∈N⟩∈[TM], then they can be separated by disjoint open
sets. Choose an element of the exhaustion Ki which contains m in its interior. Then,
int(Ki) and Qi+1 ∪ε(Qi+1) are disjoint open sets containing m and q, respectively.
For (2), since M is dense in E(M) and connected, E(M) is connected as well.
In order see (3) that E(M) is compact, it suffices to show that it is sequentially
compact, since it is second countable. Let (xn)n∈N be a sequence in E(M); we claim
that it has a convergent subsequence. Since we already know that [TM] is compact,
we may assume the xn’s are entirely contained in M, and further, that they have no
subsequence which is bounded in M. Define a subsequence (xni)i∈N of (xn)n∈N as
follows: Start by taking xn0 = x0 and Q0 = M. Let Q1 be an unbounded connected
component of M \K0 which contains infinitely many xn’s. Such a component exists
because there are only finitely many unbounded components of M \ K0 and the
xn’s are unbounded. Choose one such xn with n > n0, call it xn1. Suppose we
have chosen xn0, xn1, . . . , xni and ⟨Q0, Q1, . . . , Qi⟩∈TM such that xni ∈Qi and
infinitely many xn’s lie in Qi. Let Qi+1 be an unbounded component of M \ Ki+1
which contains infinitely many xn’s. Choose one such xni+1 ∈Qi+1 with ni+1 > ni.
It then follows from the definition of the topology on E(M) that (xni)i∈N converges
to q = ⟨Qi | i ∈N⟩∈[TM].
We have already observed (4) that [TM] is totally disconnected.
For (5), let U be a connected open set in E(M), which we may assume intersects
[TM]. Suppose that U \ [TM] = U ∩M = O0 ∪O1, where O0 and O1 are disjoint
open sets in M. Let U0 be the union of O0 and all sets Q ∪ε(Q) where Q ⊆O0,
and U1 the the union of O1 and all sets Q ∪ε(Q) where Q ⊆O1. Clearly, U0 and
U1 are open and disjoint. Moreover, if q = ⟨Qi | i ∈N⟩∈U ∩[TM], then Qi ⊆O0
or O1, and so q ∈U0 or q ∈U1. This shows that U ⊆U0 ∪U1, but U is connected
and U0 ∩U1 = ∅, so one of U0 or U1 is empty. It follows that either O0 or O1 is
empty, proving that U \ [TM] is connected.
Lastly, the end compactification of M is (up to a homeomorphism which is the
identity on M) the unique compactification satisfying (1) through (5) ([Ray60,
Thm. 1.8]).
□

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
53
4.4. Genus, orientability, and planarity. The classification result in Theorem
4.2 refers to the “genus” and “orientability class” of an arbitrary surface, generaliz-
ing the familiar notions for compact surfaces. Intuitively, for an orientable surface,
the genus counts the number of “handles”, while for a nonorientable surface, it
counts the number of “cross caps”18 divided by a factor of 2. In the case of surfaces
homeomorphic to finite simplicial complexes, these invariants can be computed
directly from the finite complex. For arbitrary surfaces, the genus can then be
defined as the (possibly infinite) limit of the genuses coming from an exhaustion of
the surface by finite polyhedra, as in Lemma 4.17, which is a Borel operation.
Following [Ric63], there are four orientability-classes for arbitrary surfaces:
• the orientable: those for which all compact subsurfaces are orientable;
• the infinitely nonorientable: those for which no complement of a compact
subsurface is orientable;
• the odd nonorientable: those for which every sufficiently large compact
subsurface contains an odd number of cross caps;
• the even nonorientable: likewise, but with an even number of cross caps.
Again, the ability to compute exhaustions by finite polyhedra and their invariants
(e.g., Betti numbers), by Lemma 4.17, implies that we can compute the orientability
class of an arbitrary surface in a Borel way, as well (cf. Corollary 3.25). We also
need the notion of a planar surface, i.e., one in which every compact subsurface has
genus zero, which can likewise be checked in a Borel way. To summarize:
Lemma 4.24. The following can be computed in a Borel fashion on C(Top, R2):
1. Genus (in N ∪{∞}).
2. Planarity (“yes” or “no”).
3. Orientability class (“orientable”, “infinitely nonorientable”, “odd nonori-
entable”, or “even nonorientable”.)
□
We can now define (non)planarity and (non)orientability for ends:
Definition 4.25. Suppose that q = ⟨Qi | i ∈N⟩is an end of a surface M.
1. We say that q is nonplanar if Qi is nonplanar for all i ∈N.
2. We say that q is nonorientable if Qi is nonorientable for all i ∈N.
Given a surface M and the tree TM defined in Section 4.3, we can define subtrees
T ′
M and T ′′
M of TM such that ⟨Q0, . . . , Qn⟩∈T ′
M (T ′′
M, respectively) if and only if
for all i ≤n, Qn is nonplanar (nonorientable). Then, for q ∈[TM], we have that
q ∈[T ′
M] ([T ′′
M], respectively) if and only if q is a nonplanar (nonorientable) end
of M. By Lemmas 4.20 and 4.24, these trees can be likewise computed in a Borel
way. Combining this with Lemma 4.21, we have the following:
Lemma 4.26. The map C(Top, R2) →Tr(C(Top, R2))3 given by
(U, c) 7→(TM(U,c), T ′
M(U,c), T ′′
M(U,c))
is Borel.
□
18A “cross cap” is simply an embedded Möbius band.
