J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
4.5. Classification by countable structures. Recall that K(NN) is the space
of all compact subsets of the Baire space NN, endowed with the Vietoris topol-
ogy. Let K(NN)3 be the closed subspace of K(NN)3 consisting of all nested triples
(K, K′, K′′) of compact subsets K ⊇K′ ⊇K′′ of NN. The space K({0, 1}N)3 is
defined similarly for the Cantor space {0, 1}N. On such nested triples, we write
(K, K′, K′′) ∼=3 (L, L′, L′′) if there is a homeomorphism f : K →L that restricts
to homeomorphisms K′ →L′ and K′′ →L′′.
By Lemma 4.26, we have a Borel function on C(Top, R2) which, given a surface
M = M(U,c), for (U, c) ∈C(Top, R2), produces a triple (TM, T ′
M, T ′′
M) of trees of
open subsets of M whose branch spaces are exactly the space of ends [TM], the space
of nonplanar ends [TM]′, and the space of nonorientable ends [TM]′′. Using Lemma
4.20, we can then obtain a triple (SM, S′
M, S′′
M) of nested trees on N isomorphic to
(TM, T ′
M, T ′′
M), in the obvious sense, and compute the triple
(e(M), e(M)′, e(M)′′) := ([SM], [S′
M], [S′′
M]) ∈K(NN)3.
Applying Theorem 4.2, we have thus proven:
Theorem 4.27. On each genus and orientability class of C(Top, R2), there is a
Borel reduction of homeomorphism to ∼=3 on K(NN)3.
□
The following general lemma lets us fix the genus and orientability class, and
focus on equivalence in K(NN)3.
Lemma 4.28. If E is an analytic equivalence relation on a standard Borel space
X and there is a partition of X into countably many E-invariant Borel pieces Xi
(for i ∈N) such that E|Xi is classifiable by countable structures for each i ∈N,
then so is E.
Proof. By assumption, for each i ∈N, E|Xi ≤B Fi, where Fi is the orbit equivalence
relation induced by a continuous action of S∞on a Polish space Yi.
Put Y =
`
i∈N Yi and let S∞act on Y via its action on each of the disjoint pieces Yi, with
orbit equivalence relation F. Then, E ≤B F and F is classifiable by countable
structures, being an orbit equivalence relation of an S∞-action.
□
To complete the proof of Theorem 4.1, it thus suffices to produce a Borel reduc-
tion from the space K(NN)3 to some space of countable first-order structures. The
target will be the space BA3 of triples (B, F, G), where B is a countable Boolean
algebra with domain N, and F ⊆G are nested filters in B; the class of all such
structures is clearly first-order axiomatizable in the language of Boolean algebras,
together with unary predicates for F and G, and thus Borel. The reduction is,
essentially, given by Stone duality.
Lemma 4.29. The relation ∼=3 on K(NN)3 is Borel reducible to the isomorphism
relation ∼= on BA3.
Proof. First, define Ψ : K({0, 1}N)3 →BA3 by Ψ(K, K′, K′′) = (B, F, G) as follows:
Let B be the algebra of clopen subsets of K, F (G, respectively) the filter of clopen
sets whose complements are contained in K \ K′ (or K \ K′′), where we identify
(B, F, G) with an element of BA3 by using an enumeration of the clopen subsets of
{0, 1}N. Under this identification, Ψ is Borel by the arguments in [CG01], and is a
reduction to isomorphism on BA3 by classical Stone duality (see [Hal63]).
It remains to produce a Borel reduction from ∼=3 on K(NN)3 to the similarly
defined relation ∼=3 on K({0, 1}N)3. Doing so amounts to embedding all finitely

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
55
branching pruned subtrees of N<∞into {0, 1}<∞in a sufficiently uniform way.
For X countable and discrete, finitely branching pruned subtrees of X<∞can be
identified with compact subsets of XN via their spaces of branches, T 7→[T], and
vice versa (see [Kec95, Ch. 2]). Viewing the space of such trees as a Borel subset of
{0, 1}X<∞, this identification gives a Borel bijection with K(XN) under the Vietoris
topology: If Ns is a basic open set in XN, for s ∈X<∞, then for a compact set
K ⊆XN, say K = [T], K ∩Ns ̸= ∅if and only if all t ∈T are compatible with s,
clearly a Borel condition.
Given a triple of finitely branching pruned subtrees (S, S′, S′′) of N<∞, with
S ⊇S′ ⊇S′′, we define a map φ : S →{0, 1}<∞inductively as follows: Put
φ(∅) = ∅. Having defined φ(s), for s ∈S, if the immediate successors of s in
S are {s⌢(n1), s⌢(n2), . . . , s⌢(nk)}, with n1 < n2 < · · · < nk, then we define
φ(s⌢(ni)) = φ(s)⌢ti, where ti is the ith leaf node of the complete binary tree of
height ⌈log2(k)⌉under the lexicographic order. Let T, T ′, and T ′′ be the downwards
closures of the image of S, S′, and S′′, respectively, under φ. Then, φ is an order
and incompatibility-preserving map which carries S, S′, and S′′ onto dense subsets
of T, T ′, and T ′′, respectively. Consequently, the map φ∗: [S] →[T], defined by
φ∗(x) =
[
n∈N
φ(x|n)
for x ∈[S], is a homeomorphism from [S] onto [T] which carries [S′] onto [T ′]
and [S′′] onto [T ′′].
It is then evident from the preceding description that the
assignment Φ([S], [S′], [S′′]) = ([T], [T ′], [T ′′]) is a Borel reduction of ∼=3 on K(NN)3
to the corresponding relation on K({0, 1}N)3.
□
Theorem 4.1 now follows from Theorem 4.27 and Lemmas 4.24, 4.28, and 4.29.
Moving to smooth surfaces, we have noted in Example 3.18 that the inclusion map
C(C∞, R2) →C(Top, R2) is a Borel reduction of diffeomorphism ∼=C∞to homeomor-
phism ∼=Top, and so the former is also classifiable by countable structures, proving:
Corollary 4.30. The diffeomorphism relation ∼=C∞on C(C∞, R2) is complete for
countable structures.
□
Finally, by the discussion at the end of Section 4.2, we also have the following
immediate consequence:
Corollary 4.31. The relation of combinatorial equivalence ≡on the space Spoly of
all 2-dimensional polyhedra is complete for countable structures.
□
4.6. Disconnected surfaces. To close out this section, we will show that the
homeomorphism and diffeomorphism problems for all topological and smooth, re-
spectively, 2-manifolds, i.e., possibly disconnected surfaces, is of the same com-
plexity as that for connected surfaces. As the latter is an invariant subclass of
the former, it suffices to show that the larger class is still classifiable by countable
structures. We will focus on the topological case, as identical arguments apply in
the smooth case.
Using Lemma 3.41, we can compute the connected components of a 2-manifold,
as an ordered sequence of open submanifolds, in a Borel fashion.
This reduces
the question of whether two 2-manifolds are homeomorphic to whether their cor-
responding components are, up to permuting their order. As this permutation is
induced by an action of S∞, we are able to use general techniques to show that this
relation is also classifiable by countable structures.

56
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Given an analytic equivalence relation E on a standard Borel space X, define an
equivalence relation EN on XN by
(xi)i∈N EN (yi)i∈N if and only if there is a σ ∈S∞such that xσ(i) E yi for all i ∈N.
Lemma 4.32. If E is classifiable by countable structures, then so is EN.
Proof. By assumption, there is a continuous action of S∞on a Polish space Y such
that if F is the resulting orbit equivalence relation, then E ≤B F. It follows that
EN ≤B F N, so it suffices to show that F N is classifiable by countable structures.
Consider the action of S∞× SN
∞on Y N given by
(σ, (gi)i∈N) · (yi)i∈N = (gi · yσ(i))i∈N
for all (σ, (gi)i∈N) ∈S∞× SN
∞. Clearly, this action is continuous and has F N as its
orbit equivalence relation. S∞×SN
∞is isomorphic as a Polish group to SN
∞, which in
turn is isomorphic to a closed subgroup of S∞; identify N with N×N via some fixed
bijection N →N × N and let the ith coordinate of an element (gi)i∈N ∈SN
∞act on
the ith column {i} × N in the obvious way. The orbit equivalence relation induced
by a continuous action of any closed subgroup of S∞is classifiable by countable
structures (see [BK96] and [Hjo00, Thm. 2.39]), hence F N is as well.
□
Corollary 4.33. The homeomorphism relation ∼=Top on M(Top, R2) is complete for
countable structures. Similarly for the diffeomorphism relation ∼=C∞on M(C∞, R2).
Proof. Lemma 3.41 implies that there is a Borel map f : M(Top, R2) →C(Top, R2)N
such that for each (U, c) ∈M(Top, R2), f(U, c) = ⟨(Vi, c|Vi) | i ∈N⟩is a sequence
of (parameters for) connected surfaces which are exactly the connected components
of M(U,c). The map given therein may have repetition, i.e., different (Vi, c|Vi) may
represent the same component of M(U,c), but by checking one-by-one whether this
is the case using Lemma 3.21 and dropping those which represent the same open
sets as previous ones, we are able to eliminate any repetition in a Borel fashion.
Thus, f : M(Top, R2) →C(Top, R2)N is a Borel reduction of ∼=Top on M(Top, R2)
to ∼=N
Top on C(Top, R2)N. By Theorem 4.1 and Lemma 4.32, the latter is classifiable
by countable structures, and thus so is the former. The same argument applies in
the smooth case.
□
Lastly, we wish to point out a descriptive set-theoretic reason for focusing on
connected manifolds in our analysis of the complexity of different classification
problems. Consider the equivalence relation Ectbl, defined on RN by
(xi)i∈N Ectbl (yi)i∈N
if and only if
{xi : i ∈N} = {yi : i ∈N},
where the right-hand side is considered as equality between two countable sets of
reals. Notice that Ectbl is the orbit equivalence relation of the action of S∞on RN
given by permuting indices. It is known that Ectbl is not essentially countable (see
[Gao09, Exer. 8.3.3]). Suppose that M is some Borel class of manifolds which is
closed under countable disjoint unions and for which the relevant (analytic) notion
of isomorphism ∼= can be determined by showing that connected components are
pairwise equivalent. We claim that if there is an uncountable Borel set A of pair-
wise inequivalent connected manifolds in M, then Ectbl Borel reduces to ∼=. For
such an A, there is a Borel bijection f : R →A. Consider the set AN of sequences
in A, which we may map to their disjoint unions considered as elements of M.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
57
Together with f, this mapping induces a Borel reduction of Ectbl to ∼= on M. Con-
sequently, Ectbl is the minimum complexity that we can expect for the associated
classification problem on M, unless we restrict to connected manifolds. As we shall
see in the following sections, there are natural classification problems for classes
of connected manifolds which have intermediate complexity, strictly between the
concretely classifiable and Ectbl.
5. The bireducibility of isometry and conjugacy problems
The focus of this section is the well-known correspondence between complete
connected hyperbolic n-manifolds and discrete subgroups of Isom(Hn). This is, of
course, an instance of a much wider correspondence between discrete subgroups
of groups acting by homeomorphism on topological spaces and their associated
quotient spaces, one in which the Cartan–Hadamard Theorem centrally figures
([BH99, Ch. II.4]; see also its Thm. III.G.1.13). Our focus on the hyperbolic case
is for simplicity; the task of the present section is simply to show:
1. that the standard conversions from such manifolds to discrete groups and
back are each, within our framework, Borel, and, moreover,
2. that they are reductions of the relevant equivalence relations, or in other
words map isometric manifolds to conjugate subgroups, and vice versa.
The second point follows from the standard constructions and theorems, which we
now outline; in subsequent subsections, it will remain only to argue the first.
We precede our more formal argumentation with a brief review of the conversions
in question. Here again a number of closely related approaches are possible, but the
keywords for what is probably the most intuitive conversion of manifolds to discrete
groups are developing map and holonomy. The basic points of this conversion are
as follows (see [Thu97, §3.4–5] or [Rat19, §8.4–5], for example, for more detailed
and general accounts); throughout, we assume that n ≥2:
• A hyperbolic structure (i.e., an (Isom, Hn)-structure) on a connected man-
ifold M lifts to its universal cover ˜
M; recall that the latter may be viewed
as the space of endpoint-preserving homotopy classes of paths in M from
some fixed basepoint x ∈M.
• If M is a complete connected hyperbolic n-manifold, then the developing
map defines a continuous surjection s from ˜
M onto Hn. The idea of this map
is a simple one: fix first a z ∈Hn and extend the map x 7→z to an isometry
e of a neighborhood of x in M with a neighborhood of z in Hn. Any “point”
[γ] ∈˜
M (i.e., endpoint-preserving homotopy class of a path γ from x to
some y ∈M) traverses a series of charts in M, which patch together via
transition maps at overlaps to constitute a unique path extending e ◦γ in
Hn, and we let s([γ]) equal its endpoint. The argument that the function
s is well-defined is facilitated by the assumption, authorized by Lemma
3.39, that the charts and overlaps in the atlas defining M are all simply-
connected; it also critically leverages the rigidity of Isom(Hn), in the sense
flagged in Example 3.17. Note also that the focus which this construction
fosters on complete connected manifolds is, from our perspective, already
a natural one, by Lemmas 3.40 and 3.45 and the discussion in Section 4.6.
• The aforementioned rigidity amounts simply to the fact that any element of
the pseudogroup Isom with connected nonempty domain uniquely extends
to an element g of Isom(Hn), and hence that the association of each γ

58
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
above to a finite sequence of transition maps on connected overlaps in fact
defines, via the sequential composition of their unique extensions, a map
t : ˜
M →Isom(Hn). (Again, the verification that this map is well-defined
is standard.) Writing p for the covering map ˜
M →M, the restriction of t
to p−1(x) then represents π1(M, x) as a discrete subgroup Γ of Isom(Hn)
(this is its holonomy representation), one with the property that the quo-
tient structure Hn/Γ induced by the natural action of Isom(Hn) on Hn is
isometric to M. Moreover, since M is a hyperbolic manifold, and since any
torsion element of Γ would fix a point of Hn whose quotient neighborhoods
could never then be isometric to an open subset of Hn, we find that Γ must
be torsion-free.
The reverse conversion, of torsion-free discrete subgroups Γ of Isom(Hn) into hy-
perbolic n-manifolds, is even simpler; as noted, this is just the map Γ 7→Hn/Γ to
the quotient by the natural action of Γ. In our framework, though, a manifold is a
collection (U, c) of charts, so we need to work a little harder: we enumerate a dense
subset Q = (qi)i∈N of Hn and let each Ui be a ball of maximal radius about qi such
that Ui ∩g · Ui = ∅for any g ∈Γ\{id}; natural transition maps φi,j associate to
this family, and we verify in Section 5.3 that this construction is both as desired
and, in the appropriate sense, Borel.
Readers for whom these conversions are unfamiliar are encouraged to consider the
relation between discrete subgroups of Isom+(R) ∼= R and the associated induced
metrics on topological copies of S1. What this example in its transparency obscures
(since Isom+(R) is abelian), however, is the interconnection of the relations of
conjugacy on the group side with those of isometry on the manifold side.
The
conversion of a manifold M to a discrete group described above involved the choice
of an isometry of a neighborhood of some point x ∈M with an open set in Hn. It is
not difficult to see that varying these choices corresponds to conjugating the output
Γ by elements of Isom(Hn), and it is a theorem that the isometries of hyperbolic
manifolds amount to just these variations; the general form of [MT98, Thm. 1.18]
(or see [Thu97, Cor. 3.5.12]), for example, is the following:
Theorem. For any complete connected hyperbolic n-manifold M, there exists a
torsion-free discrete subgroup Γ of Isom(Hn) such that M ∼= Hn/Γ. Such a Γ is
unique up to conjugation by elements of Isom(Hn), and conversely, Hn/Γ is, for
any such Γ, a complete connected hyperbolic n-manifold.
After a brief review of spaces of discrete subgroups, we record Borel measurable
realizations of the two directions of this correspondence in Sections 5.2 and 5.3.
5.1. Spaces of discrete subgroups. We begin by describing the spaces of sub-
groups to and from which our manifold parametrizations will usefully reduce. Recall
from Section 2.3 that, given a locally compact Polish group G, F(G) denotes the
Polish space of closed subsets of G, endowed with the Chabauty–Fell topology. The
following aggregates results recorded in both [CEM06, I.3] and [BP92, E.1].
Lemma 5.1. Let G be a locally compact Polish group.
1. The closed subgroups of G form a closed subset S(G) of F(G).
2. The set D(G) of discrete subgroups of G is open in S(G).
3. The set Stf(G) of torsion-free subgroups of G is closed in S(G).

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
59
In particular, D(G), Stf(G), and Dtf(G) = D(G) ∩Stf(G) each inherit a Polish
subspace topology from F(G).
Proof. All assertions except the last appear in the aforementioned references; the
framing there is in terms of second countable Lie groups, but a locally compact
Polish structure in each case clearly suffices. The last assertion is then immediate
from the fact that the subspace topology on countable intersections of open subsets
of Polish spaces is Polish.
□
Observe that G acts continuously on S(G) by conjugation, and the subsets D(G),
Stf(G), and Dtf(G) are all invariant under this action. We will write E(G, −) for
the resulting orbit equivalence relation on the relevant subset (−) of D(G).
In
particular, note that E(G, Dtf(G)) ≤B E(G, D(G)) via the inclusion of Dtf(G) in
D(G). As noted, our focus for the remainder of this section will be the groups
G = Isom(Hn) for n ≥2; in Sections 7 and 8, we will tend to approach these
groups by way of their subgroups Isom+(Hn) of orientation-preserving isometries,
whose theory is, in oddly compatible senses, both simpler and richer (in part for
its interactions with Riemannian theory; Isom+(Hn) is the group of conformal
homeomorphisms of Hn [BP92, §A.4]). Such an approach is fairly standard, and
natural, given how frequently results in the orientable setting generalize; in our
own context, the main tool for this kind of upgrade is provided by Lemma 6.11
below. Note lastly that Isom(Hn), and hence Isom+(Hn) ≤Isom(Hn), is endowed
with the compact-open topology, from which it is immediate that each group acts
continuously on Hn.
5.2. Discrete groups from manifolds. The task of this subsection is to show
that the conversion outlined in this section’s introduction, of hyperbolic manifolds
into discrete groups, is Borel. More formally, for any n ≥1, let C∗
c(Isom, Hn) denote
the restriction of M∗(Isom, Hn) (see equation (5) of Section 3.3 and the discussion
surrounding it) to parametrizations of complete connected (Isom, Hn)-manifolds
and ∼=Isom(Hn) the isometry relation on it.
We will prove the following theorem.
Theorem 5.2. There exists a Borel function ν : C∗
c(Isom, Hn) →Dtf(Isom(Hn))
witnessing that ∼=Isom(Hn) ≤B E(Isom(Hn), Dtf(Isom(Hn)).
Proof. Observe that, by the lemmas of Section 3.3, C∗
c(Isom, Hn) is Borel and that
we may, moreover, precede ν with the reparametrizing function r
of Lemma 3.39; we may and will, in other words, without loss of generality
assume that the parametrized atlases therein all have geodesically convex charts
and overlap. Next, enumerate in ordertype ω a dense subset Q of Hn, and for any
(U, c) let ¯q(U,c) be the q ∈Q ∩U0 of minimum index. Any γ : [0, 1] →M(U,c)
representing an element of π1(M(U,c), ¯q(U,c)) is covered by (the images of) a finite
sequence of Ui(j) ∈U (0 ≤j ≤k), one in which we may take Ui(0) = Ui(k) = U0. As
described in this section’s introduction (wherein the identification of the quotient-
image of U0 with U0 plays the role of e), γ thereby traverses a series of transition
maps φi(j),i(j+1) (0 ≤j < k), each of which uniquely extends to an element gj of
Isom(Hn). The following facts are standard; see [Rat19, §8.4] or [CEM06, I.1.4]:
• the map taking γ to g0 · · · · · gk−1 ∈Isom(Hn) is unaffected by endpoint-
preserving homotopies of γ;

60
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
• the image ν(U, c) of the induced homomorphism of π1(M(U,c), ¯q(U,c)) into
Isom(Hn) is both discrete and torsion-free.
Our task is simply to show that the assignment (U, c) 7→ν(U, c) defines a Borel
map from C∗
c(Isom, Hn) to Dtf(Isom(Hn)). To that end, it will suffice to show that
the ν-preimages of the sets {F ∈F(Isom(Hn)) | F ∩U ̸= ∅}, where U ranges
over the open subsets of Isom(Hn), are Borel, since these sets generate the Effros
Borel structure on F(Isom(Hn)). Observe therefore that ν−1[{F ∈F(Isom(Hn)) |
F ∩U ̸= ∅}] is the set of those (U, c) such that there exist
i. rational 0 = a0 < · · · < ak+1 = 1, and
ii. continuous maps γj : [aj, aj+1] →Ui(j) for 0 ≤j ≤k, with i(0) = i(k) = 0
and γ0(0) = γk(1) = ¯q(U,c) and gj(γj(aj+1)) = γj+1(aj+1) for 0 ≤j < k,
where gj is that element of Isom(Hn) uniquely extending φi(j),i(j+1) (and
the operation of Isom(Hn) on points of Hn is that induced by the action of
Isom(Hn) on Hn),
iii. such that g0 · · · · · gk−1 ∈U.
On its surface, this is a Σ1
1 condition. Note, however, that by our assumptions
deriving from Lemma 3.39 we may replace (ii) with
ii’. maps pj : {aj, aj+1} →Q ∩Ui(j) for 0 ≤j ≤k, with i(0) = i(k) = 0 and
p0(0) = pk(1) = ¯q(U,c), and such that pj(aj+1) and pj+1(aj+1) both fall in
Ui(j),i(j+1) for 0 ≤j < k.
This is because, by the convexity of the regions in question, the functions pj will
determine uniform parametrizations γj : [aj, aj+1] →Ui(j) of the unique geodesics
from pj(aj) to pj(aj+1) and from pj+1(aj+1) to pj+1(aj+1) which, concatenated, are
homotopic to those of the type described in item (ii), and moreover, that any “loop”
as in item (ii) is homotopic to one of this form. Since items (i), (ii’), and (iii) only
quantify over countably many Borel conditions, then, ν−1[{F ∈F(Isom+(Hn)) |
F ∩U ̸= ∅}] is Borel, as desired.
□
Observe that the above argument will, with only superficial modifications, more
generally apply to define a Borel function C∗
c(G, X) →D(G) reducing the relation
of (G, X)-isomorphism to that of G-conjugacy whenever the conditions of Lemma
3.39 hold (namely, that X is locally geodesically convex and locally compact Polish,
and G is a Borel subpseudogroup of Isom).
Remark 5.3. It’s standard to topologize spaces of hyperbolic manifolds by way of
the Chabauty–Fell topology on D(Isom(Hn)), together with identifications M 7→
ΓM much like those constructed just above. More precisely, both [CEM06] and
[BP92] topologize the space MFn of baseframed complete connected hyperbolic n-
manifolds via its bijection with Dtf(Isom(Hn)), then endow the space MBn of base-
pointed complete connected hyperbolic n-manifolds and the space MWn of unbase-
pointed complete connected hyperbolic n-manifolds each with the quotient topologies
associating to the forgetful functors MFn →MBn and MFn →MWn. These
conversions are a further reason the Chabauty–Fell topology is sometimes termed
the geometric topology: natural notions of convergence of manifold structures in
more plainly geometric senses (the (ε, r)-relations of [CEM06, I.3.2] or the formally
stronger relations of [BP92, pp. 166–168] or the smooth topology of [BLL] exploited
in the proofs of Theorem 7.2 below) turn out to coincide with those inherited, via
these conversions, from the Chabauty–Fell topology on D(Isom(Hn)). Within this

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
61
framework, however, the space MWn of hyperbolic n-manifolds without distin-
guished basepoint fails to be Hausdorff. A main heuristic for this fact is recorded
in [CEM06, p. 68]; there, distinct sequences in MB2 which map to a single sequence
in MW2, but whose limits do not map to a single point, are described. Against
this background, two main remarks are in order.
First, observe that the quotient of C∗
c(Isom, Hn) by ∼=Isom(Hn) is, as a set, naturally
isomorphic to MWn. Observe next that our computation, in Corollary 6.7 below,
that ∼=Isom(Hn) is essentially universal countable is a significantly stronger and more
precise result than the non-Hausdorffness of MWn; it carries, for example, the
heuristic not only that the MWn parametrization of hyperbolic n-manifolds isn’t
Hausdorff (or even T0; see Lemma 8.23), but that no meaningful one can be. In
contrast, by the analyses of Sections 7 and 8, failures of Hausdorffness for the
restrictions of MW2 and MW3 to geometrically finite manifolds are of a weaker
sort: those analyses furnish each of these restrictions with a natural standard Borel
structure, or, in other words, with a Borel isomorphism with a Polish space.
Second, the ν of Theorem 5.2 is, in essence, defined by viewing C∗
c(Isom, Hn) as
a space of atlases on “covertly baseframed” manifolds; more precisely, the choice of
a dense Q ⊆Hn and canonical framing of Hn allows us to see C∗
c(Isom, Hn) as such
in a Borel way. A similar enhancement is at work in the arguments of Section 7
below.
5.3. Manifolds from discrete groups. In this subsection, we describe a Borel
reduction reversing that of Theorem 5.2. An immediate corollary is that the re-
lations of equivalence among complete connected hyperbolic n-manifolds and of
conjugacy among discrete torsion-free subgroups of Isom(Hn) are not just Borel
bireducible; they are classwise Borel equivalent as well.
Theorem 5.4. For any n ≥1, there exists a Borel function σ : Dtf(Isom(Hn)) →
C∗
c(Isom, Hn) witnessing that E(Isom(Hn), Dtf(Isom(Hn)) ≤B ∼=Isom(Hn).
In essence, our task is to show that the conversion of a discrete Γ to the manifold
Hn/Γ factors through C∗

the following variant of [Rat19, §6.6, Lem. 1]:
Lemma 5.5. For any torsion-free discrete group Γ ≤Isom(Hn) and compact K ⊆
Hn, there exists an ℓ> 0 such that d(x, y) > ℓfor all x ∈K and y ∈{g · x | g ∈
Γ\{id}} ∩K.

open ball about x of maximal radius such that g · DΓ(x) ∩DΓ(x) = ∅for all
g ∈Γ\{id}.19 Note that DΓ(x) is geodesically convex. Let σ of such a Γ equal the
(U, c) defined by:

Since the

is Borel for any connected compact K ⊆Hn. But this set is a relatively open subset

is a relatively open subset of Z0.
For item (2), recall from Definition 3.11 that the charts φk,ℓ: Uk,ℓ→Uℓ,k com-
prising the second coordinate in a manifold parameter (U, c) are triples of the type
(domain, range, sequence recording φk,ℓoutputs on a dense subset of its domain).
That σ-preimages of Borel subsets of its first or second coordinates are Borel we just
argued. Hence we may confine our attention to the third coordinate, which is, more
formally, a sequence (xi)i∈N in Hn ∪{u} wherein xi = φk,ℓ(qi) if qi ∈dom(φk,ℓ)
and xi otherwise equals the default variable u. Our task then further reduces to
showing that the σ-preimage of the collection of Isom elements mapping a given
qi to an arbitrary open U ⊆Hn is Borel. But this is just {Γ | Γ · qi ∩U ̸= ∅}, a
relatively open subset of Dtf(Isom(Hn)) by the continuity of the action of Isom(Hn)
on Hn, and this completes the argument.

An immediate corollary of Theorems 5.2 and 5.4 is that
∼=Isom(Hn) ∼B E(Isom(Hn), Dtf(Isom(Hn))
or, in plainer English, that the relations of isometry for complete connected hy-
perbolic n-manifolds and those of conjugacy for discrete torsion-free subgroups of
Isom(Hn) are Borel bireducible. In fact, a stronger relation between them holds;
see [MR12, §2] for further discussion of the following notion.
Definition 5.6. Equivalence relations E and F on standard Borel spaces X and
Y are classwise Borel isomorphic if there exist Borel functions X →Y and Y →X
inducing inverse bijections of the sets X/E and Y/F.
The utility of this definition derives from the fact that Borel reductions do not,
in general, satisfy the Schröder–Bernstein property: a priori, there may exist Borel
lifts X →Y and Y →X of injections X/E →Y/F and Y/F →X/E, respectively,
without there existing Borel lifts of any bijection X/E →Y/F together with its
inverse. The ν and σ of Theorems 5.2 and 5.4, respectively, are, on the other hand,
Borel lifts of exactly the latter sort.
Corollary 5.7. The equivalence relations ∼=Isom(Hn) and E(Isom(Hn), Dtf(Isom(Hn))
of Theorems 5.2 and 5.4 are classwise Borel isomorphic.
Proof. That ν and σ induce bijections follows from the discussion which introduced
this section (along with its references); that these are inverse bijections follows
from the stronger fact that ν ◦σ = id. For the latter, observe that σ(Γ) is, in
the sense discussed in Remark 5.3, implicitly basepointed at q0, and that DΓ(q0)
may, moreover, be regarded as encoding a frame at q0. In fact, σ is the realization
in our setting of the map witnessing the homeomorphism Dtf(Isom(Hn)) →MFn
described in the orientation-preserving case at [BP92, Prop. E.1.9], and ν is the
realization of its left-inverse.
(A subtle point does arise here, via the implicit
precomposition, in the proof of Theorem 5.2, of ν with the reparametrizing r of
Lemma 3.39: to conserve the data of σ(Γ), we will require a version of r taking any
(U, c) with q0 ∈U0 to a (V, d) with q0 ∈V0; this presents no difficulty.)
□

64
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
In closing, note that replacing the group Isom(Hn) with its subgroup Isom+(Hn)
alters essentially none of this section’s arguments or results. Rather than clutter
things any further, we will occasionally reference Corollary 5.7 below as though it
had been formulated for the latter.
6. The conjugacy problem for discrete subgroups of Lie groups
In this section, we will consider the conjugacy relation on discrete subgroups of
more general Lie groups. As noted in Lemma 5.1, the set D(G) of discrete subgroups
of a locally compact Polish group G is itself a Polish space when endowed with
the Chabauty–Fell topology, and G acts continuously on D(G) by conjugation.
As above, we write E(G, D(G)) for the resulting orbit equivalence relation, and
E(G, Dtf(G)) for its restriction to the conjugation-invariant closed subset Dtf(G)
of torsion-free discrete subgroups of G.
We begin by noting two general facts about actions of locally compact Polish
groups. First, if G is compact, then any orbit equivalence relation resulting from
a continuous action of G on a Polish space X is concretely classifiable: each orbit
is compact, being a continuous image of G, and can be viewed as a “point” in the
space K(X) of all compact subsets of X, so the map which sends each x ∈X to
its orbit is a continuous reduction to equality. In fact, when G is a compact Lie
group, it has only countably many conjugacy classes of closed subgroups [Pal60,
Cor. 1.7.27]. For this reason, we confine our attention to noncompact groups.
Second, if G is locally compact and acts in a Borel way on any standard Borel
space, then the resulting orbit equivalence relation is essentially countable, that is,
bireducible with a countable Borel equivalence relation [Kec92]. In particular, the
conjugacy relation E(G, D(G)) for such a G is always essentially countable.
As mentioned in our introduction, Stuck and Zimmer [SZ94] showed that if F2 is
the (discrete) free group on two generators, then E(F2, D(F2)) is not concretely clas-
sifiable; they then extended this to any noncompact semisimple Lie group. Thomas
and Velickovic [TV99] strengthened the result for F2 by proving that E(F2, D(F2))
is, in fact, a universal countable Borel equivalence relation (see [Gao00] for a sim-
plified proof). Andretta, Camerlo, and Hjorth [ACH01] then generalized this to
any countable group containing F2:
Theorem 6.1 (Andretta–Camerlo–Hjorth). If Γ is a countable group which con-
tains a nonabelian free subgroup, then E(Γ, Dtf(Γ)) is a universal countable Borel
equivalence relation.
Note that if Γ contains F2, then D(F2) ⊆Dtf(Γ) and E(F2, D(F2)) is a sube-
quivalence relation of E(Γ, Dtf(Γ)). However, containment between countable Borel
equivalence relations does not, in general, imply Borel reducibility [Ada02], and
whether containing a universal countable Borel equivalence relation implies uni-
versality is a well-known open problem (see [JKL02] and [Tho09b]). The bulk of
[ACH01] is devoted to establishing universality for specific such examples using a
technical coding scheme; see also [Mar17] for related results and arguments.
Our main results in this section consist of generalizations of Theorem 6.1 to
a variety of Lie groups.
In particular, we show that if G is any matrix group
containing a discrete copy of F2, then the conjugacy relation E(G, Dtf(G)) is essen-
tially countable universal (Theorem 6.2). We then extend this to any noncompact
semisimple Lie group (Corollary 6.5), sharpening Stuck and Zimmer’s result. Then,

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
65
using the material of Section 5, we conclude that the isometry relation on hyper-
bolic n-manifolds is, likewise, essentially countable universal (Corollary 6.7), for
any n ≥2. We conclude the section with some lemmas concerning restrictions of
conjugacy relations to finitely generated subgroups which will be of use in Sections
7 and 8.
6.1. Matrix groups and their relatives. By a matrix group, we mean a closed
subgroup of GL(n, R) or GL(n, C). Our main result here is as follows:
Theorem 6.2. If G is a matrix group which contains a discrete nonabelian free
subgroup, then E(G, Dtf(G)) is essentially countable universal.
The key observation we use to prove Theorem 6.2 is that, given a countable
subgroup Γ of a matrix group G, conjugacy of subgroups of Γ by elements of G
can be reduced to conjugacy by elements of an intermediate, but still countable,
group Γ. This will enable us to leverage Theorem 6.1 and obtain a reduction. As
a concrete example, when G = SL(n, R) and Γ = SL(n, Z), one can show explicitly
that conjugation in Γ by elements of G can be reduced to conjugation by elements
of Γ = SL(n, Q); the idea is that conjugacy of two subsets corresponds to an
over-determined consistent linear system, which can thus be solved in terms of the
coefficients of the matrices involved. We caution that this intermediate group may
fail to be discrete in G; it will typically be dense.
Lemma 6.3. Let G be a matrix group and Γ a countable subgroup of G. Then,
there is a countable subgroup Γ of G such that:
i. Γ ≤Γ, and
ii. whenever ∆, ∆′ ⊆Γ are conjugate by a matrix in G, they are conjugate by
a matrix in Γ.
Proof. Suppose that G is a subgroup of GL(n, R); the complex case is identical.
We construct Γ as follows: Begin by letting Γ0 = Γ.
For each finitely many
A0, . . . , Am, B0, . . . , Bm ∈Γ0 such that there is some X ∈G for which
XAiX−1 = Bi
for all i ≤m, we add one such witness X to Γ0. Let Γ1 be subgroup of G generated
by Γ0 and the countably many witnesses thus added. In particular, Γ1 is countable.
Repeat this construction to define an increasing chain of countable subgroups Γn
(for n ∈N) of G, so that whenever finitely many elements of Γn are conjugate by an
element of G, they are conjugate by an element of Γn+1. Let Γ = S
n∈N Γn. Then,
Γ is a countable subgroup of G satisfying (i) and having the property that if finitely
many elements of Γ are conjugate by an element of G, then they are conjugate by
an element of Γ.20
Suppose that ∆, ∆′ are subsets of Γ which are conjugate via some X ∈G:
X∆X−1 = ∆′.
If ∆and ∆′ are both finite, then they are conjugate via an element of Γ, by
construction. So, assume that ∆and ∆′ are both infinite and enumerate them as
20Logicians will recognize Γ as a kind of elementary submodel. In fact, any countable Σ1-

the Löwenheim–Skolem Theorem.

∆= {Am : m ∈N} and ∆′ = {Bm : m ∈N} in such a way that, for all m ∈N,
XAmX−1 = Bm,
or equivalently,
XAm = BmX.
For each m ∈N, let

Then, each Cm is a linear subspace of M(n, R) containing X and thus so is C =

Since X ∈Cm0, we have that

for all i ≤m0. Hence, Y ∈Cm0 = C. But then,

as claimed.
□
Proof of Theorem 6.2. Suppose that G is a matrix group and that Γ is a discrete
copy of the free group on two generators lying inside of G. Let Γ be as in Lemma 6.3
and denote by Dtf(Γ) the space of all torsion-free subgroups of Γ which are discrete
in G. Then, Γ acts continuously by conjugation on Dtf(Γ), and we can write EΓ
for the resulting orbit equivalence relation. By Lemma 6.3(ii), the inclusion map
Dtf(Γ) →Dtf(G) is a reduction of EΓ to E(G, Dtf(G)).
It remains to say why EΓ is a universal countable Borel equivalence relation.
Theorem 6.1 tells us that the conjugacy relation on all torsion-free subgroups of Γ
is universal, but we have restricted to the invariant set of those which are discrete
in G, so we must further examine its proof.
There, we see that the reduction
(described on [ACH01, p. 203]), from a particular known universal countable Borel
equivalence relation to conjugacy, maps entirely into the space of subgroups of the
arbitrary free subgroup fixed at the start of the proof. By taking that free subgroup
to be the discrete Γ living inside of our Γ, we have that all of its further subgroups
are, of course, discrete in G. Thus, when applied to our case, their construction
shows that EΓ is universal countable, which completes the proof.
□
Not every Lie group is a matrix group, but many are closely related to via
quotients. In general, if G is a locally compact Polish group and N a discrete normal
subgroup, then G/N is locally compact Polish and the quotient map π : G →G/N
is continuous. If G is a Lie group, then G/N is also a Lie group, and π is smooth.
The quotient map induces a direct image map from subgroups of G to subgroups
of G/N, and an inverse image map going the other way. Direct images need not
preserve discreteness; consider G = R, N = Z, and Γ = tZ for t irrational. Inverse
images, on the other hand, always preserve discreteness, and the resulting map is
a reduction between conjugacy relations (cf. [Tho12, Lem. 4.2]).

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
67
Lemma 6.4. Suppose that G is a locally compact Polish group, N a discrete nor-
mal subgroup of G, and π : G →G/N the quotient map. Then, the inverse im-
age map π−1 is a continuous injection D(G/N) →D(G) and a reduction from
E(G/N, D(G/N)) to E(G, D(G)); likewise for the restrictions to Dtf(G/N) and
Dtf(G).
Proof. To see that π−1 preserves discreteness, let Γ ≤G/N be a discrete subgroup
of G/N. Fix an open neighborhood U of the identity, eN = N in G/N, such that
Γ ∩U = {eN}. Then,
π−1[Γ] ∩π−1[U] = π−1[Γ ∩U] = π−1[{eN}] = N.
Let V ⊆π−1[U] be an open neighborhood of the identity in G such that N ∩V =
{e}. If g ∈π−1[Γ] ∩V , then g ∈π−1[Γ] ∩π−1[U] = N, so g = e. Thus, π−1[Γ]
is discrete in G. That π−1 : D(G/N) →D(G) is injective is clear and that it is
continuous follows from standard facts about the Chabauty–Fell topology.
To see that π−1 is a reduction, suppose that ∆, ∆′ ∈D(G/N) are such that
π(g)∆π(g)−1 = ∆′ for some g ∈G. If h ∈π−1[∆], then
π(ghg−1) = π(g)π(h)π(g)−1 ∈∆′,
while if h′ ∈π−1[∆′], then
π(g−1h′g) = π(g)−1π(h′)π(g) ∈∆,
showing that gπ−1[∆]g−1 = π−1[∆′]. Conversely, if gπ−1[∆]g−1 = π−1[∆′] for some
g ∈G, then clearly π(g)∆π(g)−1 = ∆′. Finally, note that π−1 clearly preserves
being torsion-free.
□
Consequently, if G is a Lie group which has a quotient, by a discrete normal
subgroup, which is a matrix group containing a discrete nonabelian free subgroup,
then E(G, Dtf(G)) is essentially countable universal. Examples of this sort include
universal covers of matrix groups and all noncompact semisimple Lie groups.
To elaborate on the semisimple case, we will need to recall some basic facts from
Lie theory, which we summarize here (we refer to [Pat88, Appendix B]; see also
[Kna02]): Suppose that G is a connected Lie group and g its Lie algebra. Then,
G acts on g by automorphisms via the adjoint representation G →Ad(G), where
Ad(G) is a closed subgroup of GL(g).
In particular, Ad(G) is a matrix group.
Since G is connected, the kernel of the adjoint representation is the center Z(G) of
G [Pat88, B12], so Ad(G) ∼= G/Z(G).
A Lie group G is semisimple if it is connected and has no nontrivial connected
solvable normal subgroups; this implies that Z(G) is discrete [Pat88, B38]. If G is
noncompact and semisimple, then these properties are inherited by Ad(G), as they
can be characterized in terms of the Lie algebra (see [Pat88, B38, B48]), and so
Ad(G) contains a discrete nonabelian free subgroup by [Pat88, Thm. 3.8]. Thus,
E(Ad(G), Dtf(Ad(G))) is essentially countable universal by Theorem 6.2, and hence
so is E(G, Dtf(G)) by Lemma 6.4. Thus, we have proved our strengthening of the
Stuck and Zimmer result mentioned above:
Corollary 6.5. If G is a noncompact semisimple Lie group, then E(G, Dtf(G)) is
essentially countable universal.
□
We can also easily extend Theorem 6.2 to quotients of matrix groups:

68
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Theorem 6.6. Let G be a matrix group and N a discrete normal subgroup of G.
If G/N contains a discrete nonabelian free subgroup, then E(G/N, Dtf(G/N)) is
essentially countable universal.
Proof. It suffices to show that Lemma 6.3 applies to G/N, as the remainder of
the proof of Theorem 6.2 goes through unchanged. Suppose that Γ is a countable
subgroup of G/N.
Since N is countable, π−1[Γ] is a countable subgroup of G.
Then, we can apply Lemma 6.3 to π−1[Γ] and G to obtain a countable subgroup
π−1[Γ] of G which contains π−1[Γ] and such that conjugation of subsets of π−1[Γ]
by elements of G reduces to conjugation by elements of π−1[Γ].
Let Γ = π[π−1[Γ]] = π−1[Γ]/N. Then, Γ is a countable subgroup of G/N which
contains Γ. If ∆, ∆′ are subsets of Γ which are conjugate in G/N, then π−1[∆] and
π−1[∆] are subsets of π−1[Γ] which are conjugate in G, hence conjugate in π−1[Γ].
But then, as in the proof of Lemma 6.4, ∆and ∆′ must be conjugate in Γ.
□
We suspect that these results can be extended to all Lie groups which contain a
discrete nonabelian free subgroup; for connected Lie groups, these are exactly those
which are nonamenable as locally compact groups [Pat88, Thm. 3.8]. However, the
methods used here seem specific to groups of matrices or related groups, and not
applicable to the broader class of all locally compact Polish groups.
6.2. The isometry problem for hyperbolic n-manifolds. Recall that the group
Isom(Hn) of all isometries of n-dimensional hyperbolic space is a locally compact
Polish group when endowed with the compact-open topology. In fact, Isom(Hn)
may be regarded as a matrix Lie group.
The more familiar identifications of
Isom+(H2) and Isom+(H3) with PSL(2, R) and PSL(2, C) will play a prominent
role in Sections 7 and 8, respectively, below.
For n ≥1, the Lorentz group21 O(1, n) consists of all (n + 1) × (n + 1) real
invertible matrices A such that AJAt = J, where

6.3. Conjugacy and finitely generated subgroups. While the lemmas of Sec-
tion 6.1 are concerned with transferring universality in the conjugacy relation for
discrete subgroups between a group and its quotients, the lemmas below will be put
to use in transferring concrete classifiability and (essential) hyperfiniteness, when
restricted to finitely generated subgroups, in Sections 7 and 8. Let us first verify
that the latter restriction defines a standard Borel space.
Lemma 6.8. Let G be a locally compact Polish group. The set Dfg(G) of finitely
generated discrete subgroups of G is a Borel subset of D(G).
Proof. Let f : GN →F(G) be given by f((gn)n∈N) = ⟨gn⟩n∈N, the closure of the
subgroup generated by the sequence (gn)n∈N. We claim that this map is Borel. If
U ⊆G is open, then f((gn)n∈N) ∩U ̸= ∅if and only if ⟨gn⟩n∈N ∩U ̸= ∅, which
occurs if and only if there is a word in finitely many of the gn’s which is in U; this
is an open condition. Likewise, if K ⊆G is compact, then f((gn)n∈N) ∩K = ∅if
and only if there is a rational r > 0 such that for every word in any finite number of
the gn’s, that word is at least distance r away from K (with respect to some fixed
compatible metric on G), a Borel condition. This shows that the inverse images
under f of the subbasic open sets generating the Chabauty–Fell topology on F(G)
are Borel in GN, and hence that f is Borel. Note that f−1[D(G)] is exactly the set
of sequences in GN which generate discrete groups.
Let G<∞be the (Borel) subset of GN consisting of all sequences in G which are
eventually equal to the identity. Then, Dfg(G) = f[G<∞∩f −1[D(G)]]. Since D(G)
is Borel in F(G) and f is countable-to-one when restricted to G<∞∩f −1[D(G)], it
follows by Luzin–Novikov uniformization (see Section 2.1) that Dfg(G) is Borel.
□
Our next lemma concerns the following general notion: given Borel equivalence
relations E and F, a Borel homomorphism f from E to F is a weak Borel reduction22
if the preimage of each F-class under f contains only countably many E-classes.
In this case, we say that E is weakly Borel reducible to F.
Lemma 6.9. Suppose that E and F are essentially countable Borel equivalence
relations and that E is weakly Borel reducible to F.
1. If F is concretely classifiable, then so is E.
2. If F is essentially hyperfinite, then so is E.
Proof. For the first assertion, it suffices to consider the case when f is a weak Borel
reduction of E to =Y on some standard Borel space Y . Since E is essentially count-
able, there is a countable Borel equivalence relation E′ which is Borel bireducible
with E. Let g be a Borel reduction from E′ to E. Then, f ◦g is a weak Borel
reduction of E′ to =Y , hence by [Tho09a, Lem. 2.1], E′ is concretely classifiable.
But E′ is bireducible with E, so E is concretely classifiable as well.
For the second assertion, observe first that a subequivalence relation of a hyper-
finite relation is hyperfinite. Next, [Tho09b, Thm. 4.4] tells us that if a countable
Borel equivalence relation E′ weakly reduces to E0, then E′ ⊆R for some countable
Borel equivalence relation R ≤B E0; since the latter is equivalent to the hyperfinite-
ness of R, it follows then that E′ ≤B E0 as well. Proceed now as above: fix a weak
Borel reduction f of an essentially countable Borel equivalence relation E to E0
along with a witness g : E′ →E of one side of the bireducibility of a countable
22The notion of a weak Borel reduction is usually reserved for countable Borel equivalence
relations, where it is equivalent to being a countable-to-one homomorphism.

70
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Borel equivalence relation E′ with E. As before, f ◦g is a weak Borel reduction of
E′ to E0, hence E′ ≤B E0, and thus E ≤B E0 as well.
□
Focusing next on Dfg(G), we have the following variation on Lemma 6.4.
Lemma 6.10. Let G be a locally compact Polish group, N a finite normal subgroup
of G, and π : G →G/N the quotient map.
1. The inverse image map π−1, when restricted to Dfg(G/N), is an injective
Borel reduction from E(G/N, Dfg(G/N)) to E(G, Dfg(G)).
2. The direct image map π is a Borel homomorphism from E(G, D(G)) to
E(G/N, D(G/N)). Moreover, when restricted to Dfg(G), this map is a weak
Borel reduction of E(G, Dfg(G)) to E(G/N, Dfg(G/N)). Consequently, if
E(G/N, Dfg(G/N)) is concretely classifiable or essentially hyperfinite, re-
spectively, then so is E(G, Dfg(G)).
Proof. Part (1) is proved exactly like Lemma 6.4, with π−1 restricted to Dfg(G/N).
Note that if Γ ≤G/N is generated by {g0N, . . . , gnN}, then π−1[Γ] is generated
by {g0, . . . , gn} ∪N, so this map preserves finite generation.
For (2), clearly if Γ ≤G is finitely generated, then so is π[Γ]. We must, however,
show that the direct image preserves discreteness. Suppose that Γ ≤G is discrete,
but that π[Γ] is not. Then, there is a sequence (gnN)n∈N in π[Γ], each gn ∈Γ,
converging to the identity eN, but such that no gnN = eN, that is, no gn ∈N.
G/N is locally compact, so we may assume that all of the gnN’s are in some compact
neighborhood of the identity. As N is finite, the quotient map is proper, so the
gn’s all lie in some compact set in G. Thus, there is a subsequence (gnℓ)ℓ∈N which
converges, necessarily to some k ∈N. But Γ is discrete, so these gnℓ’s must be
ultimately equal to k, and thus gnℓN is ultimately equal to eN, a contradiction.
This shows that the direct image map is well-defined D(G) →D(G/N), and it is
clearly a Borel homomorphism.
To see that the direct image map is a weak reduction when restricted to Dfg(G),
suppose that ∆∈Dfg(G) has generators d1, . . . , dm and ∆′ ∈Dfg(G) is such that
π[∆] and π[∆′] are conjugate in G/N. That is, there is some g ∈G such that
gπ[∆]g−1N = gNπ[∆]g−1N = π[∆′].
It follows that ∆′ is generated by some subset of g{dik | k ∈N, i ≤m}g−1.
Thus, every subgroup of G which is mapped to one conjugate to π[∆] in G/N
must be conjugate in G to one generated by a subset of {dik | k ∈N, i ≤m}, of
which there are only finitely many. Hence, π is finite-to-one on conjugacy classes,
and in particular, a weak reduction.
□
The final lemma of this section can be used to transfer complexity results between
Isom+(Hn) and Isom(Hn).
Lemma 6.11. Suppose that G is a locally compact Polish group, G0 a closed normal
subgroup of index 2, and i ∈Z(G) an element of order 2 such that G = G0 ⊔iG0.
Then:
1. E(G, D(G)) and E(G0, D(G0)) are Borel bireducible.
2. E(G, Dfg(G)) and E(G0, Dfg(G0)) are Borel bireducible.
Proof. Suppose that H and K are subgroups of G0. If H and K are conjugate by
some element of G0, they are conjugate in G. Conversely, if H and K are conjugate

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
71
in G, say by some element g, then either g is in G0 or ig is. In the latter case, since
i is central,
(ig)H(ig)−1 = igHg−1i−1 = gHg−1ii−1 = K.
Thus, the inclusion map from subgroups of G0 to subgroups of G is a reduction of
conjugacy.
Next, consider the map which takes a subgroup H of G to its intersection with
G0, call it H0. Note that H = H0 ⊔iH0. If H and K are conjugate subgroups of
G, say K = gHg−1 for some g ∈G, then
K0 ⊔iK0 = K = gHg−1 = g(H0 ⊔iH0)g−1 = gH0g−1 ⊔i(gH0g−1),
so K0 = gH0g−1. Conversely, if H0 and K0 are conjugate by some h ∈G0, then
hHh−1 = h(H0 ⊔iH0)h−1 = hH0h−1 ⊔i(hH0h−1) = K0 ⊔iK0 = K.
Thus, the mapping H 7→H0 is a reduction of conjugacy as well.
Clearly, both of these mappings preserve discreteness, proving (1). The inclusion
map preserves finite generation trivially, while the mapping H 7→H0 preserves finite
generation by Schreier’s Lemma [Rot95, Lem. 7.56], proving (2).
□
7. The conjugacy problem for finitely generated Fuchsian groups
In this section and its sequel, we return to hyperbolic manifolds as our primary
objects of study.
A main motivation as we proceed, though, derives from the
group-theoretic analyses of the previous section; this is the question what is the
Borel complexity of the conjugacy relation for finitely generated discrete subgroups
of a Lie group? For it is among the effects of the extensions, in Theorem 6.2 and
Corollary 6.5, of the results of [ACH01] from countable to Lie group settings to
render the conjugacy relation on finitely generated discrete subgroups descriptive
set-theoretically interesting in ways which it had not been before. More precisely,
although a countable group may have uncountably many discrete subgroups, only
countably many of those are finitely generated, and hence the conjugacy relation
on the latter collection trivially reduces to =N. In contrast, moduli spaces M(S)
of finite-type hyperbolic surfaces S may be regarded as witnessing that both of the
Lie groups PSL(2, R) and PSL(2, C) possess uncountably many conjugacy classes of
finitely generated discrete subgroups (see the discussions preceding Lemma 7.5 and
in and just after Theorem 8.13 below), and it is therefore natural to ask how much
of the complexity attested by Theorem 6.6 manifests on these families of subgroups
alone.
The latter two groups correspond to the n = 2 and n = 3 instances of Isom+(Hn);
recall that in Corollary 6.7, we applied our analysis of the conjugacy relation for
discrete torsion-free subgroups of each to draw conclusions about the complexity of
the isometry relation for the associated classes of hyperbolic n-manifolds. Below,
these roles will be reversed: it will tend to be classification results on the mani-
fold side, together with the translation mechanisms of Section 5, which underwrite
our complexity computations on the group side. These results leverage deep and
specific geometric phenomena, leaving the question of the complexity of the conju-
gacy relation on finitely generated discrete subgroups for other Lie or even locally
compact Polish groups very generally open; see our conclusion’s Question 17. A
related distinction of these results is their encompassing the most general of man-
ifold finiteness conditions which one might invoke, namely the fifth of those which
we now review.

72
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Definition 7.1. Fix n ≥2 and let M = Hn/Γ be the quotient hyperbolic manifold
associated to a torsion-free discrete subgroup Γ of Isom(Hn). The convex core of
M is the smallest convex submanifold of M whose inclusion into M is a homotopy
equivalence.
i. M is closed, and Γ is cocompact, if M is compact and without boundary.
ii. M is of finite volume, and Γ is a lattice, if vol(M) < ∞.
iii. M and Γ are geometrically finite if Γ is finitely generated and the convex
core of M is of finite volume.23
iv. M is topologically finite or tame if M is homeomorphic to the interior of a
compact manifold, possibly with boundary.
v. M is algebraically finite if Γ is finitely generated.
These conditions are of (weakly) decreasing stringency; in the case of n = 2,
for example, we have (i) ⇒(ii) ⇒(iii) ⇔(iv) ⇔(v), with neither of the first
two implications reversible [MT98, Theorem 0.8]. For each n, in other words, the
classes of n-manifolds associated to these conditions are nondecreasing in size, and
in this and the next section we will compute, in dimensions 2 and 3, respectively,
the complexity of the isometry relation on each of them. In this section, our main
result is the following:
Theorem 7.2. The orientation-preserving isometry problem for any of the classes
(i) through (v) of complete orientable hyperbolic 2-manifolds listed above is Borel
bireducible with the relation =R of equality on the real numbers. In particular, the
classification of
1. finitely generated torsion-free discrete subgroups of PSL(2, R) up to conju-
gacy, or, equivalently, of
2. algebraically finite complete orientable hyperbolic 2-manifolds up to orienta-
tion-preserving isometry
is Borel bireducible with =R.
Via the lemmas of Section 6, this analysis readily extends from the finitely gen-
erated torsion-free discrete subgroups of G = PSL(2, R) to those of G = SL(2, R),
and of G = Isom(Hn), results we record in this section’s conclusion. Each strength-
ens the relevant instance of Stuck and Zimmer’s result that for any Lie group G,
the conjugacy problem for lattices in G is concretely classifiable [SZ94, Corollary
3.2], to the exact degree that condition (v) above strengthens condition (ii); see
Theorem 8.5 for a parallel strengthening (from (ii) to (iv)) in the G = PSL(2, C)
setting below.
For now, however, for concision we let G = PSL(2, R) and recall that a Fuchsian
group is simply an element of D(G). We also fix the notations Dcc(G), Dfv(G),
Dgf(G), and Daf(G) for the subspaces of Dtf(G) corresponding to the group finite-
ness conditions (i), (ii), (iii), and (v), respectively, in Definition 7.1 above. In the
notation of previous sections, then, Theorem 7.2 then conjoins four basic claims:
(a) =R ≤B E(G, Dcc(G)),
(b) Dcc(G), Dfv(G), and Dgf(G) = Daf(G) form a ⊆-increasing chain of conjuga-
tion-invariant Borel subsets of D(G),
(c) E(G, Daf(G)) ≤B =R, and
23For equivalent definitions of geometric finiteness in dimension 3, see [Mar16, p. 145]; for
more general discussion, see [Kap08, §2], [Bow93], [Rat19, §12].

(d) items (a) through (c) together entail the naturally associated claims for

The arguments of items (a) and (c) are more interesting; for these, we will require
a few definitions. We call a surface hyperbolizable if it admits a complete hyperbolic
24One could even, for a third proof, deduce item (c) from Theorem 8.13 below.

metric. We call it finite-type if it’s homeomorphic to a closed orientable surface with
finitely many points removed; this includes, of course, the possibility that no points
have been removed at all. Note (again by [CK70], applied either to the natural
compactifications or coupled with the n-transitivity of manifold homeomorphism
groups for all n > 0) that there are only countably many homeomorphism classes
of finite-type hyperbolizable surfaces. Let F denote some fixed collection of unique
representatives of such classes and let C collect the closed surfaces in F. Recall
that a group Γ acts properly discontinuously on a locally compact space X if the
set {g ∈Γ | gK ∩K ̸= ∅} is finite for every compact K ⊆X. For us the primary
significance of this condition is that the quotient of a topological manifold by a
properly discontinuous action is a Polish space (more particularly, it is an orbifold;
see [Thu22a, Prop. 13.2.1]). The following semiformal definition will suffice for our
purposes (we forego, for example, defining the relevant topologies; for details, see,
e.g., [Abi80] or [FM12]).
Definition 7.4. Let S be a finite-type hyperbolizable surface. Identify S with the
interior of a smooth compact manifold ˆS whose boundary circles, if any, correspond
to the punctures of ˆS. Write Diff+(S) for the group of orientation-preserving dif-
feomorphisms of ˆS with itself which restrict to the identity on ∂ˆS and Diff0(S) for
its subgroup of elements which are isotopic to the identity. The group Diff+(S) acts
on the space of hyperbolic metrics on S by pullback; we have thus the Teichmüller
space
Teich(S) = {finite-volume hyperbolic metrics on S}/Diff0(S),
mapping class group
MCG(S) = Diff+(S)/Diff0(S),
and moduli space
M(S) = Teich(S)/MCG(S)
of S.
For us the essential points are then as follows:
• If S is given by n punctures of a closed genus-g surface then Teich(S) carries
a real analytic structure, of dimension 6g + 2n −6, with respect to which
the natural action of MCG(S) is properly discontinuous [Mar16, 2.10]. In
consequence, M(S) is a Polish space, and it is uncountable in all cases but
one, namely when g = 0 and n = 3.
• In Section 8, we’ll leverage a higher analogue of an alternative presenta-
tion of Teich(S) as the quotient by the G-conjugacy action of a space of
discrete faithful representations π1(S) →G. In this alternative presenta-
tion, the natural view is of Teich(S) and M(S) as spaces of marked and
unmarked groups, respectively, up to conjugacy; in that of Definition 7.4,
it is of Teich(S) and M(S) as spaces of marked and unmarked hyperbolic
manifolds, respectively, up to isometry.
Here we’ll privilege the latter view; by the results of Section 5, though, it’s clear in
either case that `

on the pretext of a more refined calculation, we take a detour that will be useful to
us in our second proof of the theorem’s remainder as well.
What we will argue is that q−1(M(S)) is, for any S ∈F, a relatively Gδ subset
of Dfv(G); it is, in other words, an intersection of Dfv(G) with countably many
open subsets of D(G). We will do so via a more geometric rendering of the space
D(G) described in [BLL21], which we briefly now review. Fix a unit vector w in the
tangent bundle TH2 of H2. Any Γ ∈Dtf(G) then determines a vectored oriented
hyperbolic 2-manifold (X, v) in which X is the quotient of H2 by the natural action
of Γ and v is the associated image of w in TX. (We exclude torsion for simplicity:
the equivalence we’re recording is a special case of that of D(G) with vectored
orbifolds, as in [BLL21, §3.4]; see Remark 7.6 below.) Quoting now from [BLL21,
p. 23]:
There is a natural smooth topology on the set of isometry classes of
vectored oriented Riemannian [manifolds], where (Xi, vi) →(X∞, v∞)
if there is a sequence ri →∞and smooth embeddings

The key point for our purposes is then [BLL21, Proposition 3.4.3], which might here
be rendered as: any choice of w as above induces a homeomorphism of the space
Dtf(G) with the space of vectored oriented hyperbolic 2-manifolds endowed with the
smooth topology. We denote some fixed choice of such a homeomorphism by h.
Intuitively, a sequence of vectored manifolds (Xi, vi) as above converges to
(X∞, v∞) if their vi-neighborhoods “look more and more like” those of v∞, in a
sense quantified by the increasing sequence of real numbers ri. Fixing next a com-
pact oriented surface Z (possibly with boundary) and r > 0, it is straightforward
to see that, with respect to this topology,
U(r, Z) := {(X, v) | there exists a Y homeomorphic to Z with BX(p, r) ⊆Y ⊆X}
is open (here, as above, p denotes the basepoint of v and BX(p, r) denotes the
radius-r open ball about it in X). More precisely, suppose for contradiction that
a sequence (Xi, vi) in the complement of U(r, Z) converges to an (X∞, v∞) ∈
U(r, Z), as witnessed by a sequence (ri) and submanifold Y ⊆X∞, respectively. By
compactness, Y ⊆BX∞(p∞, ri) for some i, and it follows that for j ≥i sufficiently
large, (Xj, vj) ∈U(r, Z) as well, the contradiction desired.
It follows immediately that if S ∈F is closed, then for sufficiently large r,
