Remark 7.6. We were convinced of Theorem 7.2 long before we knew quite how
we would argue it, and it was in the course of related discussions that Ferrán Valdez
directed our attention to Theorem 4.1.1 of [BLL21], which may be stated as follows.
Let Do
fv(G) = {Γ ∈D(G) | H2/Γ has finite volume} so that, much as above, any
Γ ∈Do
fv(G) determines a vectored hyperbolic 2-orbifold (X, v) with image [X] in the
associated finite-volume orbifold moduli space ˆ
M(S). Denote the map (X, v) 7→[X]
by ˆq; then: for any orbifold S arising in the above fashion, the restriction of ˆq to
ˆq−1( ˆ
M(S)) is a fiber orbibundle whose regular fibers are homeomorphic to T 1S;
moreover, ˆq−1( ˆ
M(S)) is a 6g + 2(k + l) −3 dimensional manifold, where g, k, and
l record the genus and numbers of cusps and cone points, respectively, of S. Note
the two differences from the setting of Theorem 7.2: the restriction to Dfv(G) on
the one hand, and an expanded attention to groups Γ possibly possessing torsion
on the other. It seems clear upon inspection that Theorem 7.2 may be extended
to subsume the latter possibility as well; as noted, we have foregone this extension
simply because its requirements — definitions, parametrizations, and moduli spaces
of 2-orbifolds — would take us too far afield.
Proof 2 of Theorem 7.2 (sketch). Again we need only to prove item (c), namely
that the relation E(G, Daf(G)) is concretely classifiable, and we will apply to this
end Glimm’s theorem that an orbit equivalence relation is concretely classifiable if
and only if any orbit is open in its closure ([Gli61, Theorem 1]; more precisely, what
we require is the theorem’s implication (1)⇒(3). Note that for this implication, the
theorem’s requirement that the domain be locally compact (as Daf(G) in general
is not) is superfluous). Here it’s again valuable to consider these orbits in their
materializations as families of vectored hyperbolic 2-manifolds (X, v), for fixing
any one of them allows us to then view conjugations by elements of G as simply
relocating and reorienting the unit vector v within TX. It is easy to see, either
directly or by the aforementioned [SZ94, Corollary 3.2], that if this X is compact
then its associated orbit is closed; the more interesting case is when X possesses
ends which the vectors v may, in the above framing, “exit”. More precisely, in the
interesting case X is the union of a compact bounded surface X′, in which its genus
concentrates, together with the finitely many components of X\X′ comprising its
ends; (Y, w) is then in the closure of the orbit of X if there exist a sequence of
unit vectors vi ∈TX with basepoints pi ∈X along with reals ri →∞and maps
ψi : BX(pi, ri) →Y sending each vi to w whose images converge to the metric on
Y . There are two nontrivial possibilities. In the first, these vi exit a flare; here,
if the ri grow sufficiently slowly, convergence is to a vectored Y = H2, simply,
corresponding to the discrete group {I}. In fact, this is the only exit of a flare
possible, and if the vi remain within bounded distance of X′ then Y is isometric
to X, a point equally applying when the end in question is a cusp, which brings us

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
79
to the second nontrivial possibility. This is when the sequence vi exits a parabolic
end. Assume first that these vi all point out of the cusp, and that the fixed points
of the parabolic elements associated to this cusp converge to ∞∈ˆR: then it is not
too hard to see that the limit of the associated Γi is in fact the non-discrete group
{z 7→z + t | t ∈R} ∈S(G). Rotations of those vi will, on a subsequence, converge
to some fixed angle θ ∈2π with the geodesics from their basepoints straight out
the cusp, and the associated Γi will converge to a conjugate of {z 7→z + t | t ∈R}
in S(G) with fixed point in ˆR at an angle θ from ∞. Hence the closure in S(G) of
the orbit O associated to a noncompact finite-type (X, v) is the union of O with,
topologically, a finite number of points and circles; it follows that O is open therein,
and this concludes the proof.
□
These themes of sorting and “exiting” ends, of mischief around cusps, and of
limits of surface structures all play fundamental roles in the classification of hyper-
bolic 3-manifolds, as we’ll soon see. We conclude this section with the following
easy corollary of Theorem 7.2.
Corollary 7.7. The classification of complete algebraically finite hyperbolic 2-
manifolds up to isometry is Borel bireducible with the relation =R of equality on
the real numbers, as is the classification of finitely generated torsion-free discrete
subgroups of PGL(2, R) or SL(2, R) up to conjugacy.
Proof. The first of these assertions records the upgrade, via Lemma 6.11(2), from
a complexity computation for the orientation-preserving isometry relation on the
algebraically finite parameters of C∗
c(Isom+, H2) to that on the algebraically finite
parameters of C∗
c(Isom, H2).
By Corollary 5.7 and the topological isomorphism
PGL(2, R) ∼= Isom(H2), this also gives the first half of the second assertion. For
the latter’s second half, apply Lemma 6.10(2) to Theorem 7.2(1).
□
8. The conjugacy problem for finitely generated Kleinian groups
In this section, we establish, alongside a number of subsidiary propositions, the
results on finitely generated Kleinian groups and hyperbolic 3-manifolds which ap-
pear as our introduction’s Theorems K and L. Several of these results may be argued
in more than one way. Below, we will often begin by outlining the most heuristic
argument, in part as a way of introducing the relevant objects, before turning to
more formal, and economical, statements and proofs of subsections’ main results.
We will also, in parallel with Section 7, broadly proceed according to the hierarchy
of finiteness conditions described in Definition 7.1 therein.
8.1. Overview. A Kleinian group Γ is a discrete subgroup of PSL(2, C). If Γ is
torsion-free then the quotient of H3 by the natural action of Γ is a hyperbolic 3-
manifold.
More broadly, the n = 3 instance of Corollary 5.7 describes a Borel
correspondence between torsion-free Kleinian groups and complete orientable hy-
perbolic 3-manifolds, by way of the standard identification (again via equation (6))
of PSL(2, C) with the group Möb(ˆC) of all Möbius transformations of the Riemann
sphere ˆC. This is because the natural identification of the latter with the boundary
of the open unit ball model of hyperbolic space H3 in turn induces, via canonical
extensions of these transformations, an isomorphism of Möb(ˆC) with Isom+(H3).
The rapport at work here between the geometrically unbounded ball model of H3
and its topological boundary (or sphere at infinity) will be critical in what follows,

80
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
as will the sorting of non-identity elements of Isom+(H3) into the three classes par-
abolic, loxodromic, or elliptic according to whether they fix 1, 2, or infinitely many
points in H3 ∪ˆC, respectively; see [Ser05] for an efficient review of this material
which aligns well with this section’s main concerns. Main book-length treatments
include [MT98, Kap09, Mar16] (see also [MSW02]); each abundantly attests the
richness of this section’s main subject, namely finitely generated Kleinian groups,
and the PSL(2, C)-conjugacy relations among them.
Our analysis will draw heavily on the manifold side of this story; let us therefore
begin by reviewing its role in Section 7.
At the heart of that section were the
Teichmüller and moduli spaces T (S) and M(S), respectively, of a hyperbolizable
surface S. These are, intuitively, the spaces of marked and unmarked hyperbolic
structures on S, notions we’ll make more precise below. Mediating between them
is the mapping class group MCG(S) of S; M(S) is, more precisely, the space of
orbits of a properly discontinuous action of MCG(S) on T (S). In the most clean
and classical setting, S is a closed oriented surface of finite genus g, punctured in
some finite number n of places, and T (S) and M(S) each parametrize finite-volume
hyperbolic structures on S; as noted, each in this case is a Polish space of dimension
6g + 2n −6. Our interest was wider than this, though, extending, as it did, to the
possibly infinite volume geometrically finite (or, equivalently, algebraically finite)
structures on such S. But the classification of such structures (weakly) reduces to
the finite-volume case, and this was the crux of our main proof of Theorem 7.2.
Lastly, let us note for future reference the close relationship between Out(π1(S))
and MCG(S); by the Dehn–Nielsen–Baer Theorem, for example, the latter is an
index-2 subgroup of the former if S is closed (see [FM12, Ch. 8]).
Call the material of the preceding paragraph the 2-dimensional template; much
of what follows can be understood in terms of how it both persists and breaks down
in 3 hyperbolic dimensions. To begin with, the class of geometrically finite hyper-
bolic 3-manifolds no longer coincides with that of the algebraically finite ones, and
it is the former which now figures as the tractable class, the one whose elements are
classifiable by points in classical Teichmüller and moduli spaces. Elements of the
latter class, on the other hand, are in a natural sense limits of elements of the for-
mer; this is the celebrated Density Conjecture of Bers, Sullivan, and Thurston, fully
rendered a theorem in [Ohs11, NS12]. Heuristically, this carries the consequence
that elements of the latter admit classification by limits of hyperbolic surface struc-
tures, and this is the loose meaning of the eponymous ending lamination in the
following landmark theorem.
The Ending Lamination Theorem ([Min10, BCM12]). A hyperbolic 3-manifold
with finitely generated fundamental group is uniquely determined by its topological
type and its end invariants.
Variations of the theorem will appear below; the form recorded here is Brock,
Canary, and Minsky’s rendering of what had been known since Thurston’s sem-
inal [Thu82] as the Ending Lamination Conjecture. (Valuable further references
for this large subject include [Min03a], [Min03b], [Mar16], and [Bow23], with the
latter, in particular, supplying portions of the proof of the conjecture unrecorded in
[Min10, BCM12].) For us the most immediate point is that the theorem’s classifying
pair (homeomorphism type, ending invariants) is naturally construed as an element

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
81
of a Polish space, as will soon grow clear.25 Thus, much as for the main results of
Sections 4, 5, and 7, a major theorem of low-dimensional topology describes, from
our perspective, a reduction — one moreover which, if Borel, would appear to im-
mediately settle the manifold framing of this section’s guiding question: What is the
Borel complexity of the classification of algebraically finite hyperbolic 3-manifolds
up to isometry?
But here we should tread carefully.
For upon closer inspection, the theorem
describes a classification of marked manifolds — or, equivalently, on the group side,
of representations; its more careful formulation in [BCM12, p. 3], for example, is
as follows (only the theorem’s broader contours are presently the point; its more
technical content will clarify as this section unfolds):
The Ending Lamination Theorem (the case of incompressible ends). Let Γ be a
finitely generated torsion-free non-abelian group, let ρ : Γ →PSL(2, C) be a discrete
faithful representation, and let N 0
ρ denote the hyperbolic manifold H3/ρ(Γ) minus a
standard open neighborhood of whatever cusps it may possess. Suppose further that
each of the ends of N 0
ρ is incompressible. Then the representation ρ is determined
up to conjugacy in PSL(2, C) by the marked homeomorphism type of the relative
compact core, and the end invariants, of N 0
ρ.
The points to highlight here are, first, that the theorem’s objects are discrete
faithful representations of a fixed finitely generated 3-manifold group Γ. It will
accordingly be convenient to conduct this section’s complexity analyses — whether
of Kleinian groups or of hyperbolic manifolds — in parallel with this machinery,
that is, to segment our work into analyses of those groups isomorphic to some fixed
such Γ, or, on the manifold side, homotopy equivalent to some fixed such H3/ρ(Γ).
Since there are only countably many such groups (by [Sco73]), and the isomorphism
relation between them is plainly analytic, this practice is, from a Borel complexity
perspective, an innocuous one: it will amount on either the group or manifold side
to localizing attention to an element of a partition of our parameter space into
countably many Borel subsets. The details of the relevant arguments are by now
sufficiently routine that we won’t belabor them here or below.
Second, as noted, the theorem classifies discrete faithful representations in
PSL(2, C), or what we may think of as marked Kleinian groups, up to conjugacy.
Classifying unmarked Kleinian groups isomorphic to Γ up to conjugacy, or un-
marked hyperbolic 3-manifolds homotopy equivalent to H3/ρ(Γ) up to isometry,
then amounts to forgetting the marking, or, more mathematically, to quotienting
by a natural action of Out(Γ), much as in the 2-dimensional template. In these
contexts, however, unlike in the 2-dimensional setting, this action is not in general
properly discontinuous, and it is here that the story grows interesting.
It is time to be more precise. To that end, fix a finitely generated fundamental
group Γ of an orientable hyperbolic 3-manifold and consider the following spaces:
• D[Γ] = {Γ′ ∈D(PSL(2, C)) | Γ′ ∼= Γ}. By the reasoning above, D[Γ] is a
Borel subset of Daf(PSL(2, C)) and, thus, itself is a standard Borel space.
25Note for comparison that the Kerékjártó–Richards classification of surfaces in Section 4 was
in terms of how discrete data (orientability, planarity) arrayed on elements of the uncountable
Polish space K(NN) of end-configurations. In contrast, in the present section, where only finitely-
ended manifolds are under consideration, the concern will be for how elements of uncountable
Polish spaces of data (ends’ asymptotic geometries) array on elements of discrete spaces of end-
configurations.

82
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
• DF(Γ) = {ρ : Γ →PSL(2, C) | ρ is discrete and faithful}, topologized as a
subspace of Hom(Γ, PSL(2, C)) endowed with the compact-open topology
(it is closed therein [Jør76]). For an equivalent description, fix some n gener-
ators gi of Γ and view DF(Γ) as a subspace of the product space PSL(2, C)n
via the identification ρ 7→(ρ(g1), . . . , ρ(gn)). Thus DF(Γ) may be regarded
as a space of marked Kleinian groups which are group-isomorphic to Γ.
• AH(Γ) is the space of orbits of the conjugacy action of PSL(2, C) on DF(Γ)
given by (h · ρ)(g) = hρ(g)h−1.
Connecting these spaces are natural maps p : DF(Γ) →D[Γ] and q : DF(Γ) →
AH(Γ).
Lemma 8.1. The map p is Borel, the map q is continuous, and if Γ is nonabelian
then AH(Γ) is Polish.
Proof. The sets UV = {Γ′ ∈D[Γ] | Γ′ ∩V ̸= ∅}, with V ranging over the open
subsets of PSL(2, C), generate the Borel σ-algebra of D[Γ]. Thus to show p Borel
measurable, it suffices to show that the sets p−1[UV ] = {ρ ∈DF(Γ) | im(ρ)∩V ̸= ∅}
are Borel.
In fact they’re open, for if ρ(g) ∈V for some word gi(0) · · · gi(k) in
the generators of Γ then the product of sufficiently small neighborhoods of those
generators will fall within V as well.
The map q is continuous by definition. The more remarkable fact is that, since
discrete and faithful PSL(2, C)-representations of nonabelian torsion-free groups are
nonelementary (see the discussion preceding Lemma 8.7 below for a definition), AH
of a nonabelian Γ forms a closed subset of the character variety, and in fact smooth
complex manifold, denoted as R0(Γ, PSL(2, C)) in [Kap09] (see its sections 4.3, 8.1,
and 8.8 for details; the key point is that the discrete and faithful representations
of such a Γ are necessarily nonradical in the sense of its p. 62). Our conclusion
follows.
□
Two more objects round out the picture; since our main question revolves less
around the Borel structures they possess than those they might admit, we regard
them for the moment simply as sets:
• C(Γ) is the quotient of D[Γ] by E(PSL(2, C), D[Γ]), or in other words by
the natural conjugacy action of PSL(2, C).
• E(Γ) is the collection of complete invariants (homeomorphism type, ending
invariants) which, by the Ending Lamination Theorem, classify the points
of AH(Γ).
We have then a bijection e : AH(Γ) →E(Γ), as well as natural set maps u : D[Γ] →
C(Γ) and v : AH(Γ) →C(Γ), with the latter the quotient by the ζ of the following
lemma.
Lemma 8.2. Writing ζ for the natural action of Out(Γ) on AH(Γ), C(Γ) is
isomorphic to the set of ζ-orbits in AH(Γ); moreover, if Γ is nonabelian, then
E(PSL(2, C), D[Γ]) and the orbit equivalence relation Eζ induced by ζ are Borel
bireducible.
Proof. The natural action of Aut(Γ) on DF(Γ) descends to one on AH(Γ); Inn(Γ)
falls within the kernel of the latter, and it is the induced action of Out(Γ) =
Aut(Γ)/Inn(Γ) which we are denoting by ζ. The composition up forgets first the
marking, then the representative, of a conjugacy class, while vq forgets them in
the opposite order, but the effect is the same; more precisely, the main square in

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
83
the diagram just below commutes. For a Borel reduction of E(PSL(2, C), D[Γ])
to Eζ, note that since Γ is finitely generated, Aut(Γ) is countable, hence Luzin–
Novikov uniformization furnishes a Borel right-inverse s : D[Γ] →DF(Γ) to p;
by the aforementioned commutativity, qs : D[Γ] →AH(Γ) is then a reduction as
desired. For a Borel reduction of Eζ to E(PSL(2, C), D[Γ]), note that Lemma 8.1
together with [Kec95, Exer. 18.20(iii)] furnishes a Borel right-inverse t to q; just as
above, pt : AH(Γ) →D[Γ] is then a reduction as desired.
□

Recall now the view of DF(Γ) as a space of marked Kleinian groups isomorphic
to Γ. Next, regard Γ as the fundamental group of some fixed complete hyperbolic
3-manifold N; DF(Γ) may then also be viewed as the space of marked complete
hyperbolic 3-manifolds Mρ which are homotopy equivalent to N. Here Mρ is what in
the notation of Theorem 5.4 would be denoted Mσ(im(ρ)), but now the isomorphism
ρ : π1(N) →im(ρ) encodes something further as well: by Whitehead’s Theorem
[Hat02, Theorem 4.5, Prop. 1B.9], it encodes the homotopy class of a homotopy
equivalence N →Mρ, and it is this that we take as our definition of a marking of
Mρ. Note that the passage between these two perspectives requires nothing more
than the machinery of Section 5. In particular, it is Borel, and PSL(2, C)-conjugacy
on the group side continues to correspond to orientation-preserving isometry on the
manifold side; formal details are left to the reader. With the aid of a lemma from
Section 8.2, we are now in a position to prove the following.
Proposition 8.3. Fix a complete orientable hyperbolic 3-manifold N with finitely
generated π1(N).
The classification of marked complete hyperbolic 3-manifolds
homotopy equivalent to N up to marked orientation-preserving isometry is Borel
reducible to =R; so, thus, is the classification of all marked complete hyperbolic
3-manifolds with finitely generated fundamental group.
Proof. Since the concluding classification problem amounts to a countable sum of
those of the first sort, it will suffice to prove the first claim. By our discussion just
above, our task is to show that the PSL(2, C)-conjugacy relation on DF(π1(N))
Borel reduces to the equality relation on a Polish space. By Lemma 8.1, if π1(N)
is nonabelian then q : DF(π1(N)) →AH(π1(N)) is such a reduction. If, on the
other hand, π1(N) is abelian, then its discrete faithful images in PSL(2, C) are
elementary; for a definition, see the discussion preceding Lemma 8.7, whereupon
the latter furnishes us with a Borel reduction of PSL(2, C)-conjugacy on D[π1(N)]
to =C. Precomposing this with the map p : DF(π1(N)) →D[π1(N)] then defines a
weak reduction of PSL(2, C)-conjugacy on DF(π1(N)) to =C, and this, by Lemma
6.9, completes the argument.
□

84
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
On display here is, arguably, a limitation of the invariant descriptive set theoretic
framework: its failure to distinguish between abstract and more “meaningful” so-
lutions to classification problems. The essential content of Proposition 8.3, namely
that marked algebraically finite hyperbolic 3-manifolds naturally, but somewhat ab-
stractly, correspond up to marked isometry to points in a Polish space AH(π1(M))
has been known for a long time. But it was only with the provision, via the End-
ing Lamination Theorem, of more geometrically meaningful complete invariants for
such M that their classification problem was generally accredited as solved.
As noted in Section 2.2, however, anti-classification results derive much of their
force from precisely this indiscriminancy. And it is results of this latter sort which
form this section’s main contribution. In contrast with both Proposition 8.3 and
the Ending Lamination Theorem, we will show not only that the most obvious
assignment of complete invariants to fundamental classes of unmarked hyperbolic
3-manifolds fails to take values in any Polish space, but that every Borel assignment
must fail to. More precisely:
Theorem 8.4. Let S be a closed orientable hyperbolizable surface. The classifi-
cation up to isometry of doubly degenerate hyperbolic manifolds homeomorphic to
S × R is Borel equivalent to E0. In particular, there exists no Borel assignment
of complete numerical invariants, up to PSL(2, C)-conjugacy, to the elements of
D[π1(S)].
The theorem supplies the third item of the following.
Theorem 8.5. The Borel complexity degrees of the isometry problem for the major
finiteness classes of complete hyperbolic 3-manifolds are as follows:
• for finite-volume manifolds, it is =N;
• for geometrically finite manifolds, it is =R;
• for algebraically finite manifolds, it is at least E0.
Corresponding results hold for the PSL(2, C)-conjugacy relation on the correspond-
ing classes of Kleinian groups.
Thus, intriguingly, we see both a rise and a fall in the complexity of isometry
problem for classes of hyperbolic manifolds as we pass from 2 dimensions to 3
(compare Theorem 7.2). We will argue Theorem 8.5’s three items in order in the
following three sections, recording a number of related results along the way.
8.2. Classifying the basics. To better suggest the scope of the Ending Lamina-
tion Theorem, it is useful to first consider the latter in a few restricted contexts; let
us proceed according to the hierarchy of finiteness conditions recorded in Definition
7.1. Applied to item (i) of these, for example, the Ending Lamination Theorem
tells us that homeomorphism type alone is a complete invariant for closed hyper-
bolic 3-manifolds up to isometry, since the latter have no ends; as such, it recovers
the dimension 3 instance of Mostow’s Rigidity Theorem [Mos68].26
Within our
framework, the corollary that the classification of closed hyperbolic 3-manifolds up
to isometry is Borel bireducible with =N is then immediate, by [CK70], much as it
was for Corollary 3.24. Turning next to item (ii), finite-volume complete hyper-
bolic n-manifolds, we know from Lemma 7.3 that they define a Borel subspace of
26The theorem’s more obvious implication is that marked homeomorphism type is a complete
invariant for marked closed 3-manifolds up to isometry, but from this the unmarked version follows.
Similar remarks apply to the finite-volume case.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
85
C∗
c(Isom+, Hn), and from [SZ94, Cor. 3.2] and Corollary 5.7 that their classification
is Borel reducible to =R, as noted above. When n = 3, the Ending Lamination
Theorem supplies something a little better, namely a countable list of complete in-
variants (namely, by the proof just below, the homeomorphism types of the associ-
ated pared manifolds discussed at the end of this subsection); much more generally,
Stuck–Zimmer’s result admits the following refinement:
Proposition 8.6. For any n ≥3, the classification of finite-volume complete hyper-
bolic n-manifolds up to isometry, and hence of lattices in Isom(Hn) up to conjugacy,
is Borel bireducible with =N.
Clues to this fact are the pretty results that, viewed as a suborder of R, the
collection of finite volumes of complete hyperbolic n-manifolds is of ordertype ωω
when n = 3 (by Jørgensen–Thurston; see [Gro81]), and of ordertype ω when n > 3
[Wan72, BGLM02], and thus that this collection, in any event, is countable. The
cleanest approach, though, is probably the following.
Proof. By Mostow–Prasad rigidity [Pra73], such manifolds are fully classified by
their fundamental groups, and by [Rat19, Theorem 12.7.4], any such manifold de-
formation retracts onto a compact one.
Hence again by [CK70] there are only
countably many such manifolds, and the argument concludes just as for Corollary
3.24.
□
As noted, by the above coupled with Theorem 7.2, the classification problem for
finite-volume hyperbolic n-manifolds decreases (strictly, then weakly) in complex-
ity as n rises. For the more general question of how the complexity of manifold
classification problems varies with dimension, see our conclusion’s Question 4 and
surrounding discussion.
We venture next onto the terrain of infinite-volume manifolds and, thereby, onto
more substantial considerations of manifolds’ ends. Within the framework of the
Ending Lamination Theorem, this is the point at which markings of these manifolds
and of their ends assume a greater importance; so, in consequence, do the distinc-
tions organizing diagram (8) of this section’s introduction as well. Recall that the
preferred setting for that diagram was when Γ was non-abelian. By dispensing next
with the class of elementary torsion-free Kleinian groups, we will free ourselves to
thereafter restrict our attention, as is common, to that preferred setting. We will
review a few other objects for subsequent use along the way.
Recall the identification, discussed above, of ˆC with the sphere at infinity en-
closing the Poincaré ball model of H3, and the attendant identification of the group
of Möbius transformations of ˆC with Isom+(H3). Note next that any discrete sub-
group Γ of Isom+(H3) determines a closed limit set Λ(Γ) ⊆ˆC of limit points of
the action of Γ on H3, along with a complementary ordinary set or domain of dis-
continuity Ω(Γ) = ˆC\Λ(Γ). Such a Γ is elementary if the set Λ(Γ) is finite, or,
equivalently, if Γ contains an abelian subgroup of finite index. Elementary Kleinian
groups are of such anomalous simplicity as to regularly merit separate treatments,
a tradition on display in both the first proof of Theorem 7.2 and the next lemma,
which will help to streamline our subsequent analyses. Moreover, the class E of
elementary torsion-free Kleinian groups forms a Borel subset of Daf(PSL(2, C)); to
see this, observe again that the group-isomorphism relation on the latter is analytic
and possesses only countably many classes, and that E consists of exactly 2 of them,
as the proof below will show.

86
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Lemma 8.7. The classification of elementary torsion-free Kleinian groups up to
conjugacy is Borel bireducible with the relation =R of equality on the real numbers.
Proof. See [Mar16, pp. 3, 60-61] and [Bea95, Thm. 4.3.1] for arguments that such
a group Γ of Möbius transformations is either:
(1) cyclic, generated by a loxodromic element conjugate to a transformation of
the form z 7→λz for some unique λ ∈C with |λ| > 1;
(2) cyclic, generated by a parabolic element conjugate to the transformation
z 7→z + 1;
(3) a rank 2 abelian group, conjugate to a group generated by the elements
z 7→z + 1 and z 7→z + λ for some unique λ ∈C with positive imaginary
part.
Such groups, in other words, admit complete invariants of the form (type, λ) ∈
{1, 2, 3} × C (with λ = 0 when the type is 2).
That the assignment of these
invariants is, over each type, continuous is not hard to see; a type 1 Γ’s associated
λ falls in some open U ⊆C, for example, if and only if its conjugate taking the
repelling and attracting fixed points (with respect to a choice of generator g ∈Γ)
of Γ to 0 and ∞, respectively, maps g to a z 7→κz with κ ∈U. This will then hold
as well for all type 1 Γ′ with generators in a sufficiently small open neighborhood
of g, and the latter is a relatively open condition; details of this and the remaining
cases are left to the reader. (To be clear, we do not claim that the assignment
is continuous as a map to {1, 2, 3} × C: we’ll note that it isn’t below. We claim
only that the assignment is continuous on the preimage of each {i} × C (as one
could alternately argue for the matrix representations of these groups via the trace
function), and that this establishes the lemma.)
□
With Lemma 8.7 behind us, we will henceforth focus on nonelementary torsion-
free Kleinian groups; within diagram 8, this will free us to assume that Γ is non-
abelian, whereupon Lemma 8.1 will apply. In particular, the topology which AH(Γ)
therein inherits from DF(Γ) will be Polish. Since at least [Thu22a], this topology
has been known as the algebraic topology (this is the source of the “A” in AH(Γ);
the “H” is for hyperbolic), in contradistinction to the geometric (i.e., Chabauty–Fell)
topology predominating in our Sections 5 and 7 above. The natural setting for the
latter topology is a set, like D[Γ], of closed subgroups of PSL(2, C), as we’ve seen —
but from there it pulls back along p : DF(Γ) →D[Γ] to define a topology on the set
underlying DF(Γ) which we will denote τG. By a standard abuse, we will also term
τG, as well as its associated quotient topology on the set underlying AH(Γ), the
geometric topology; similarly for the algebraic topology τA which we first endowed
DF(Γ) with when introducing it above.
The interactions between these two topologies can be quite subtle: as we will
see shortly, a sequence of groups or manifolds may converge to one thing in one
topology and to another in the other. The evolution of associated objects alongside
them (elements’ limit sets or domains of discontinuity, for example) is, in general,
more transparent when these two limits agree. More formally, it is more transparent
when convergence is with respect to the so-called strong topology τS generated by
τA ∪τG on DF(Γ). We write SH(Γ) (“S” is for strong) to denote the underlying
set of AH(Γ) endowed with the quotient topology induced by τS. The following
observation will be useful below.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
87
Lemma 8.8. For any finitely generated Kleinian group Γ, the Borel σ-algebras
generated by τA and τS on DF(Γ) and, thus, on AH(Γ) and SH(Γ) coincide.
Proof. To prove the second assertion, clearly it suffices to prove the first one. For
this, fix a generating set {gi | i < n} of elements of Γ and view DF(Γ) as a subspace
of the product space PSL(2, C)n in the manner of its initial definition above, so that
as i and V , respectively, range below k and among the open subsets of PSL(2, C),
the sets
U A
i,V := {ρ : Γ →PSL(2, C) | ρ(gi) ∈V }
define a subbasis for τA. Similarly, as V ranges among the open subsets of PSL(2, C),
the sets
U G
V := {ρ : Γ →PSL(2, C) | im(ρ) ∩V ̸= ∅}
generate the Borel σ-algebra BG generated by τG; write BA and BS, respectively,
for the algebras generated by τA and τS as well. From this presentation it’s clear
that each U G
V is open in τA, and hence that BG ⊆BA. Thus BS = BA.
□
Let us now tie a few threads of our discussion together. In the proof of Lemma
8.7, we hinted that the type-class (1) therein isn’t closed. In fact there exist se-
quences of representations ρi : Z →PSL(2, C) with images all of type (1) which
converge algebraically and geometrically to groups of type (2) and type (3), respec-
tively. For elegant description of one example, see [Thu22a, Example 9.1.4]; see
also the treatment in [Mar16, §4.9]. These are the building blocks of more com-
plex and arresting examples (of simultaneous algebraic and geometric convergence,
respectively, to finitely and infinitely generated groups, for instance (in particular,
Dfg(PSL(2, C)) is not closed)), and they are emblems of the ways that loxodromic
elements of Kleinian groups can “degenerate”, in those groups’ limits, into parabolic
subgroups, or, at the level of the quotient manifolds, into cusps.
We encountered such structures in Section 7; in three hyperbolic dimensions the
story is as follows (see [Mar16, §3.2] for further discussion):
• The parabolic elements of a Kleinian group Γ organize into maximal para-
bolic subgroups of the stabilizers of their fixed points. These are of rank 1
or 2 and patterned on the second or third type-class, respectively, of Lemma
8.7 (whose representatives therein fix the point ∞∈ˆC). Sullivan showed in
[Sul81] that if Γ is finitely generated then these subgroups sort into finitely
many conjugacy classes within Γ; these, in turn, correspond to the cusps of
N = H3/Γ.
• Topologically, the latter form ends (or what might be more precisely termed
end neighborhoods) of N which, like their Lemma 8.7 prototypes’ quotients,
are either homeomorphic to S1 × R × R or to S1 × S1 × R.
• Geometrically, cusps fall within the ε-thin part of their ambient manifold
N, where ε is at most the Margulis constant for hyperbolic 3-space: in a
sense further encapsulated by horoballs, the geometry of N attenuates in
its cusps, “pulling away” from its main and thicker parts.
In consequence of this last point, hyperbolic 3-manifolds N are often studied via
their decusped portion N 0, resulting from replacing neighborhoods of N’s rank-1
and rank-2 cusps by (or, equivalently, retracting them to) boundary annuli or tori,
respectively. This is the meaning of the N0
ρ appearing in the second statement of
the Ending Lamination Theorem in Section 8.1.

88
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
At times, this practice in turn dictates a finer approach to diagram (8). See
[CM04, §7] or [NS12, §4] for succinct accounts of the deformation spaces AH(M, P)
of pared manifolds (M, P) like N 0
ρ above. We’ll forego this formalism except in
the proof of Theorem 8.13 and in Section 8.4 below; each of the latter, though,
will involve an appeal to the subdiagram of (8) associated to AH(M, P). We defer
a discussion of the details until then; note for later use, though, the following
consequence of the finiteness recorded in the first bulleted item above:
Lemma 8.9. Write par for the set of parabolic elements of PSL(2, C). For any
finitely generated π1(M) of a complete orientable hyperbolic 3-manifold M, the set
P(π1(M)) = {ρ−1(par) | ρ ∈DF(π1(M))} is countable.
□
We’ve now surveyed most of the basics that we need to proceed, in Sections
8.3 and 8.4, respectively, to the classes of geometrically finite and algebraically
finite hyperbolic 3-manifolds.
To see that the former defines a standard Borel
space, however, we must first keep a promise from Section 7: we must show that
computing a manifold’s convex core is a Borel operation.
To that end, recall from above the limit set Λ(Γ) and domain of discontinuity
Ω(Γ) = ˆC\Λ(Γ) associated to a Kleinian group Γ. Each induces further objects of
interest: the natural action of Γ on Ω(Γ) is, as the latter’s name suggests, prop-
erly discontinuous, with the result that Ω(Γ)/Γ is a 2-manifold encoding essential
information about the ends of M = H3/Γ, as we’ll see in Section 8.3.
Relat-
edly, let CH(Λ(Γ)) denote the convex hull, in H3, of Λ(Γ); the quotient manifold
CH(Λ(Γ))/Γ is then precisely the convex core CC(M) of M. Let us check that the
derivations of these objects from Γ are Borel.
Lemma 8.10. For any finitely generated nonelementary torsion-free Kleinian group
∆, the maps
1. f1 : D[∆] →F(ˆC) : Γ 7→Λ(Γ),
2. f2 : D[∆] →F(H3) : Γ 7→CH(Λ(Γ)), and
3. f3 : D[∆] →O(ˆC) : Γ 7→Ω(Γ), and
are each Borel measurable.
Proof. By [Mar16, Theorem 4.5.6], the map assigning limit sets to points in SH(∆)
is continuous, and f1 results from precomposing it with the Borel map qs of diagram
8. Thus f1 is Borel, and since complementation induces a homeomorphism F(ˆC) →
O(ˆC), so too is f3. The function f2 is then the composition hgf1, where g : F(ˆC) →
F(H3∪ˆC) maps F to its convex hull in H3∪ˆC and h : F(H3∪ˆC) →F(H3) intersects
the result with H3. Since, plainly, h is Borel, for item (2) it only remains to check
that g is as well. As U ranges among the open subsets of H3 ∪ˆC, the subbasic
open sets O1(U) = {H ∈F(H3 ∪ˆC) | H ∩U ̸= ∅} generate the family of Borel
subsets of F(H3 ∪ˆC). Thus it suffices to show that g−1(O1(U)) is Borel for any
such U. In fact, it is open: given F ∈g−1(O1(U)) with some convex combination
of x0, . . . , xk ∈F as witness, clearly there exist neighborhoods V0 ∋x0, . . . , Vk ∋xk
such that the same combination of any y0 ∈V0, . . . , yk ∈Vk falls in U as well, and
thus that
F ∈

This completes the proof.
□

It’s now straightforward to conclude that the assignment of the convex core of

Lemma 8.11. For any finitely generated nonelementary torsion-free Kleinian group
∆, there exists a Borel map f4 : D[∆] →Cc(Isom+, H3)2 encoding the convex core
computation Γ 7→CC(H3/Γ).
Proof. In the notation of Lemma 3.31, f4(Γ) will equal ((U, c), (V, c|V)), with (U, c)

Vi,j. That this assignment is Borel is immediate from Lemma 8.10. That these
choices of Vi cohere, so that (V, c|V), moreover, defines an open submanifold of
M(U,c), follows from the fact that CH(Λ(Γ)) is Γ-invariant.
□
The following corollary then ties up all loose ends from Section 7. For simplicity,
write C for the Borel parameter space of complete orientable hyperbolic 2-manifolds
with finitely generated noncyclic fundamental group, and recall that it was just
these sorts of manifolds whose convex cores we doubled in our first proof of Theorem
7.2 above.
Corollary 8.12. Dgf(PSL(2, C)) is a Borel subset of Dtf(PSL(2, C)). In addition,
there exists a Borel function C →Cc(Isom+, H2)2 : M 7→CC(M) computing a
hyperbolic 2-manifold’s convex core.
Proof. For the first assertion, note that by Lemma 8.11 coupled with the argument,
for example, of [CEM06, Thm. I.3.1.4], the collection of nonelementary Γ possessing
a convex core of volume at most k is, for any k > 0, a Borel set. Thus the collection
of those possessing a finite-volume convex core is Borel as well. Since the convex
cores associating to torsion-free elementary Kleinian groups are all also of finite
volume (consider their limit sets), Lemma 8.7 then completes the argument.
For the second assertion, let H denote the geodesic plane with boundary ˆR ⊆ˆC
in the Poincaré ball model of H3, so that H is a PSL(2, R)-invariant copy of H2.
Aggregate the functions, as ∆ranges, of Lemma 8.11 into a single function which
we also, in an abuse, denote by f4. Write f5 for the composition

8.3. Classifying geometrically finite ends. We turn in this section to the re-
maining manifold finiteness conditions (iii), (iv), and (v) of geometric, topological,
and algebraic finiteness, respectively; let us begin with a word on their relation-
ship. Historically, the Ending Lamination Theorem was the second of a trio of out-
standing conjectures on noncompact hyperbolic 3-manifolds appearing in [Thu82]
which were each resolved in the first decade of this century.
The first of these
was the Tameness or Marden Conjecture that, in our working terminology, every
algebraically finite hyperbolic 3-manifold is topologically finite. This conjecture was
independently proven by [Ago04] and [CG06] in 2004, and implies the structural

90
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
possibilities for such manifolds’ ends to be mercifully limited. To simplify discus-
sion, assume until otherwise indicated that M is a cusp-free algebraically finite
complete orientable hyperbolic 3-manifold with nonelementary holonomy group Γ
(for more general M, first pare M back to a decusped manifold M0 as in Section 8.2,
whereupon the discussion below will broadly apply). In this case, in combination
with [Can93] the Tameness Theorem tells us that
• topologically, the ends of M all possess a neighborhood of the form S ×
[0, ∞) for some surface S (they are tame); moreover,
• metrically, these ends are of each of exactly one of two sorts: each is ei-
ther geometrically finite or simply degenerate (also known as geometrically
infinite).
What distinguishes between these two sorts of ends of M is their relation to the
convex core CC(M) of M: geometrically finite ends possess neighborhoods disjoint
from CC(M), while the geometrically infinite ends of M fall entirely within CC(M).
Note here the minor and defensible overloading of terminology: M is a geometrically
finite manifold if and only if all of its ends are geometrically finite, i.e., if and only
if CC(M) contains no geometrically infinite ends. These sorts of manifolds are
this subsection’s main subject. We’ll develop some machinery in the course of a
heuristic argument that their isometry relation is concretely classifiable, then we’ll
give a short, higher-level proof of this fact, thereby establishing the second item of
Theorem 8.5.
By the first bulleted item above, associated to either geometric sort of end is a
“ray” [0, ∞) of induced geometric structures on a surface S. Further differences be-
tween geometrically finite and geometrically infinite ends are how these geometric
structures on S × {t} evolve within them as t →∞and, relatedly, how these two
types of ends are represented by the action of Γ on the boundary of H3. Recall that
Ω(Γ) denotes the portion of that boundary on which Γ acts properly discontinu-
ously. What is sometimes termed the Kleinian manifold ˆ
M associated to Γ is the
manifold with boundary (H3 ∪Ω(Γ))/Γ = M ∪∂ˆ
M, and the components of this
boundary bijectively correspond to the geometrically finite ends of M, abutting and
compactifying their corresponding end “at infinity”. Moreover, Γ acts by isometries
on the hyperbolic Poincaré metric on Ω(Γ), thus this boundary Ω(Γ)/Γ naturally
carries a hyperbolic structure, one which in fact is the subject of one of this field’s
most fundamental theorems (our formulation of it is as in [KS89, Kap09]).
Ahlfors’s Finiteness Theorem ([Ahl64]). If Γ is a nonelementary finitely gen-
erated torsion-free Kleinian group then Ω(Γ)/Γ is a finite union of finite-volume
hyperbolic Riemann surfaces.
It is these surfaces, viewed, as in Section 7, as points in their associated Teich-
müller spaces, which comprise the ending invariants of the geometrically finite ends
of M — or, more precisely, of a marking of M, for it is only by way of such markings
that the Teichmüller representations of these surfaces are well-defined. Guided by
diagram (8), we may be much more precise. Suppose Γ is the image of a discrete
faithful representation ρ of an abstract group ∆. Recall that a Kleinian group Γ is
geometrically finite if M ∼= H3/Γ is, and let
GF(∆) = {[θ] ∈AH(∆) | the image of θ ∈DF(∆) is geometrically finite}.
The Ending Lamination Theorem assigns complete invariants (marked homeomor-
phism type, ending invariants) to the marked manifolds associated to elements of

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
91
GF(∆); as usual, to simplify statements, since (by tameness, for example) there are
only countably many marked homeomorphism types of geometrically finite man-
ifolds, we are free from a Borel complexity point of view to restrict attention to
those of the same type as the marking Mρ of M. We denote this subspace of GF(∆)
by QH(ρ), and write DF(ρ) for its preimage by q : DF(∆) →AH(∆). As θ ranges
within DF(ρ), the homeomorphism type of ∂ˆ
Mθ is constant; by defining the Te-
ichmüller space of a possibly disconnected space as the product of the Teichmüller
spaces of its components, we may then paraphrase this case of the Ending Lamina-
tion Theorem27 as follows: If ˆ
Mρ is boundary-incompressible, then the assignment
QH(ρ) →Teich(∂ˆ
Mρ) outlined above is injective (and in fact bijective); in particu-
lar, it completely classifies the elements of DF(ρ) up to PSL(2, C)-conjugacy. We
do so to foreground its main idea, which is that the geometry of a geometrically
finite M’s boundary surfaces completely encodes the geometry of M, and thus
that our analysis of such manifolds’ isometry relations should ultimately reduce,
conceptually, to that of Section 7.
From a Borel complexity perspective, though, the italicized result above adds
little to Proposition 8.3; moreover, our primary interest at this stage is in PSL(2, C)-
conjugacy relations on Dgf(PSL(2, C)), not on DF(ρ). Referring again to diagram
(8), however, observe that if we write D[ρ] for the p-image of DF(ρ) then since each
of these sets is Borel (by [Kec95, Exer. 18.14]) and PSL(2, C)-conjugacy-invariant,
and Dgf(PSL(2, C)) is, as above, a countable union of such sets, our analysis of
PSL(2, C)-conjugacy relations on the latter readily reduces to one on any fixed
such D[ρ]; note next that for this analysis, the italicized instance of the Ending
Lamination Theorem above is, in fact, quite suggestive. For the diagram’s map
s|D[ρ] may be seen as assigning its domain’s elements markings; composed then with
q, it takes D[ρ] into the domain of the assignment QH(ρ) →Teich(∂ˆ
Mρ) above,
which we may in turn regard as a restricted instance of the map e of diagram
(8). Thus eqs|D[ρ] first marks the groups ∆of D[ρ], then classifies the resulting
marked groups up to conjugacy.
Since there are only countably many ways of
marking a countable group, it follows that postcomposing eqs|D[ρ] with a map
f : Teich(∂ˆ

of E(PSL(2, C), D[ρ]) to the identity relation on some set Y . Of course, from Section

a point we’ll return to in 8.4; see [Bro01] more particularly for failures of maps
from AH(Γ) to Polish spaces of ending invariants to be continuous. Thus in this
