Note that we’ve now proved all but the top item of Theorem 8.5. Note also
that in the above proof we derived our lower bound from the class of elementary
Kleinian groups; a naive question is whether it holds in the nonelementary context
as well. For its connection to the material of Section 8.4, let us briefly argue from
Bers’s Simultaneous Uniformization Theorem [Ber60], a special case of the bijection
QH(ρ) →Teich(∂ˆ
Mρ) discussed above, that indeed it does. Fix a closed hyperboliz-
able surface S; in the present terminology, Bers’s theorem tells us that every pair in
Teich(S)2 forms the ending invariants of a marked 3-manifold M ∼= H3/Γ ∼= S × R
for some geometrically finite Γ ≤PSL(2, C) (the pair encodes the marked hyper-
bolic structures of ∂ˆ
M’s two components lying at either end of R). Forgetting the
marking then amounts to quotienting Teich(S)2 by the natural properly discontinu-
ous action of MCG(S); since the uncountable Polish result parametrizes conjugacy
classes of geometrically finite Kleinian groups, this witnesses that =R, indeed, is a
≤B-lower bound, as desired.
8.4. Classifying geometrically infinite ends. It was Theorem 8.13 we’d had
chiefly in mind in this section’s introduction when describing geometrically fi-
nite manifolds as the tractable class of algebraically finite hyperbolic 3-manifolds.
We now consider those manifolds parametrized by Daf(PSL(2, C))\Dgf(PSL(2, C)),
which we’d in turn described as limits of geometrically finite manifolds. This we’d
meant in multiple senses:
• As noted in Section 8.3, GF(Γ) may be identified with the interior of
the space AH(Γ).29 Reciprocally, AH(Γ) is the closure of GF(Γ), as was
29 Let us be precise: implicit in such statements is a choice, first of all, of ambient character
variety, either that denoted R0(Γ) in [Kap08, §4.2] (our default choice) or its relative version
(Rpar) in which noncyclic abelian subgroups of Γ are only ever mapped to parabolics. Notations
vary.
Consider first, though, the case in which every abelian subgroup of Γ is cyclic, so that
the choice is immaterial. Then int(AH(Γ)) consists of the convex cocompact points of AH(Γ); in
other words, in our notation, it is exactly GF⋆(Γ, ∅). For the more general case, let P collect all
conjugates of elements of noncyclic abelian subgroups of Γ. Then with respect to the appropriate
character variety, the interior of AH(Γ, P) is again exactly GF⋆(Γ, P); each of these cases is due
to Sullivan [Sul85].

94
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
conjectured by Bers, Sullivan, and Thurston, and ultimately proven in
[Ohs11, NS12].
• As we’ve also seen, manifolds parametrized by Dgf(PSL(2, C)) admit in-
variants falling in Teichmüller spaces associated to their geometrically fi-
nite ends. Manifolds parametrized by Daf(PSL(2, C))\Dgf(PSL(2, C)), on
the other hand, possess geometrically infinite ends, with natural invariants
falling in the boundary of their associated electric Teichmüller spaces; see
[Kla22]. We’ll return to these latter invariants below.
• By the Tameness Theorem, manifolds M = H3/Γ of the topological form
S × R for S a finite-type hyperbolizable surface are, as Γ ranges, the pro-
totypes of any of these sorts of ends. In the simplest case, Γ ∼= π1(S) is a
Fuchsian subgroup of PSL(2, R) ≤PSL(2, C) with limit set the meridian
circle ˆR in ˆC. The quasi-Fuchsian groups (of the first kind) Γ are the quasi-
conformal deformations of these Fuchsian ones; their limit sets Λ(Γ) are, in
general, more meandering Jordan curves than ˆR in ˆC. These groups remain
geometrically finite, with their associated Kleinian manifolds (H3 ∪Ω(Γ))/Γ
“capped” by two topological copies of S corresponding to the two compo-
nents of ˆC\Λ(Γ) (these were the groups at work in our second argument
of the lower bound in Theorem 8.13 above). The asymptotic case, though,
is that of doubly degenerate Kleinian groups Γ ∼= π1(S), whose limit sets
Λ(Γ) have grown to engulf ˆC entirely (so that both Ω(Γ) and these groups’
Kleinian manifolds’ boundaries are empty). These groups are geometrically
infinite, and form the setting for this subsection’s main results.
Continuing with this last point, we’ll term a Kleinian Γ ≤PSL(2, C) a surface
group if Γ ∼= π1(S) for some finite-type hyperbolizable surface S (see again the
discussion preceding Definition 7.4 for definitions).
By proceeding from surface
groups to a more general analysis, this subsection reprises a pattern in the literature
(the sequence [Min10] then [BCM12], for example; alternately, see [Bow23, p. 5]:
“understanding the particular [case of M homeomorphic to S × R] is in some sense
the key to understanding the Ending Lamination Theorem”). In fact this section’s
results may be read as a further explanation for this pattern: they tell us broadly
that once one’s classified surface groups, it’s all downhill from there.
Before proceeding, let us address an ambiguity signaled by the parenthetical
“(of the first kind)” above; the question there was whether the peripheral elements
of π1(S) should by definition be forbidden from taking non-parabolic values in
the isomorphism ρ : π1(S) →Γ witnessing that Γ is quasi-Fuchsian (if so, then
the parenthetical’s superfluous).
To save ourselves locutions of this sort, until
otherwise indicated we’ll adopt both this and the broader convention that Γ is a
surface group only if it is the image of an isomorphism ρ : π1(S) →Γ placing the
set P of peripheral elements of π1(S) in bijection with the set of parabolic elements
of Γ; moreover, wherever there’s no danger of confusion, the notation P should
henceforth be understood to denote the above distinguished subset. The parings of
the quotient manifolds H3/Γ ∼= S × R of surface groups Γ then possess either 0, 1,
or 2 geometrically infinite ends. We addressed the first (quasi-Fuchsian) possibility
in Section 8.3; we turn now to the latter two.
Definition 8.14. A surface group Γ is doubly degenerate if Λ(Γ) = ˆC. For any
finite-type hyperbolizable surface S, write D⋆
dd[π1(S)] for {Γ ∈D⋆[π1(S), P] |

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
95
Λ(Γ) = ˆC} and DD⋆(π1(S)) for {ρ ∈AH⋆(π1(S), P) | Λ(im(ρ)) = ˆC}.
A sur-
face group Γ is singly degenerate if it is neither geometrically finite nor doubly
degenerate; write D⋆
sd[π1(S)] ⊆D⋆[π1(S), P] and SD⋆(π1(S)) ⊆AH⋆(π1(S), P) for
the naturally associated spaces of singly degenerate Kleinian groups and classes of
representations, respectively. A manifold H3/Γ is singly or doubly degenerate if
and only if Γ is.
Lemma 8.15. For any S as above, D⋆
dd[π1(S)] is a Borel subset of D⋆[π1(S)]
and DD⋆(π1(S)) is a Borel subset of AH⋆(π1(S)); similarly for D⋆
sd[π1(S)] and
SD⋆(π1(S)).
Proof. The first assertion follows from the proof of Theorem 8.13 together with
Lemma 8.10(1); for the second, observe that DD⋆(π1(S)) = (pt)−1(D⋆
dd[π1(S)])
in the notation of diagram (9). The third and fourth are then immediate from
definitions, together with Corollary 8.12.
□
A main class of examples of doubly degenerate hyperbolic 3-manifolds arises
as follows. Among the homeomorphisms φ of a finite-type hyperbolizable surface
S with itself are distinguished ones, termed pseudo-Anosov, with the property of
preserving (while rescaling in inverse proportions) transverse pairs of measured
laminations on S.
We discuss the latter below; fundamental references for the
former include [FLP12], [FM12] and especially [Thu22b], wherein it is shown that
the mapping torus Mφ of a pseudo-Anosov φ admits a hyperbolic metric. Mφ is an
S-bundle over a circle, and “unrolling” it along that circle defines an infinite cyclic
cover Nφ of Mφ which is necessarily doubly degenerate. For a gentle discussion of
this example, see [Min03a, §1.2].
Our first result below, the lower bound half of Theorem 8.4, leverages a more
elaborate manifestation of this phenomenon appearing in [ABB+20]. Therein, re-
alizations of the free group Fn as Schottky subgroups of pseudo-Anosov elements
of MCG(S) induce quasi-isometric embeddings of geodesics in Fn into Teich(S)
giving rise, in turn, to a family of doubly degenerate hyperbolic 3-manifolds all of
topological type S×R. The cyclic covers described above are dense in this example;
in the authors’ own words (after replacing their Σ with our S):
The idea is to use the Schottky group ⟨φ0, . . . , φn⟩[of pseudo-
Anosov elements of Diff+(S)] to associate to every element γ ∈
{0, . . . , n}Z of the shift space a pair consisting of the following ele-
ments:
(1) a hyperbolic 3-manifold Nγ homeomorphic to S × R
(2) a ‘coarse base point’ Pγ, i.e., a subset of Nγ with universally
bounded diameter.
Shifting a string corresponds to shifting the base point of Nγ and
convergence of γi ∈{0, . . . , n}Z corresponds to based Gromov–
Hausdorff convergence of the associated pairs (Nγ, Pγ).
In particular, the shift action induces isometry. Thus when n = 1, the construction
supplies what might be regarded as a “coarse embedding” of the relation EZ of
shift-equivalence on {0, 1}Z into the PSL(2, C)-conjugacy relation on D⋆
dd[π1(S)],
and the following is what a descriptive set theorist would consequently suspect.
Theorem 8.16. If S is a closed orientable hyperbolic surface then
E0 ≤B E(PSL(2, C), D⋆
dd[π1(S)]).

96
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
To prove the theorem, one would like to simply extract a Borel reduction of EZ to
E(PSL(2, C), D⋆
dd[π1(S)]) from the construction described above. The senses, how-
ever, in which that construction’s necessarily coarse complicate naive approaches.
What seems much easier is to couple the following (which figures as Theorem 6.4.5
in [Gao09]) with the construction to produce an F with E0 ≤B F, and then to
show that F weakly reduces to E, and this will be our strategy.
Lemma 8.17. For any Borel equivalence relation E on a Polish space X, the
following are equivalent:
• E0 ≤B E.
• There exists an E-nonatomic, E-ergodic probability Borel measure on X.
Proof of Theorem 8.16. Fix an S as in the statement of the theorem and write Y
for D⋆
dd[π1(S)] and X for {0, 1}Z (more formally, we are writing X for the space
G(F)/F ∼= G0(F) of [ABB+20, 12.19]). The construction described above associates
to each x ∈X an element of DD⋆(π1(S)) which we will denote by Nx; by [ABB+20,
12.19], this map is both continuous and injective, hence its graph Z0 = {(x, Nx) |
x ∈X} is a Borel subset of X × DD⋆(π1(S)). Recalling the notations of diagram
(8), write Z1 ⊆X × DF(π1(S)) for {(x, ρ) | q(ρ) = Nx} and Z2 ⊆X × Y for
{(x, Γ) | Γ = p(ρ) for some (x, ρ) ∈Z1}. The set Z1 is Borel because q and Z0 are,
thus Z2 is Borel because p is countable-to-one.
Now recall from Section 2.2 the relation EZ of shift-equivalence on X and define
the relation F on Z2 by (x, Γ)F(x′, Γ′) if and only if xEZx′. We claim that E0 ≤B F.
To see this, first fix an EZ-nonatomic, EZ-ergodic probability Borel measure µX on
X (the standard product measure works). Fix next a probability Borel measure
µY on Y for which µ(Z2) = ε > 0, where µ denotes the product measure of µX
and µY . (One way to see that such measures exist is via the Jankov–von Neumann
uniformization theorem [Kec95, Theorem 18.1]: there exists a σ(Σ1
1)-measurable
f : X →Y with (x, f(x)) ∈Z2 for all x ∈X, and the pushforward f∗µX of µX by
f is then a µY as desired.) Next note that ε−1µ is a probability Borel measure on
Z2 which is nonatomic and ergodic with respect to F because µX is with respect
to EZ. Thus by Lemma 8.17 there exists a Borel h reducing E0 to F. The map
Z2 →Y : (x, Γ) 7→Γ, on the other hand, is plainly Borel. By [ABB+20, Prop. 12.19]
it defines a countable-to-one mapping of F-classes to E(PSL(2, C), Y )-classes and
thus its precomposition by h witnesses, via Lemma 6.9, that E(PSL(2, C), Y ) is
not concretely classifiable. It then follows from the Glimm–Effros Dichotomy (see
Section 2.2) that E0 ≤B E(PSL(2, C), Y ).
□
We turn next to the upper bound half of Theorem 8.4. The result we’ll apply to
that end will in fact bound the complexity of the isometry relation for a broader
class of manifolds than those under consideration in Theorem 8.16. But we should
precede its application with some discussion, as promised, of the space EL(S) of
ending laminations of a hyperbolic surface S.
A geodesic lamination of such an S is a foliation (i.e., a partition) of a closed
subset of S by geodesic curves. ML(S) is the space of transversely measured lam-
inations on S whose support is a geodesic lamination, and it is endowed with a
natural weak∗topology; see [Bon01], [CEM06, I.4] or any of the references sur-
rounding our first statement of the Ending Lamination Theorem above. Within
ML(S) are distinguished filling laminations, and their image under the measure-
forgetting quotient map from ML(S) is what we denote the ending lamination space

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
97
EL(S) of S. It is Polish [Gab14, Rmk. 1.6]. Its elements are what parametrize ge-
ometrically infinite structures on (S × [0, ∞))-homeomorphic ends of algebraically
finite hyperbolic 3-manifolds; thus by the Ending Lamination Theorem, alongside
Teichmüller invariants for geometrically finite ends they are, as S ranges, what pa-
rametrize those manifolds themselves. Additional significance attaches to EL(S) via
its homeomorphism with closely related spaces, namely that of minimal foliations
on S, or the boundary at infinity of either the curve complex or electric Teichmüller
space of S (see [Kla22, Ham06]); these reinforce the heuristic that EL(S) describes
the asymptotic behavior of sequences of curves or hyperbolic structures on S.
Technical constraints or parabolic elements can complicate EL(S)’s parametriz-
ing role as we’ve described it just above; see this section’s conclusion for further
discussion. These elements are by definition under control, though, in the setting of
DD⋆(π1(S)), and there EL(S) plays this role optimally; writing ∆for a product’s
diagonal, the following is [LS09, Theorem 6.5].
Theorem 8.18. For any finite-type hyperbolizable surface S the ending invariant
map e : DD⋆(π1(S)) →(EL(S) × EL(S))\∆is a homeomorphism.
Recall that MCG of a closed surface S is an index-2 subgroup of Out(π1(S)). In
combination with Theorem 8.18, the following slight rephrasing of [PS21, Cor. 1.2]
should then suggest the upper bound in question.
Theorem 8.19. Let S be a finite-type hyperbolizable surface. The orbit equivalence
relation induced by the natural action of MCG(S) on EL(S) is hyperfinite.
From this we will argue the following.
Theorem 8.20. For any finite-type hyperbolizable surface S, both the relations
E(PSL(2, C), D⋆
dd[π1(S)]) and E(PSL(2, C), D⋆
sd[π1(S)]) are essentially hyperfinite.
Recall from Section 2.2 that the latter phrase is tantamount to “≤B E0”. We
turn to the theorem’s proof after noting its utility in proving Theorems 8.4 and 8.5.
Proof of Theorem 8.4. Any closed orientable hyperbolizable surface S is as in the
premises of both Theorems 8.16 and 8.20, which together imply that
E0 ≤B E(PSL(2, C), D⋆
dd[π1(S)]) ≤B E0.
By the reasoning of Lemma 6.11, this then also holds of E(Isom(H3), D⋆
dd[π1(S)]),
where we’re momentarily (i.e., in only this proof and the next one) reading the sec-
ond argument as the space of discrete type-preserving copies of π1(S) in Isom(H3).
The translation of this result into manifold terms via Corollary 5.7 is by now rou-
tine; this completes the proof.
□
Proof of Theorem 8.5. The theorem’s first two items are simply Proposition 8.6 and
Theorem 8.13; its third is immediate from Theorem 8.4 together with the obser-
vation that D⋆
dd[π1(S)] is, in the above reading, an Isom(H3)-conjugation invariant
subset of D(Isom(H3)).
□
Proof of Theorem 8.20. Let us first argue the doubly degenerate case. Fix a finite-
type hyperbolizable surface S and write F for the equivalence relation induced
by the natural action of MCG(S) on EL(S) and E for the equivalence relation
induced by the natural action of MCG(S) on (EL(S) × EL(S))\∆. By Theorem
8.19, F is hyperfinite, thus the product F × F is as well [JKL02, Prop. 1.3]. By

98
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
the countability of MCG(S), the inclusion (EL(S) × EL(S))\∆→EL(S) × EL(S)
induces a weak reduction E →F × F; from this we conclude via Lemma 6.9 that
E is hyperfinite.
Consider next the commuting subdiagram of diagram (9) anchored at opposite
corners by D⋆
dd[π1(S)] and DD⋆(π1(S)); therein, much as in the proof of Theo-
rem 8.13, qs defines a Borel reduction of E(PSL(2, C), D⋆
dd[π1(S)]) to the orbit
equivalence relation E′ induced by the action of Out(π1(S), P) on DD⋆(π1(S)).
By the Dehn–Nielsen–Baer Theorem for punctured surfaces (see [FM12, Theo-
rem 8.8]), MCG(S) is an index-2 subgroup of Out(π1(S), P), so each E′-class
contains only finitely many classes of the e−1-homeomorphic image of E. From
this it follows by [DJK94, Prop. 1.3(vii)] that E′ is hyperfinite, and thus that
E(PSL(2, C), D⋆
dd[π1(S)]) is essentially hyperfinite, as claimed.
The argument for singly degenerate manifolds is almost identical; the key points
here are that the ending invariant map
e′ : SD⋆(π1(S)) →(EL(S) × Teich(S)) ⊔(Teich(S) × EL(S))
replacing the homeomorphism e above is still Borel, and the MCG(S)-action on
each of the latter factors is again hyperfinite. One may argue that e′ is Borel as
follows (there may be better ways): argue via [NS12, Theorem 8.1], for example,
that its restriction to the “slice” associated to any fixed degenerate end-structure
is Borel, and conclude that e′ also is via [LS09, Theorem 6.6] and [AB06, Lem.
4.5.1].
□
Remark 8.21. Upon inspection, Przytycki–Sabok in fact show in the course of
proving [PS21, Cor. 1.2] that if S is a finite-type hyperbolizable surface S other than
the thrice-punctured sphere then the equivalence relation induced by the natural
action of MCG(S) on EL(S) is Borel equivalent to E0 (the heart of their argument
is a bireduction with a tail-equivalence relation, the E∗
t of their [PS21, p. 7]). More
particularly, E0 ≤B F in the proof-notation just above (a fact not far in flavor from
[Mas85, Theorem 2]), and the proof’s other terms are linked closely enough that one
might hope to reverse its direction to show that E0 ≤B E(PSL(2, C), D⋆
dd[π1(S)])
for this broader class of surfaces S. Indeed, for this one would only need to show
that F ≤B E via a reduction EL(S) →(EL(S) × EL(S))\∆. The natural attempt
restricts to the identity on the first coordinate, and must therefore restrict on
the second coordinate to a fixed-point-free MCG(S)-equivariant endomorphism f :
EL(S) →EL(S), but pseudo-Anosovs’ fixed points easily rule out the existence of
such f. The attempt for D⋆
sd[π1(S)] faces similar difficulties, all of which we note
simply to say that the relations between dynamical phenomena like [Mas85] and
non-classifiability results like Theorem 8.16 remain to be better understood.
The preceding results are particularly suggestive in combination with the works
[CS12, CS13]. Letting Γ = π1(M) for an algebraically finite hyperbolic 3-manifold
M, the central object of study therein is “the topological quotient
AI(Γ) = AH(Γ)/Out(Γ),
which we may think of as the moduli space of unmarked 3-manifolds homotopy
equivalent to M” ([CS13, p. 221], with notations modified slightly to align with
our own); we may, of course, equally well think of AI(Γ) as a moduli space of
the PSL(2, C)-conjugacy classes of D[Γ]. The summary theorem in [CS13] is the
following.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
99
Theorem 8.22. Let M be a compact 3-manifold with non-abelian fundamental
group whose interior admits a complete hyperbolic metric. Then the moduli space
AI(π1(M)) is T1 if and only if M is not an untwisted interval bundle.
In our second proof of Theorem 7.2, we invoked the (1) ⇒(3) portion of Glimm’s
[Gli61, Theorem 1]; the following distills its (2) ⇒(3) portion.
Lemma 8.23. If a countable group G acts Borel-measurably on a Polish space X
and X/G is T0 then the associated orbit equivalence relation is concretely classifi-
able.
Together with Theorem 8.16, this affords us the following strengthening of The-
orem 8.22:
Corollary 8.24. If an M as in Theorem 8.22 isn’t an untwisted interval bundle
then AI(π1(M)) is Borel-isomorphic to a Polish space. If, on the other hand, M is
an untwisted interval bundle over a closed orientable hyperbolic surface S then for no
Polish topology on AH(π1(M)) inducing its usual Borel structure is the topological
quotient AH(π1(M))/Out(π1(M)) even T0.
Proof. For any M under consideration, temporarily write E(M) for the orbit equiv-
alence relation induced by the Out(π1(M))-action on AH(π1(M)). For the first
assertion, deduce from its premise, Theorem 8.22, and Lemma 8.23 that E(M) is
concretely classifiable, as witnessed by a Borel map r from AH(π1(M)) to some
Polish space Y ; being countable-to-one, im(r) is a Borel subset of Y , and r induces
a Borel isomorphism AI(π1(M)) ∼= im(r). For the second assertion, observe that
π1(M) = π1(S) and for any of the topologies in question,
E0 ≤B E(PSL(2, C), D⋆
dd[π1(S)]) ≤B E(M)
by Theorem 8.16 and Lemma 8.2, respectively; then apply the contrapositive of
Lemma 8.23.
□
A second corollary bounds the Borel complexity of the isometry relation for a
substantial class of hyperbolic 3-manifolds.
Corollary 8.25. The classification up to isometry of algebraically finite hyperbolic
3-manifolds whose holonomy groups Γ either
(1) are surface groups, or
(2) aren’t group-isomorphic to π1(S) for any surface S,
is essentially hyperfinite.
Proof. In case (2), the isometry problem is concretely classifiable, by Theorem 8.22,
Lemma 8.23, Lemma 8.2, and Corollary 5.7. In case (1), either Γ is geometrically
finite, whereupon by Theorem 8.13 the isometry problem is again concretely classifi-
able, or Γ ∈D⋆
dd[π1(S)]∪D⋆
sd[π1(S)], whereupon Theorem 8.20 applies and Lemma
6.11 and Corollary 5.7 again convert our conclusion to one about the isometry
problem for manifolds.
□
It is an artifact of our restrictive definition of surface group that Corollary 8.25
looks more exhaustive than it really is. For an algebraically finite hyperbolic 3-
manifold may be of a third kind as well: its holonomy group Γ may be isomorphic
to π1(S) for some surface S, but by no map placing Γ’s parabolic elements and

100
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
the peripheral elements of π1(S) in correspondence. Non-alignment can entail phe-
nomena of two broad sorts — accidental parabolics, and compressible boundaries —
whose analyses require extra levels of care. These phenomena aren’t unrelated to
the discontinuities, already noted, of the ending invariant map (see [AC96, Bro01]
for well-known examples, and [Can10] for a useful survey), which complicate ap-
peals to results like Theorem 8.19, and while Anderson–Lecuire show in [AL13]
that revision to the strong topology of Lemma 8.8 can rectify these discontinuities,
their results apply only in incompressible settings. We do, nevertheless, have ideas
for how these analyses will go. Faced more immediately, though, with the choice
between further delaying circulation of this paper’s other results and delaying a
complete analysis of the complexity of E(PSL(2, C), Daf(PSL(2, C))), we are opt-
ing for the latter.
9. Further questions
We conclude with a list of some of the most salient questions which remain. Let
us stress from the outset, though, that we regard this list as unavoidably, even
inherently, incomplete, for what we’ve found in the course of this project is that
those we’ve consulted about it can, in general, pose even better questions within
its framework than we ourselves can. May they continue to do so. Put differently,
our aim in what follows is less to conclude the kind of dialogue that informs this
work than to shape a place for more of it.
In any case, some obvious questions remain open. The most conspicuous among
them is that of the complexity of the homeomorphism relation for manifolds30 of
dimension greater than 2. Since, as discussed in our introduction, Iannella and
Weinstein [IW] have recently settled the 3-dimensional case, the state of the art
posing of this question is as follows:
Question 1. For any n ≥4, what is the Borel complexity of the homeomorphism
relation for connected topological n-manifolds?
As mentioned in Example 3.18, topological 3-manifolds admit smooth structures
which are unique up to diffeomorphism. Thus, much as in our Theorem E, the
Iannella–Weinstein complexity computation for 3-manifolds up to homeomorphism
readily implies a parallel one for smooth 3-manifolds up to diffeomorphism.
In
higher dimensions, however, smooth and topological structures no longer couple so
neatly; thus we also ask the following:
Question 2. For any n ≥4, what is the Borel complexity of the diffeomorphism
relation for connected smooth n-manifolds?
These give rise to further questions. Recall, for example, item (2) of the Hjorth–
Kechris theorem quoted in our introduction, whereby for any n ≥2 the biholomor-
phism relation on complex n-manifolds is turbulent and, consequently, not classi-
fiable by countable structures. Questions 1 and 2 are partly asking whether this
phenomenon manifests in the less rigid settings of topological or smooth manifolds.
As for complex manifolds, the first possibility of an affirmative answer is in 4 real
dimensions, by the results cited above.
We record the negative framing of the
question since it reads a little better:
30Recall from Example 3.18 our adherence to the convention that the absence of the phrase
“with boundary” connotes “without boundary”.

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
101
Question 3. Is the homeomorphism relation on connected topological n-manifolds
classifiable by countable structures for every n ≥1? Similarly for the diffeomor-
phism relation on connected smooth n-manifolds.
A close relative of these questions is that of whether complexity weakly increases
with dimension. Recall that it does not for complete finite-volume hyperbolic man-
ifolds, by Theorem 7.2 and Proposition 8.6, but that even for this class, complexity
varies weakly monotonically in dimensions n ≥2; thus, depending on how seriously
one takes the setting of one hyperbolic dimension,31 we arguably know at present
of no non-complexity-monotonic class of manifolds.
Question 4. Write ≤B for the Borel reducibility ordering of the class of analytic
equivalence relations on Polish spaces. Is the map sending n to:
• the homeomorphism relation on connected topological n-manifolds, or
• the diffeomorphism relation on connected smooth n-manifolds
a weakly increasing function from the positive integers to ≤B?
Naively, of course, one expects both maps to be, in part because there exist such
simple and uniform ways of converting lower-dimensional manifolds into higher-
dimensional ones.
The most obvious among them, however, do not in general
furnish reductions; see [McM62], for example, for an uncountable family of non-
homeomorphic contractible 3-manifolds whose products with R are each homeo-
morphic to R4 (cf. [Whi35, Gli60]). In contrast, by invariance of dimension, the
product with the closed interval [0, 1] does reduce the homeomorphism relation
for connected n-manifolds without boundary to the homeomorphism relation for
connected (n + 1)-manifolds with boundary, but the problem of relating the lat-
ter to that of connected (n + 1)-manifolds without boundary then remains. Again
the obvious ideas — namely, taking either interiors or doubles of manifolds with
boundaries — fail in general to furnish reductions of the suitable homeomorphism
relations; see [Gro71] for 3-dimensional examples of failures of the latter. What’s
lacking, in other words, are answers to the following:
Question 5. For any n ≥2, is the homeomorphism relation for connected topo-
logical n-manifolds with boundary Borel reducible to that for connected topological
n-manifolds without boundary? Similarly for smooth n-manifolds.
As noted in Example 3.18, the reverse reduction always holds, thus the question
is whether the homeomorphism or diffeomorphism relations on n-manifolds with
boundary and without boundary are Borel equivalent, or, conversely, whether any
additional complexity derives purely from manifolds’ boundaries.
Progress on these questions may well come in blocks. A positive answer to ei-
ther instance of Question 3, for example, would completely settle the corresponding
Question 1 or 2 as well as the corresponding instance of Question 4; the bearing
of Question 5 on Question 4 is similar. Above, we’ve given topological and smooth
manifolds primary and parallel attention simply because these are, classically, the
most prominent classes of manifolds; note moreover that in all complexity compu-
tations thus far, these two classes have behaved identically.
Further interest attaches to the 4-dimensional setting because it’s these classes’
first opportunity to behave differently; in certain restricted senses, in fact, we
31See §4 of the excellent [CFKP97] for an argument that it should be taken seriously.

102
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
know that they will. Here we’re referring, of course, to Milnor’s discovery of so-
called “exotic” smooth structures on topological manifolds M [Mil56]. As noted
in our proof of Corollary 3.26, there are, up to diffeomorphism, only countably
many such structures when M is compact (and in fact just finitely many if also
dim(M) ̸= 4, with exactly 28, for example, on Milnor’s initial setting of the 7-
dimensional sphere [KM63]). On the other hand, building on work of Donaldson
[Don83] and Freedman [Fre82], Taubes [Tau87] showed that R4 posses uncountably
many non-diffeomorphic smooth structures (see also [DMF92]). That the diffeo-
morphism relation on smooth manifolds homeomorphic to R4 is of strictly greater
Borel complexity than that of the homeomorphism relation on such manifolds fol-
lows trivially; the more interesting question, recorded already in [For18], is just
what the complexity of the former relation is.
Question 6. What is the Borel complexity of the diffeomorphism relation on
smooth structures on R4?
As noted in our introduction, Gompf and Panagiotopoulos have, at the time
of writing, shown the complexity to be at least E0; in addition, they’ve identified
a topological 4-manifold whose smooth structures, up to diffeomorphism, reduce
the Friedman—Stanley jump =+ of equality [GP25]. Question 6 is in some sense
a subquestion of Question 2; one might alternatively conjoin them in the ques-
tion Does the Borel complexity of the diffeomorphism relation on connected smooth
4-manifolds concentrate on the class of manifolds homeomorphic to R4? Note re-
latedly that our pseudogroup framework renders the first challenge in answering
Question 6 — namely, parametrizing smooth structures — particularly straightfor-
ward: if f is the embedding M(C∞, R4) →M(Top, R4) induced by the forgetful
pseudogroup embedding C∞→Top, then f−1([R4]∼
=Top) is the parameter space de-
sired. Observe moreover that the latter is naturally endowed with a standard Borel
structure if [R4]∼
=Top forms a Borel subset of M(Top, R4), an interesting question in
its own right which opens onto several more.
One might approach this last question, for example, via the homotopical or
(co)homological invariants of elements of M(Top, R4). We have seen in Corollary
3.25 that the computation of these invariants on the space K(Top, Rn) of all compact
topological n-manifolds is Borel, and it appears likely a consequence of Lemma
3.41 and Theorem 4.16 that this is also the case for the space M(Top, R2) of all
topological 2-manifolds (the same would hold for M(Top, R3), via the Iannella–
Weinstein work cited above). However, as mentioned in the discussion surrounding
Corollary 3.25 (particularly of [Bec92], see also [Kec95, 33.I]), their computation
for broader classes of topological spaces (even just compact subsets of R2) is not.
Add to this the existence of non-triangulable manifolds in every dimension greater
than 3 (as surveyed in [Man16a]), and the following question is a natural one.
Question 7. For which n and subspaces of M(Top, Rn) are the assignments of
the classical homotopical, homological, and cohomological invariants of algebraic
topology Borel measurable functions?
Returning to the discussion of M(C∞, R4) above, there we saw the two main ways
of altering a parameter space of manifolds; more precisely, associating to any Borel
pseudogroup G on a model space X are questions both of how (G, X)-equivalence
varies on varying subdomains of M(G, X), and as G is replaced by finer or coarser

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
103
pseudogroups H. The, loosely speaking, functorial way our parametrizations inter-
act with these sorts of variations (the way relaxing manifolds’ smooth structures to
topological ones manifests as an embedding M(C∞, R4) →M(Top, R4), for exam-
ple) is among our framework’s chief charms. Within it, these two axes of variation
entail two further families of questions.
A prospect associating to the first axis is a stratification of classes of (G, X)-
manifolds, for fixed G and X, according to the complexity they embody.
As
we’ve seen, for example, the homeomorphism relations for compact topological
2-manifolds and for topological 2-manifolds, respectively, are of minimal and maxi-
mal complexity among analytic equivalence relations which possess infinitely many
classes and are classifiable by countable structures. Which intermediate complex-
ity degrees are realized by naturally occurring intermediate classes of topological
2-manifolds? More precisely:
Question 8. For which analytic equivalence relations E and natural parameter
spaces X with K(Top, R2) ⊆X ⊆M(Top, R2) is the relation ∼=Top restricted to X
Borel equivalent to E?
Similarly, of course, for dimensions n ≥2. The question’s not solely of abstract
interest. In their study of the large-scale geometry of big mapping class groups, for
example, Mann and Rafi explicitly cite the complexity computations of [CG01] as
motivation for restricting attention to a class of surfaces they call tame [MR23, p.
2242]; a natural question is how much complexity they’re in the process screening
out.
Question 9. What is the Borel complexity of the homeomorphism relation for
surfaces which are tame in the sense of Mann–Rafi?
We’ve also seen that the complexity of the isometry relation for classes of hy-
perbolic n-manifolds ranges much as the homeomorphism relation for topological
2-manifolds does: by Corollary 6.7, Theorem 7.2, and Proposition 8.6, the range
of the former is from ∼=R to E∞and ∼=N to E∞, respectively, when n = 2 and
n > 2, with the minimum corresponding in all cases to the class of compact (or,
alternately, complete finite-volume) manifolds. One might then pose a version of
Question 8 for these classes as well, however, there is a sense in which we already
know an answer. A class of equivalence relations closely associated to those in-
duced by Polish group actions is that of the idealistic32 equivalence relations (see
[Kec92, KM16]); since this class is closed under classwise Borel isomorphism, it
follows from our Corollary 5.7 that the isometry relations on hyperbolic manifolds
all fall within this class. Since they are, moreover, Borel equivalence relations, it
follows from [KM16, Lem. 3.7] that for every n ≥2 and E ≤B E∞, there exists an
X ⊆C∗
c(Isom, Hn) such that the isometry relation on X is Borel equivalent to E.
Note, however, that this doesn’t entirely exhaust this line of inquiry: the question
of how this correspondence plays out, or more particularly of which of these X are
already objects of interest, remains a good one, and it’s in this direction that the
word natural in Question 8 is pointing above.
Against this background, we highlight a more definite question regarding classes
of hyperbolic manifolds of intermediate complexity. As shown in Corollary 8.25, for
32All orbit equivalence relations of Polish group actions are idealistic [Kec92]; it is an open
question whether the converse holds up to bireducibility, see e.g., [CM25].

104
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
a wide range of algebraically finite hyperbolic 3-manifolds the isometry relation has
Borel complexity at most E0; we ask whether other possible degrees are realized.
Here again we stress the word natural, as the discussion above shows that such
classes X exist abstractly.
Question 10. Is there a natural parameter space X ⊆C∗(Isom, H3) on which the
isometry relation ∼=Isom has Borel complexity strictly between E0 and E∞?
Zooming out, observe that all of the Borel complexity degrees of manifolds in
Theorems A–E and J–L of our introduction reduce to the orbit equivalence re-
lation of some Polish group action.
This leads to the rather broad question of
whether orbit equivalence relations represent a global upper bound for all classifi-
cation problems for manifolds based on, say, locally compact model spaces. Note
that there is a universal orbit equivalence relation, instantiated for example by the
homeomorphism relation on all compact metric spaces [Zie16].
Question 11. Does there exist a locally compact Polish space X and Borel pseu-
dogroup G on X such that the relation ∼=G of (G, X)-equivalence on M(G, X) is not
Borel reducible to an orbit equivalence relation of a Polish group action?
Returning to the discussion of axes of variation, the second and more obvious
one refers to the question of how complexity varies as we vary the pseudogroup G on
a fixed model space X. Here the questions are too numerous to list, and our caveat
above that others will be able to supply even better ones than we might particularly
applies.
Let us therefore merely draw attention to those (G, X)-structures that
might not leap so readily to mind. As in our Section 3.1, we direct readers to the
first sections of [Thu97, §3] for a sampling of such structures which is suggestive in
its range. Its Example 3.1.9, foliations, points even in the direction of structures
for which the model space X is not locally connected, namely the matchbox or
solenoidal manifolds of [AO95, Sul14]. To further shed the requirement that our
model space X be locally compact would jeapordize the underpinnings of our model
space machinery (see again Section 2.3), and this is one of the reasons we’ve focused
on finite-dimensional manifolds.
However, by a theorem of Henderson [Hen70],
every separable infinite-dimensional manifold is homeomorphic to an open subset
of the separable Hilbert space ℓ2. Thus the Effros–Borel structure on closed subsets
of ℓ2 induces a standard Borel structure on its open subsets, a natural candidate
parameter space for the separable infinite-dimensional manifolds on which we may
consider their complexity.
Question 12. What is the Borel complexity of the homeomorphism relation for
connected separable infinite-dimensional manifolds?
The next few questions concern the optimality of our results. For example, as
discussed in Section 6, the Andretta–Camerlo–Hjorth theorem [ACH01] tells us
that whenever a discrete group contains a nonabelian free subgroup, the conjugacy
relation on its space of subgroups is a universal countable Borel equivalence relation.
We then saw in Theorem 6.2 and its corollaries that this phenomenon also holds
for the conjugacy relation on discrete subgroups in a wide variety of Lie groups.
However, our arguments seem specific to “matrix-like” groups and the most far-
reaching generalization remains open:

MANIFOLD CLASSIFICATION FROM THE DESCRIPTIVE VIEWPOINT
105
Question 13. If G is a locally compact Polish group which contains a discrete non-
abelian free subgroup, is the conjugacy relation E(G, D(G)) on discrete subgroups
of G essentially countable universal?
In the more tractable case of a connected Lie group G, it fails to contain a discrete
nonabelian free subgroup if and only it is a compact extension of a solvable subgroup
[Pat88, Thm. 3.8].
Thus, we conjecture the following dichotomy for conjugacy
relations on the discrete subgroups of such a group G:
Conjecture. If G is a connected Lie group, then exactly one of the following holds:
1. E(G, D(G)) is essentially hyperfinite; or
2. E(G, D(G)) is essentially countable universal.
Implicit in this conjecture is that the conjugacy relation on discrete subgroups of
a compact-by-solvable Lie group is essentially hyperfinite. This is clearly related to
the solvable case of Weiss’s problem [Wei84] on whether discrete amenable groups
induce only hyperfinite equivalence relations. Recent progress [CJM+23] has re-
solved this problem for a large class of solvable discrete groups and therefore may
be helpful in addressing our conjecture above.
Returning to the question of optimality, recall that in Sections 7 and 8 we con-
fined our attention to torsion-free subgroups solely for the sake of simplicity. Thus
we may wonder:
Question 14. How does the removal of the qualifier torsion-free affect our Theo-
rems J and K?
Selberg’s lemma that, for example, every finitely generated Kleinian group is
virtually torsion-free [Sel60], suggests that it should not. But the lemma is too
essentially an existence result for its implications to be entirely obvious in our
contexts. As discussed in Section 8.4, we’ve also stopped short, in Theorem K, of
resolving the following.
Question 15. What is the Borel complexity of the isometry relation on alge-
braically finite hyperbolic 3-manifolds?
There are, as well, multiple natural questions about the generalizations of our
results from Sections 7 and 8 beyond 3 hyperbolic dimensions and the Lie groups
PSL(2, R) and PSL(2, C).
Question 16. Fix n ≥4. What is the Borel complexity of the isometry relation
on the space of geometrically finite hyperbolic n-manifolds?
What is the Borel
complexity of the isometry relation on the space of algebraically finite hyperbolic
n-manifolds? Equivalently, what is the Borel complexity of the conjugacy relations
on the spaces of geometrically finite and finitely generated discrete subgroups of
Isom+(Hn), respectively?
Recall that our answers to these questions in dimensions 2 and 3 drew on a rich
geometric literature; we lack answers in any higher dimension in part because there
there’s no comparable geometric theory to draw from. As in our introduction, we
note that answers to Question 16 may help us to understand both the reasons and
degrees to which this should or shouldn’t remain the case. Lastly, one may ask,
still more generally:

106
J. BERGFALK AND I. B. SMYTHE
Question 17. Does there exist a Lie (or even locally compact Polish) group G
for which the conjugacy relation on finitely generated discrete subgroups is not
essentially hyperfinite?
